Market Model and Trading Process In this section, we introduce the market model. We consider a state space { 1 , 2 , … , I } \{1,2,\ldots,I\} with I ≥ 2 I\geq 2 , comprising mutually exclusive and exhaustive outcomes. Each state i ∈ { 1 , 2 , … , I } i\in\{1,2,\ldots,I\} corresponds to an Arrow-Debreu security, which pays 1 1 dollar if state i i occurs. These securities are traded in a prediction market organized by a central market maker. A total of J &gt; 1 J&gt;1 traders engage in repeated transactions with the market maker at discrete time points t = 0 , 1 , 2 , … t=0,1,2,\ldots . We represent the market maker’s wealth at time t t as W t ∈ ℝ W_{t}\in\mathbb{R} and their outstanding securities position (i.e., the number of securities already sold) as 𝐐 t ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{Q}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} . The market maker’s initial values are W 0 &gt; 0 W_{0}&gt;0 and 𝐐 0 = 𝟎 \mathbf{Q}_{0}=\mathbf{0} . For each trader indexed by j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J , we use w j , t ∈ ℝ w_{j,t}\in\mathbb{R} and 𝐪 j , t ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{q}_{j,t}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} to denote their wealth and security positions at time t = 0 , 1 , 2 , … t=0,1,2,\ldots . The initial values are w j , 0 &gt; 0 w_{j,0}&gt;0 and 𝐪 j , 0 = 𝟎 \mathbf{q}_{j,0}=\mathbf{0} . At each time t t , one trader interacts with the market maker (we will specify the order of arrivals of the traders in later discussions) by submitting an order Δ ​ 𝐪 ∈ ℝ I \Delta\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} . For instance, in the context of a football match outcome, such an order might be expressed as Δ ​ 𝐪 = ( 2 , 1 , − 1 ) \Delta\mathbf{q}=(2,1,-1) , representing 2 2 shares for a buying order on “win”, 1 1 share for a buying order on “loss”, and 1 1 share for a selling order on “tie”. At any time t t , if the j j -th trader pays Δ ​ w \Delta w to the market marker (if Δ ​ w &lt; 0 \Delta w&lt;0 , then it means the trader is paid − Δ ​ w -\Delta w by the market maker) and buys Δ ​ 𝐪 \Delta\mathbf{q} share of security, then the market state is updated as follows: 3 3 3 In this work, most of the results are irrelevant to the condition of the nonnegative wealth restriction (bankruptcy restriction). However, we specify the difference when it is necessary. (1) Trader j : { w j , t + 1 = w j , t − Δ ​ w 𝐪 j , t + 1 = 𝐪 j , t + Δ ​ 𝐪 , Market Maker: { W t + 1 = W t + Δ ​ w 𝐐 t + 1 = 𝐐 t + Δ ​ 𝐪 . \displaystyle\textrm{Trader $j$:}\begin{cases}w_{j,t+1}=w_{j,t}-\Delta w\\ \mathbf{q}_{j,t+1}=\mathbf{q}_{j,t}+\Delta\mathbf{q}\end{cases},~~~~~\textrm{Market Maker:}\begin{cases}W_{t+1}=W_{t}+\Delta w\\ \mathbf{Q}_{t+1}=\mathbf{Q}_{t}+\Delta\mathbf{q}.\end{cases} The other traders’ positions keep unchanged, i.e., w k , t + 1 = w k , t w_{k,t+1}=w_{k,t} and 𝐪 k , t + 1 = 𝐪 k , t \mathbf{q}_{k,t+1}=\mathbf{q}_{k,t} , for k = 1 , … , J k=1,\ldots,J and k ≠ j k\not=j . To price an order Δ ​ 𝐪 ∈ ℝ I \Delta\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} , the market maker adopts a multivariate-utility (MU)-based mechanism described as follows. Suppose the market marker’ MU function is U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) : ℝ I → ℝ U(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} , which maps the net wealth position 𝐲 t ≜ W t ⋅ 𝐞 − 𝐐 t ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{y}_{t}\triangleq W_{t}\cdot\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{Q}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} to some real value. We call 𝐲 t \mathbf{y}_{t} the net wealth position because it represents all the possible wealth level if the final state is revealed at time t t . 4 4 4 For example, let 𝐲 t = ( y 1 , t , … , y I , t ) \mathbf{y}_{t}=\big(y_{1,t},\ldots,y_{I,t}\big) and Q i , t Q_{i,t} be the i i -th element of 𝐐 t \mathbf{Q}_{t} . Then, the i i -th element of 𝐲 \mathbf{y} is y i , t = W t − Q i , t y_{i,t}=W_{t}-Q_{i,t} which represents the wealth after paying the obligation if i i -th event occurs. Similar to the utility-based pricing mechanism in ( Chen and Pennock 2007 ) , the price of the order Δ ​ 𝐪 \Delta\mathbf{q} is determined by solving the following optimization problem: 5 5 5 Note that the domain of function U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) , denoted by dom ​ ( U ) \textbf{{dom}}(U) , may not necessarily be ℝ I \mathbb{R}^{I} . As a result, in problem ( 𝒫 cmin \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{cmin}} ), we explicitly add the constraint ( W t + Δ ​ w ) ⋅ 𝐞 − ( 𝐐 t + Δ ​ 𝐪 ) ∈ dom ​ ( U ) (W_{t}+\Delta w)\cdot\mathbf{e}-(\mathbf{Q}_{t}+\Delta\mathbf{q})\in\textbf{{dom}}(U) . This constraint can typically be expressed by some linear or nonlinear inequalities. 𝒫 cmin ​ ( Δ ​ 𝐪 ) : \displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{cmin}}(\Delta\mathbf{q}):~ min Δ ​ w ∈ ℝ ⁡ Δ ​ w \displaystyle~\min_{\Delta w\in\mathbb{R}}~~\Delta w (2) Subject to: U ⁡ ( ( W t + Δ ​ w ) ⋅ 𝐞 − ( 𝐐 t + Δ ​ 𝐪 ) ) ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) , \displaystyle~U\big((W_{t}+\Delta w)\cdot\mathbf{e}-(\mathbf{Q}_{t}+\Delta\mathbf{q})\big)\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}), ( W t + Δ ​ w ) ⋅ 𝐞 − ( 𝐐 t + Δ ​ 𝐪 ) ∈ dom ​ ( U ) . \displaystyle~(W_{t}+\Delta w)\cdot\mathbf{e}-(\mathbf{Q}_{t}+\Delta\mathbf{q})\in\textbf{{dom}}(U). The minimizer of problem 𝒫 cmin ​ ( Δ ​ 𝐪 ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{cmin}}(\Delta\mathbf{q}) is the price of the order Δ ​ 𝐪 \Delta\mathbf{q} . Constraint ( 2 ) can also be written as U ⁡ ( 𝐲 t + ( Δ ​ w ⋅ 𝐞 − Δ ​ 𝐪 ) ) ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) U(\mathbf{y}_{t}+(\Delta w\cdot\mathbf{e}-\Delta\mathbf{q}))\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}) , which means that the utility of the updated net wealth position is no less than that of the initial state. This is consistent with the utility-preserving property proposed in ( Chen and Pennock 2007 ) , i.e., the market maker tries to find the lowest possible charge for the order Δ ​ 𝐪 \Delta\mathbf{q} such that the post-trade utility level is no less than the initial level. To ensure the above pricing problem is well-posed, throughout this paper, we assume U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) satisfies the following assumptions. Assumption 1. U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) satisfies the following conditions: (i) it is continuous, concave and its domain dom ​ ( U ) \textbf{{dom}}(U) is convex with 𝐞 ∈ recc ​ ( dom ​ ( U ) ) \mathbf{e}\in\mathrm{recc}(\textbf{{dom}}(U)) ; (ii) it is monotonically increasing. 6 6 6 In this paper, we call a multivariate function F ⁡ ( ⋅ ) : ℝ I → ℝ F(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} monotonically increasing , if and only if for any 𝐲 , 𝐲 ^ ∈ dom ​ ( F ) \mathbf{y},\hat{\mathbf{y}}\in\textbf{{dom}}(F) , if 𝐲 ≥ 𝐲 ^ \mathbf{y}\geq\hat{\mathbf{y}} , then F ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) ≥ F ⁡ ( 𝐲 ^ ) F(\mathbf{y})\geq F(\hat{\mathbf{y}}) and inequality holds strictly when 𝐲 &gt; 𝐲 ^ \mathbf{y}&gt;\hat{\mathbf{y}} . Assumption 1 is mild and natural for the utility function except for the requirement 𝐞 ∈ recc ​ ( dom ​ ( U ) ) \mathbf{e}\in\textrm{recc}(\textbf{{dom}}(U)) which means that the utility is well-defined even if the wealth goes to infinity. Under Assumption 1 , it can be verified that the MU-based pricing rule satisfies the axioms proposed in ( Abernethy et al. 2013 ) . These axioms serve as general requirements for a reasonable pricing mechanism. Furthermore, the MU-based pricing rule possesses several other desirable properties. For instance, it demonstrates responsiveness to any finite order with a unique price, effectively eliminates arbitrage opportunities for surebets, and exhibits a finite worst-case loss (under mild conditions). Moreover, there is abundant freedom to construct MU functions (see examples in Appendix B.1 ). The following result establishes the relation between the MU-based pricing rule with the MSR and the cost function-based mechanism. Proposition 2.1. An MU-based mechanism with a utility function satisfying Assumption 1 is equivalent with a cost function-based mechanism with a corresponding cost function. Furthermore, if an MU-based mechanism has a bounded loss under worst-case scenario, then the underlying utility can induce a proper scoring rule. Conversely, any proper scoring rule can induce an MU-based mechanism that has bounded worst-case loss. The detailed proof of the above proposition is provided in Appendix B.2 . In this work, we adopt the MU-based pricing rule in our analysis mainly due to the following reasons. First, as shown in Proposition 2.1 , the MU-based pricing rule is general enough to cover existing classical pricing rules. More importantly, the MU-based mechanism provides a tractable way to study the convergence property of prices generated by a dynamic trading process. Once we have these convergence results, they can be directly applied to other pricing rules. Second, compared with the cost function-based pricing rule ( Chen and Pennock 2007 ; Abernethy et al. 2013 ) and the LMSR ( Hanson 2003 ) , the MU-based mechanism could explicitly incorporate the risk preferences and the heterogeneous beliefs of all participants in a unified framework, which enables us to explore how these heterogeneous factors affect the final prices of these securities. Third, it is more convenient to construct the MU function in practice compared with the other mechanisms. For example, using risk-measure to define the cost function needs it to be convex and translation invariant , which means that the cost function should not be strictly convex . Indeed, it is usually not an easy task to customize a valid cost function in an analytical form. 7 7 7 Although ( Föllmer and Schied 2011 ) show that any convex risk measure (cost function) can be defined numerically via an acceptance set or by the dual representation, it requires solving a constrained optimization problem whenever a new order is submitted. In contrast, the MU-based utility only requires the function to be concave and monotone , and it can be constructed in various ways, including the classic expected utility theory (EUT) ( Chen and Pennock 2007 ) and convex risk measures (e.g., ( Chen and Pennock 2007 ; Föllmer and Schied 2011 ; Hu and Storkey 2014 ; Frongillo et al. 2015 ) ). Moreover, the price of an order can be computed by a simple line search. We provide additional properties of the MU function and some examples in Appendix B . On the trader’s side, we assume that each trader j ∈ { 1 , … , J } j\in\{1,\ldots,J\} also adopts an MU function, V j ​ ( ⋅ ) : ℝ I → ℝ V_{j}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} , to evaluate his/her net wealth position. 8 8 8 The choice of trader’s MU function V j ​ ( ⋅ ) V_{j}(\cdot) is similar to that of the market maker’s MU function U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) . It can be based on conventional expected utility or constructed using a convex risk measure. Typically, in these formulas, the trader’s subjective belief is explicitly specified. Following the spirit of ( Sethi and Vaughan 2016 ) , the j j -th trader solves the following problem to decide the amount of securities to trade, 𝒫 ¯ j ​ ( w j , t , 𝐪 j , t ) : \displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(w_{j,t},\mathbf{q}_{j,t}):~ max Δ ​ 𝐪 ∈ ℝ I , Δ ​ w ∈ ℝ ⁡ V j ​ ( ( w j , t − Δ ​ w ) ⋅ 𝐞 + ( 𝐪 j , t + Δ ​ 𝐪 ) ) \displaystyle~\max_{\Delta\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{I},~\Delta w\in\mathbb{R}}~~V_{j}\big((w_{j,t}-\Delta w)\cdot\mathbf{e}+(\mathbf{q}_{j,t}+\Delta\mathbf{q})\big) subject to Δ w = arg min w ∈ ℝ { w | U ( ( W t + w ) ⋅ 𝐞 − ( 𝐐 t + Δ 𝐪 ) ) ≥ U ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) , \displaystyle~\Delta w=\arg\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}}~\big\{w~|~U\big((W_{t}+w)\cdot\mathbf{e}-(\mathbf{Q}_{t}+\Delta\mathbf{q})\big)\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}), ( W t + w ) ⋅ 𝐞 − ( 𝐐 t + Δ ​ 𝐪 ) ∈ dom ​ ( U ) , \displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~(W_{t}+w)\cdot\mathbf{e}-(\mathbf{Q}_{t}+\Delta\mathbf{q})\in\textbf{{dom}}(U), (3) ( w j , t − w ) ⋅ 𝐞 + ( 𝐪 j , t + Δ 𝐪 ) ∈ dom ( V j ) } . \displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~(w_{j,t}-w)\cdot\mathbf{e}+(\mathbf{q}_{j,t}+\Delta\mathbf{q})\in\textbf{{dom}}(V_{j})\big\}. In this problem, the term ( w j , t − Δ ​ w ) ⋅ 𝐞 + ( 𝐪 j , t + Δ ​ 𝐪 ) (w_{j,t}-\Delta w)\cdot\mathbf{e}+(\mathbf{q}_{j,t}+\Delta\mathbf{q}) in the objective function represents the j j -th trader’s net wealth position after the current trading. Problem 𝒫 ¯ j ​ ( w j , t , 𝐪 j , t ) \bar{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(w_{j,t},\mathbf{q}_{j,t}) can be viewed as a bi-level optimization problem. It fully characterizes all the information in one round of trading, i.e., the size of the order Δ ​ 𝐪 \Delta\mathbf{q} is determined by the upper-level problem, and the payment of the order Δ ​ 𝐪 \Delta\mathbf{q} , is determined by the subproblem in constraint ( 3 ). The lower level subproblem in constraint ( 3 ) is nothing but the problem 𝒫 cmin ​ ( Δ ​ 𝐪 ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{cmin}}(\Delta\mathbf{q}) . To guarantee the whole trading process is well defined, we impose the following conditions for V j ​ ( ⋅ ) V_{j}(\cdot) , j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J . Assumption 2. Each V j ​ ( ⋅ ) V_{j}(\cdot) satisfies the following conditions: (i) it is continuous and strictly concave for non-equivalent wealth vectors 9 9 9 We call 𝐲 ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} and 𝐱 ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} are equivalent wealth, if and only if 𝐲 = 𝐱 + t ⋅ 𝐞 \mathbf{y}=\mathbf{x}+t\cdot\mathbf{e} for some t ∈ ℝ t\in\mathbb{R} . Strictly concavity of non-equivalent wealth means that, for any 𝐱 , 𝐲 ∈ dom ​ ( V j ) \mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\textbf{{dom}}(V_{j}) who are not equivalent wealth vectors, it has V j ​ ( α ​ 𝐱 + ( 1 − α ) ​ 𝐲 ) &gt; α ​ V j ​ ( 𝐱 ) + ( 1 − α ) ​ V j ​ ( 𝐲 ) V_{j}(\alpha\mathbf{x}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{y})&gt;\alpha V_{j}(\mathbf{x})+(1-\alpha)V_{j}(\mathbf{y}) for any α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in(0,1) . ; (ii) dom ​ ( V j ) \textbf{{dom}}(V_{j}) is convex with recc ⁡ ( V j ) ⊆ ℝ + I \mathrm{recc}(V_{j})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{I}_{+} and 𝐞 ∈ recc ​ ( dom ​ ( V j ) ) \mathbf{e}\in\mathrm{recc}(\textbf{{dom}}(V_{j})) ; (iii) it is monotonically increasing. Assumption 2 is similar to Assumption 1 except that it requires V j ​ ( ⋅ ) V_{j}(\cdot) to be strictly concave except along the direction 𝐞 \mathbf{e} . This condition is necessary to establish trading process convergence in the later part of this paper. Note that such an assumption is weaker than global strict concavity, and it can also accommodate risk measure-based MU function, which is linear along direction 𝐞 \mathbf{e} . Although the state { w j , t , 𝐪 j , t } \{w_{j,t},\mathbf{q}_{j,t}\} fully represents the trading process, it is complicated to study the convergence of the trading process by solving problem 𝒫 ¯ j ​ ( w j , t , 𝐪 j , t ) \bar{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(w_{j,t},\mathbf{q}_{j,t}) directly. It is more convenient to use the net wealth position to characterize the trading process. Specifically, at time t t , let 𝐱 j , t ≜ w j , t ⋅ 𝐞 + 𝐪 j , t \mathbf{x}_{j,t}\triangleq w_{j,t}\cdot\mathbf{e}+\mathbf{q}_{j,t} and 𝐲 t ≜ W t ⋅ 𝐞 − 𝐐 t \mathbf{y}_{t}\triangleq W_{t}\cdot\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{Q}_{t} be the j j -th trader’s and the market maker’s net wealth position, respectively. At time t = 0 t=0 , it has 𝐱 j , 0 = w j , 0 ⋅ 𝐞 \mathbf{x}_{j,0}=w_{j,0}\cdot\mathbf{e} and 𝐲 0 = W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 \mathbf{y}_{0}=W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e} . In each round of trading, when an order Δ ​ 𝐪 \Delta\mathbf{q} is placed at price Δ ​ w \Delta w , the variation of market maker’s wealth is denoted as 𝐳 ≜ ( z 1 , z 2 , … , z I ) = − Δ w ⋅ 𝐞 + Δ 𝐪 \mathbf{z}\triangleq\big(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{I}\big)=-\Delta w\cdot\mathbf{e}+\Delta\mathbf{q} . Then, the dynamics ( 1 ) transform into 𝐱 j , t + 1 = 𝐱 j , t + 𝐳 \mathbf{x}_{j,t+1}=\mathbf{x}_{j,t}+\mathbf{z} if the j j -th trader interacts with the market at time t t , and 𝐱 k , t + 1 = 𝐱 k , t \mathbf{x}_{k,t+1}=\mathbf{x}_{k,t} for k ≠ j k\not=j , along with the market maker’s wealth update, 𝐲 t + 1 = 𝐲 t − 𝐳 \mathbf{y}_{t+1}=\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{z} . It is worth noting that the market total wealth adheres to the following condition: (4) 𝐲 t + ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j , t = ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j , 0 + 𝐲 0 = w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 , \displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{t}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j,t}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j,0}+\mathbf{y}_{0}={w}^{\textrm{all}}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}, for all t = 0 , 1 , … t=0,1,\ldots , where w 0 all ≜ ∑ j = 1 J w j , 0 + W 0 {w}^{\textrm{all}}_{0}\triangleq\sum_{j=1}^{J}w_{j,0}+W_{0} is the total wealth of all participants in the market. Eq. ( 4 ) suggests that the total net wealth position in the market maintains at a constant level throughout the trading process. Using the wealth vectors 𝐱 j , t \mathbf{x}_{j,t} and 𝐲 t \mathbf{y}_{t} , the utility preserving property ( 2 ) can be expressed as U ⁡ ( 𝐲 t − 𝐳 ) ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) U\big(\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{z})\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}) . This formulation motivates us to consider an equivalent formulation of the j j -th trader’s decision problem: 𝒫 j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) : max 𝐳 ∈ ℝ I \displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}):~~\max_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{I}}~ V j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t + 1 ) = V j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t + 𝐳 ) \displaystyle~V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t+1})=V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}+\mathbf{z}) (5) Subject to: U ⁡ ( 𝐲 t − 𝐳 ) ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) , \displaystyle~U(\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{z})\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}), 𝐲 t − 𝐳 ∈ dom ​ ( U ) , 𝐱 j , t + 𝐳 ∈ dom ​ ( V j ) , \displaystyle~\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{z}\in\textbf{{dom}}(U),~\mathbf{x}_{j,t}+\mathbf{z}\in\textbf{{dom}}(V_{j}), for given 𝐱 j , t \mathbf{x}_{j,t} and 𝐲 j , t \mathbf{y}_{j,t} satisfying ( 4 ). The following result establishes equivalence between problems 𝒫 j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) \mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}) and 𝒫 ¯ j ​ ( w j , t , 𝐪 j , t ) \bar{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(w_{j,t},\mathbf{q}_{j,t}) in the sense of the optimal solutions. Proposition 2.2. Under Assumptions 1 and 2 , problem 𝒫 j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) \mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}) admits a unique solution 𝐳 ∗ \mathbf{z}^{*} . Furthermore, any feasible solution { Δ ​ w ∗ , Δ ​ 𝐪 ∗ } \{\Delta w^{*},\Delta\mathbf{q}^{*}\} of problem 𝒫 ¯ j ​ ( w j , t , 𝐪 j , t ) \bar{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(w_{j,t},\mathbf{q}_{j,t}) satisfying Δ ​ 𝐪 ∗ − Δ ​ w ∗ ⋅ 𝐞 = 𝐳 ∗ \Delta\mathbf{q}^{*}-\Delta w^{*}\cdot\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{z}^{*} is an optimal solution of 𝒫 ¯ j ​ ( w j , t , 𝐪 j , t ) \bar{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(w_{j,t},\mathbf{q}_{j,t}) . The above result suggests that 𝒫 j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) \mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}) captures all the essence of market evolution encoded in the original problem 𝒫 ¯ j ​ ( w j , t , 𝐪 j , t ) \bar{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(w_{j,t},\mathbf{q}_{j,t}) . It greatly simplifies the original market process characterized by w j , t w_{j,t} , 𝐪 j , t \mathbf{q}_{j,t} , W t W_{t} and 𝐐 t \mathbf{Q}_{t} with the pricing problem 𝒫 ¯ j ​ ( w j , t , 𝐪 j , t ) \bar{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(w_{j,t},\mathbf{q}_{j,t}) . Therefore, in the consequent analysis, we focus on the formulation 𝒫 j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) \mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}) . Remark 1 (No-bankruptcy Restriction). In practical applications, traders often operate under the no-bankruptcy restriction, which means that the constraint 𝐱 j , t ≥ 0 \mathbf{x}_{j,t}\geq 0 holds for all t t and j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J . When the problem 𝒫 j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) \mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}) includes such constraints, we denote the corresponding problem as 𝒫 j + ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) \mathcal{P}_{j}^{+}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}) . To facilitate analysis, we make an additional assumption. 10 10 10 Assumption 3 is not necessary to define the MU-based pricing mechanism and not necessary for general convergence result. Imposing these assumptions helps simplify the analysis in dynamic trading process. Assumption 3. U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) and V j ​ ( ⋅ ) V_{j}(\cdot) , j = 1 , … , J ~j=1,\ldots,J are continuously differentiable functions. Before we analyze the convergence property, we discuss how to compute the instantaneous prices of the securities under the MU-based mechanism. The instantaneous prices of the securities can be viewed as an integrated forecast of the corresponding event (see, e.g., Wolfers and Zitzewitz 2004 ). Let 𝐲 = ( y 1 , y 2 , … , y I ) \mathbf{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{I}) be the market maker’s net wealth posisition. The instantaneous price of each security is defined as the marginal cost per share for purchasing an infinitesimal quantity of this security. The instantaneous price of the i i -th security, i ∈ { 1 , … , I } i\in\{1,\ldots,I\} , can be computed by pricing the order Δ ​ 𝐪 = ϵ ⋅ 𝐞 i \Delta\mathbf{q}=\epsilon\cdot\mathbf{e}_{i} where ϵ &gt; 0 \epsilon&gt;0 is a small number and 𝐞 i \mathbf{e}_{i} is the i i -th unit vector. If Δ ​ w ​ ( ϵ ) \Delta w(\epsilon) is the solution of problem 𝒫 cmin ​ ( ϵ ⋅ 𝐞 i ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{cmin}}(\epsilon\cdot\mathbf{e}_{i}) , then the instantaneous price of the i i -th security is p i = lim ϵ → 0 Δ ​ w ​ ( ϵ ) / ϵ p_{i}=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\Delta w(\epsilon)/\epsilon . Under some assumptions of U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) , the price p i p_{i} can be explicitly computed. Proposition 2.3. Under Assumptions 1 and 3 , given market state 𝐲 \mathbf{y} , if one of the following conditions holds: (i) the state variable 𝐲 \mathbf{y} is an interior point of dom ​ ( U ) \textbf{{dom}}(U) or (ii) U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) U(\mathbf{y}) approaches − ∞ -\infty whenever 𝐲 \mathbf{y} approaches the boundary of dom ​ ( U ) \textbf{{dom}}(U) , then the instantaneous price of the i i -th security is (6) p i = ∂ U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) / ∂ y i ∑ k = 1 I ∂ U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) / ∂ y k , i = 1 , … , I . \displaystyle p_{i}=\frac{\partial U(\mathbf{y})/\partial y_{i}}{\sum_{k=1}^{I}\partial U(\mathbf{y})/\partial y_{k}},~~i=1,\ldots,I. If we aggregate all p i p_{i} in a vector 𝐩 = ( p 1 , … , p I ) \mathbf{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{I}) , then the price vector is exactly the normalized gradient of U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) U(\mathbf{y}) , i.e., 𝐩 = ∇ U ( 𝐲 ) / ( 𝐞 ⊤ ∇ U ( 𝐲 ) ) \mathbf{p}=\nabla U(\mathbf{y})\big/(\mathbf{e}^{\top}\nabla U(\mathbf{y})) . Note that, when the assumptions in Proposition 2.3 fail to hold, the domain boundary of U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) will affect the solution of problem 𝒫 cmin ​ ( ϵ ⋅ 𝐞 i ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{cmin}}(\epsilon\cdot\mathbf{e}_{i}) . In those cases, one can still compute the price by using the envelope theorem. However, it will be much more complicated as it will involve the form of the boundary of U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) . We omit the detailed discussions for those cases. 3. Convergence of Market States in General Setting In this section, we study the convergence properties of the market states (wealth and prices) generated by the sequential interactions between the traders and the market maker. We define the trading sequence as an infinite sequence of traders indexed by time, denoted by 𝒮 = { j 1 , j 2 , … , j t , … } \mathcal{S}=\{j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{t},\ldots\} with j t ∈ { 1 , … , J } j_{t}\in\{1,\ldots,J\} . We say a sequence 𝒮 \mathcal{S} satisfies the infinite participation or simply the IP property, if for any j ∈ { 1 , … , J } j\in\{1,\ldots,J\} , the set { t | j t = j , j t ∈ 𝒮 } \{t|j_{t}=j,j_{t}\in\mathcal{S}\} has infinite elements. We denote all trading sequences satisfying the IP property by Φ \Phi . In other words, Φ \Phi contains all trading sequences such that each trader interacts with the seller for an infinite number of times. 11 11 11 Note that the IP property is a weaker condition than the assumption made in ( Sethi and Vaughan 2016 ) . In Assumption 1 of ( Sethi and Vaughan 2016 ) , it is assumed that there exists a constant m m such that each trader must trade once during any period of length m m . Obviously, this implies the IP property but not the reverse. Therefore, our assumption allows a more flexible trading pattern than that in ( Sethi and Vaughan 2016 ) . Given a sequence 𝒮 \mathcal{S} , we can define the trading process as follows (which is referred to as Trading Process 1 ): Initialization: set 𝐲 0 = W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 \mathbf{y}_{0}=W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e} , 𝐱 j , 0 = w j , 0 ​ 𝐞 \mathbf{x}_{j,0}=w_{j,0}\mathbf{e} , ∀ j = 1 , … , J \forall j=1,\ldots,J ; 𝒮 = { j 1 , j 2 , … , j t , … } \mathcal{S}=\{j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{t},\ldots\} is given; Set t = 1 t=1 . while True do Select the j j -th trader at time t t as j = j t j=j_{t} ; Solve 𝐳 \mathbf{z} from problem 𝒫 j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) \mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}) (or problem 𝒫 j + ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) \mathcal{P}_{j}^{+}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}) if there is no bankruptcy constraint); Update: for t = t + 1 t=t+1 𝐲 t + 1 = 𝐲 t − 𝐳 , 𝐱 j , t + 1 = 𝐱 j , t + 𝐳 . \mathbf{y}_{t+1}=\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{z},~~\mathbf{x}_{j,t+1}=\mathbf{x}_{j,t}+\mathbf{z}. end while Trading Process 1 Trading Process In Trading Process 1 , we use 𝐩 t = ( p 1 , t , … , p I , t ) \mathbf{p}_{t}=(p_{1,t},\ldots,p_{I,t}) , t = 1 , 2 , … t=1,2,\ldots , to denote the instantaneous price of the securities at time t t . Given 𝐲 t \mathbf{y}_{t} , 𝐩 t \mathbf{p}_{t} can be computed by ( 6 ). As t → ∞ t\rightarrow\infty , if 𝐱 j , t \mathbf{x}_{j,t} and p i , t p_{i,t} converge to some limits 𝐱 j ∗ \mathbf{x}_{j}^{*} and p i ∗ p_{i}^{*} , respectively, then we call 𝐱 j ∗ \mathbf{x}_{j}^{*} and p i ∗ p_{i}^{*} the limiting wealth allocation for trader j j and the limiting price of the i i -th security. Some important questions arise naturally: Does the wealth allocation 𝐱 j , t \mathbf{x}_{j,t} generated from the Trading Process 1 converge to some limiting allocation? If so, what property does the limiting wealth allocation have? Similarly, does the price generated from the Trading Process 1 converge and what property does the limiting price have? To answer these questions, we first define the feasible set of all possible market states generated by the Trading Process 1 as follows, 𝒜 = \displaystyle\mathcal{A}= { ( 𝐱 1 , … , 𝐱 J ) | 𝐲 + ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j = w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 , U ( 𝐲 ) ≥ U ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) , \displaystyle\Big\{\big(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}\big)~\Big|~\mathbf{y}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j}={w}_{0}^{\textrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{e},~~U(\mathbf{y})\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}), (7) V j ( 𝐱 j ) ≥ V j ( w j , 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) , ( 𝐱 j ≥ 𝟎 , if bankruptcy is prohibitted ) j = 1 , … , J } . \displaystyle~~~~~~V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j})\geq V_{j}(w_{j,0}\cdot\mathbf{e}),~(\mathbf{x}_{j}\geq\mathbf{0},\textrm{if bankruptcy is prohibitted})~j=1,\ldots,J~\Big\}. It is not hard to prove that the feasible set 𝒜 \mathcal{A} is a compact set. We then introduce the definition of Pareto optimality of the wealth allocation with respect to the MU function V j ​ ( ⋅ ) V_{j}(\cdot) for j ∈ { 1 , 2 , … , J } j\in\{1,2,\ldots,J\} . Definition 3.1. A wealth allocation ( 𝐱 1 ∗ , … , 𝐱 J ∗ ) (\mathbf{x}_{1}^{*},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}^{*}) is called a Pareto optimal allocation if the following two conditions hold: (i) U ⁡ ( w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 − ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j ∗ ) ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) U({w}_{0}^{\textrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{e}-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{*})\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}) ; (ii) there does not exist any ( 𝐱 1 , … , 𝐱 J ) (\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}) such that U ⁡ ( w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 − ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j ) ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) U({w}_{0}^{\textrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{e}-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j})\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}) and V j ​ ( 𝐱 j ) ≥ V j ​ ( 𝐱 j ∗ ) V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j})\geq V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j}^{*}) for all j ∈ { 1 , … , J } j\in\{1,\ldots,J\} with strict inequality holds for at least one j † ∈ { 1 , … , J } j^{\dagger}\in\{1,\ldots,J\} . The Pareto optimal allocation means that, for each trader, it is not possible to increase his/her own utility without decreasing the utility of any other traders. All the Pareto optimal wealth allocations form the Pareto efficient set . Note that, the concavity of V j ​ ( ⋅ ) V_{j}(\cdot) guarantees that any Pareto efficient wealth allocation can be achieved by some weighting parameters 𝝂 = ( ν 1 , … , ν J ) ∈ ℝ + J ∖ { 𝟎 } \boldsymbol{\nu}=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})\in\mathbb{R}^{J}_{+}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\} (see, e.g., Mas-Colell et al. 1995 ) by solving the following optimization problem: 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ) : \displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}):~ max ⁡ ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 1 , … , 𝐱 J ⁡ ν j ​ V j ​ ( 𝐱 j ) \displaystyle~\max_{\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}}~~\sum_{j=1}^{J}\nu_{j}V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j}) Subject to : \displaystyle\textrm{Subject to}:~ 𝐲 + ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j = w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 , \displaystyle~\mathbf{y}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j}={w}_{0}^{\textrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{e}, (8) U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) , \displaystyle~U(\mathbf{y})\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}), 𝐱 j ≥ 𝟎 , j = 1 , … , J ( if bankruptcy is prohibitted ) . \displaystyle~\mathbf{x}_{j}\geq\mathbf{0},\quad j=1,\ldots,J~~(\textrm{if bankruptcy is prohibitted}). We then present our main result which states that the Trading Process 1 converges to some Pareto optimal allocation. Theorem 3.2. Under Assumptions 1 , 2 and 3 , the wealth allocation 𝐱 j , t \mathbf{x}_{j,t} and 𝐲 t \mathbf{y}_{t} generated from Trading Process 1 for any given trading sequence 𝒮 ∈ Φ \mathcal{S}\in\Phi converge to some limit 𝐱 j ∗ \mathbf{x}^{*}_{j} and 𝐲 ∗ \mathbf{y}^{*} , i.e., lim t → ∞ 𝐱 j , t = 𝐱 j ∗ \lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{x}_{j,t}={\mathbf{x}}^{*}_{j} for all j = 1 , … , J j=1,\dots,J and lim t → ∞ 𝐲 t = 𝐲 ∗ \lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{y}_{t}={\mathbf{y}}^{*} , if either one of the following conditions holds: (i) dom ​ ( U ) = ℝ I \textbf{{dom}}(U)=\mathbb{R}^{I} ; or (ii) dom ​ ( U ) \textbf{{dom}}(U) can be expressed as dom ​ ( U ) = { 𝐲 | 𝐲 ≥ 𝐋 } \textbf{{dom}}(U)=\{\mathbf{y}~|~\mathbf{y}\geq\mathbf{L}\} with 𝐋 &lt; W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 \mathbf{L}&lt;W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e} and satisfies lim y i → L i ​ ∂ U ∂ y i = + ∞ \underset{y_{i}\to L_{i}}{\lim}\frac{\partial U}{\partial y_{i}}=+\infty and ∂ U ∂ y i \frac{\partial U}{\partial y_{i}} is finite if 𝐲 i ≠ L i \mathbf{y}_{i}\neq L_{i} . Furthermore, the limiting allocation ( 𝐱 1 ∗ , … , 𝐱 J ∗ ) \big(\mathbf{x}^{*}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{*}_{J}\big) must be a Pareto optimal allocation. In the above result, two alternative conditions (condition (i) and (ii)) are imposed to ensure that the limit of wealth allocation is an interior point in the domain of the utility function. Condition (i) is easy to understand, while condition (ii) is a generalization of the Inada condition , which is commonly used in conventional utility optimization. Theorem 3.2 states that, as the Trading Process 1 goes on, eventually, no trader would like to modify their wealth position anymore, i.e., the wealth process ( 𝐱 1 , t , … , 𝐱 J , t ) (\mathbf{x}_{1,t},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J,t}) converges to some limiting wealth allocation. Moreover, the limiting wealth allocation ( 𝐱 1 ∗ , … , 𝐱 J ∗ ) (\mathbf{x}_{1}^{*},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}^{*}) must locate on the Pareto optimal set defined in Definition 3.1 . The Pareto optimality of the limiting allocation formally confirms the theoretical foundations of prediction markets: a prediction market can indeed incentivize people to participate, and the limiting price is a “good price” in the sense that it is supported by a wealth allocation where the well-being (measured by utility level) of any trader cannot be improved anymore without decreasing some other traders’ utility. Remark 2. We emphasize that the requirement 𝒮 ∈ Φ \mathcal{S}\in\Phi is necessary for achieving overall Pareto optimality. The trading process will still converge if one or more traders cease trading from time t &gt; 0 t&gt;0 and the remaining traders will still reach their own Pareto optimality. However, if a trader only interacts with the market maker a finite number of times, then he/she may still be willing to trade after his/her last transaction, but is deprived of that opportunity. Consequently, this trader’s final wealth along with other traders’ wealth may not be located in the Pareto optimal set. A direct result of Theorem 3.2 is that the price of the security also converges to some limiting price. Such a limiting price can be computed as follows. Corollary 3.3. When the assumptions in Theorem 3.2 are met and the trading process converges, the limiting price is given by, (9) 𝐩 ∗ = ∇ U ​ ( 𝐲 ∗ ) ∇ U ( 𝐲 ∗ ) ⊤ 𝐞 , \displaystyle\mathbf{p}^{*}=\frac{\nabla U(\mathbf{y}^{*})}{\nabla U(\mathbf{y}^{*})^{\top}\mathbf{e}}, where 𝐲 ∗ \mathbf{y}^{*} is the market maker’s the limiting wealth allocation. The price formula ( 9 ) is a natural generalization of the one derived for the scalar-valued utility function in ( Chen and Pennock 2007 ) . In the upcoming sections, we will study how the market forms prices by aggregating traders’ beliefs. To represent these beliefs, we use 𝜽 = ( θ 1 , … , θ I ) ∈ Θ I \boldsymbol{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{I})\in\Theta_{I} and 𝝅 j = ( π 1 , j , … , π I , j ) ∈ Θ I \boldsymbol{\pi}_{j}=(\pi_{1,j},\ldots,\pi_{I,j})\in\Theta_{I} to denote the market maker’s and the j j -th trader’s subjective beliefs (probabilities) on the outcomes, respectively. Unless otherwise stated, we assume that these beliefs remain constant throughout the trading process. We will use 𝐩 t = ( p 1 , t , p 2 , t , … , p I , t ) \mathbf{p}_{t}=\big(p_{1,t},p_{2,t},\ldots,p_{I,t}\big) to denote the price vector generated by Trading Process 1 , where t = 0 , 1 , 2 ​ … t=0,1,2\ldots . 4. Exponential Utility and Risk Measure-Based Market The previous section demonstrated market states converging to Pareto optimal allocations but provided limited insight into how the market forms equilibrium prices. In this section, we explore two special cases: exponential utility-based and risk measure-based MU functions. We will derive the price convergence results for these market models. 4.1. Exponential Utility Based Market We first consider the exponential utility functions -based market. The exponential utility has a constant risk aversion coefficient, which leads to trading decisions independent of the trader’s wealth level ( Makarov and Schornick 2010 ) . This feature eliminates the wealth effect in the trading process and allows us to clearly illustrate the impacts of traders’ beliefs and risk preferences upon the limiting prices. It is important to note that adopting the exponential utility to price the security is equivalent to using the LMSR pricing rule, which is a widely adopted mechanism (see Appendix B.2 or ( Chen and Pennock 2007 ) ). Specifically, given market state vectors 𝐲 = ( y 1 , … , y I ) \mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{I}) and 𝐱 j = ( x 1 , j , … , x I , j ) \mathbf{x}_{j}=(x_{1,j},\ldots,x_{I,j}) for j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J , the market maker’s MU function and the j j -th trader’s utility function are (10) U ( 𝐲 ) ≜ ∑ i = 1 I θ i 1 − e − β ⋅ y i β and V j ( 𝐱 j ) ≜ ∑ i = 1 I π i , j 1 − e − α j ⋅ x i , j α j , j = 1 , … , J \displaystyle U(\mathbf{y})\triangleq\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}\frac{1-e^{-\beta\cdot y_{i}}}{\beta}~~~\textrm{and}~~~V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j})\triangleq\sum_{i=1}^{I}\pi_{i,j}\frac{1-e^{-\alpha_{j}\cdot x_{i,j}}}{\alpha_{j}},~~j=1,\ldots,J respectively, where β &gt; 0 \beta&gt;0 and α j &gt; 0 \alpha_{j}&gt;0 are the risk aversion coefficients for the market maker and the j j -th trader, respectively. Since the exponential utility-based MU function satisfies the conditions outlined in Theorem 3.2 , both wealth allocation and instantaneous prices converge to their respective limiting values. The following results characterize the evolution of market prices generated by Trading Process 1 . Proposition 4.1. In a market equipped with the exponential utility functions defined in ( 10 ), given the market state { 𝐱 j , t , 𝐲 t } \{\mathbf{x}_{j,t},\mathbf{y}_{t}\} with the price 𝐩 t \mathbf{p}_{t} at time t t , it has the following results: (a) If the j j -th trader trades with the market maker, then the optimal trading amount is 𝐳 ∗ = ( z 1 ∗ , … , z I ∗ ) \mathbf{z}^{*}=(z^{*}_{1},\ldots,z^{*}_{I}) with (11) z i ∗ = 1 β ln ( ( π ^ i , j , t / p i , t ) β α j + β ∑ k = 1 I p k , t ​ ( π ^ k , j , t / p k , t ) β α j + β ) , i = 1 , … , I , \displaystyle z_{i}^{*}=\frac{1}{\beta}\ln\left(\frac{\big(\hat{\pi}_{i,j,t}\big/p_{i,t}\big)^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha_{j}+\beta}}}{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}p_{k,t}\left(\hat{\pi}_{k,j,t}\big/p_{k,t}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha_{j}+\beta}}}\right),~~i=1,\ldots,I, where π ^ i , j , t ≜ π i , j ⋅ e − α j ​ x i , j , t / ( ∑ k = 1 I π k , j ⋅ e − α j ​ x k , j , t ) \hat{\pi}_{i,j,t}\triangleq\pi_{i,j}\cdot e^{-\alpha_{j}x_{i,j,t}}\big/(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}\pi_{k,j}\cdot e^{-\alpha_{j}x_{k,j,t}}) . (b) The price is updated to p i , t + 1 = ( p i , t ) α j α j + β ⋅ ( π ^ i , j , t ) β α j + β ∑ k = 1 I ( p k , t ) α j α j + β ⋅ ( π ^ k , j , t ) β α j + β , i = 1 , … , I . \displaystyle p_{i,t+1}=\frac{(p_{i,t})^{\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\alpha_{j}+\beta}}\cdot(\hat{\pi}_{i,j,t})^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha_{j}+\beta}}}{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}(p_{k,t})^{\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\alpha_{j}+\beta}}\cdot(\hat{\pi}_{k,j,t})^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha_{j}+\beta}}},~~i=1,\ldots,I. (c) As t → ∞ t\rightarrow\infty , for any 𝒮 ∈ Φ {\mathcal{S}}\in\Phi , price 𝐩 t \mathbf{p}_{t} converges to the limiting price 𝐩 ∗ = ( p 1 ∗ , … , p I ∗ ) \mathbf{p}^{*}=\big(p^{*}_{1},\ldots,p^{*}_{I}\big) , where (12) p ∗ i = ( θ i 1 β ​ ∏ j = 1 J π i , j 1 α j ) 1 ∑ j = 1 J 1 α j + 1 β ∑ k = 1 I ( θ k 1 β ​ ∏ j = 1 J π k , j 1 α j ) 1 ∑ j = 1 J 1 α j + 1 β , i = 1 , … , I . \displaystyle p^{*}_{i}=\frac{\left(\theta_{i}^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{J}\pi_{i,j}^{\frac{1}{\alpha_{j}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{1}{\alpha_{j}}+\frac{1}{\beta}}}}{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}\left(\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\prod\limits_{j=1}^{J}\pi_{k,j}^{\frac{1}{\alpha_{j}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{1}{\alpha_{j}}+\frac{1}{\beta}}}},~~i=1,\ldots,I. The above result demonstrates that the limiting price corresponds precisely to the geometric mean of the risk-adjusted beliefs held by all participants, including the traders and the market maker. It is worth noting that this limiting price, p i ∗ p_{i}^{*} , remains unaffected by the chosen trading sequence, represented by 𝒮 \mathcal{S} , as long as 𝒮 ∈ Φ \mathcal{S}\in\Phi . Price formulation ( 12 ) is closely connected to the concept of the Logarithm Opinion Pool mentioned in ( Chakraborty and Das 2015 ) 12 12 12 The Logarithm Opinion Pool refers to the normalized weighted geometric mean of different opinions which is originated from the early works such as ( Morris 1974 ) and ( Bordley 1982 ) in area of the opinion pooling. . In ( Chakraborty and Das 2015 ) , as it only focuses on the static trading model, it suggests one potential research direction as extending these findings to a market involving multiple agents engaging in repeated trading until market states converge. By providing an explicit outcome for this scenario, Proposition 4.1 effectively addresses such a question raised by ( Chakraborty and Das 2015 ) . Remark 3. It is also important to note that, although the risk-neutral utility is a special case of the exponential utility, 13 13 13 If we let β → 0 \beta\rightarrow 0 or α j → 0 \alpha_{j}\rightarrow 0 in ( 10 ), then the exponential utilities become risk-neutral utilities, i.e., U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) = ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ y i U(\mathbf{y})=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}y_{i} and V j ​ ( 𝐱 j ) = ∑ i = 1 I π i , j ​ x i , j V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\pi_{i,j}x_{i,j} for all j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J . the result in Proposition 4.1 does not hold for the risk-neutral case as it needs the assumption β &gt; 0 \beta&gt;0 and α j &gt; 0 \alpha_{j}&gt;0 for all j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J . Even when the market maker adopts the exponential utility, but the trader’s utility is risk neutral, the whole trading process may not converge due to violating the strictly convex condition in Assumption 2 . Indeed, under the risk-neutral setting, after the j j -th trader interacts with the market, the price of the i i -th security will be modified exactly to the j j -th trader’s belief, π i , j \pi_{i,j} , which leads to price oscillation (see the detail in Appendix A.7 ). 4.2. Risk Measure-Based Market We have demonstrated that the exponential utility-based market can converge to a unique limiting price, regardless of the trading sequence. This subsection delves deeper into the risk-measure-based MU function, which exhibits a similar property under specific conditions. Although some literature (e.g., ( Hu and Storkey 2014 ; Frongillo et al. 2015 ) ) has investigated the risk-measure-based prediction market and developed convergence results, the existing studies provide limited information about the general convergence condition and the method to compute the limiting price. This section aims to complete these missing pieces. We mainly focus on the convex risk measures, which possess some promising properties ( ( Föllmer and Schied 2011 ) ) that have emerged as a valuable tool in decision-making. However, directly utilizing the convex risk measure to construct the MU functions in trading problem 𝒫 j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) \mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}) may not satisfy the conditions outlined in Assumptions 1 and 2 , which leads to a potential non-convergence in the trading process. To illustrate such a case, we provide a counter-example in Appendix A.3 (Example A.2 ). In order to address this issue, we need additional mild conditions on the risk-measure-based MU functions. Specifically, we assume the j j -th trader’s MU function and the market maker’s MU function are respectively constructed by some risk measures as follows, (13) V j ( 𝐱 j ) = − ρ j ( 𝐱 j ) , j = 1 , … , J , and U ( 𝐲 ) = − ρ 0 ( 𝐲 ) , \displaystyle V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j})=-\rho_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j}),~j=1,\ldots,J,~~~\textrm{and}~~~U(\mathbf{y})=-\rho_{0}(\mathbf{y}), where ρ j ​ ( ⋅ ) : ℝ I → ℝ \rho_{j}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} for j = 0 , 1 , … , J j=0,1,\ldots,J are differentiable convex risk measures. Recall that a convex risk measure has the following dual representation (see e.g., ( Föllmer and Schied 2011 ) ): (14) ρ j ( 𝐱 ) = sup 𝐩 ∈ Θ I { − 𝐩 ⊤ 𝐱 − α j ( 𝐩 ) } = − inf 𝐩 ∈ Θ I { 𝐩 ⊤ 𝐱 + α j ( 𝐩 ) } , \displaystyle\rho_{j}(\mathbf{x})=\sup_{\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}}\big\{-\mathbf{p}^{\top}\mathbf{x}-\alpha_{j}(\mathbf{p})\big\}=-\inf_{\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}}\big\{\mathbf{p}^{\top}\mathbf{x}+\alpha_{j}(\mathbf{p})\big\}, where α j ​ ( ⋅ ) : Θ I → ℝ \alpha_{j}(\cdot):\Theta_{I}\to\mathbb{R} is called the penalty function for j = 0 , … , J j=0,\ldots,J . We assume α j ​ ( ⋅ ) \alpha_{j}(\cdot) is differentiable for all j = 0 , … , J j=0,\ldots,J and define its partial derivative as follows. For some 𝐩 = ( p 1 , p 2 , … , p I ) ∈ Θ I \mathbf{p}=(p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{I})\in\Theta_{I} , the derivative of α j ​ ( 𝐩 ) \alpha_{j}(\mathbf{p}) is denoted as: (15) f i , j ( p i ) ≜ ∂ α j ​ ( 𝐩 ) ∂ p i , i = 1 , … , I , j = 0 , … , J . \displaystyle f_{i,j}(p_{i})\triangleq\frac{\partial\alpha_{j}(\mathbf{p})}{\partial p_{i}},~~~i=1,\ldots,I,~j=0,\ldots,J. If we impose some mild conditions on f i , j ​ ( ⋅ ) f_{i,j}(\cdot) , the market constructed by the risk measures will also converge to a unique limiting state. Proposition 4.2. If the functions { f i , j ​ ( ⋅ ) } | i = 1 , j = 1 I , J \{f_{i,j}(\cdot)\}|_{i=1,j=1}^{I,J} defined in ( 15 ) satisfy the following two conditions: (i) f i , j ​ ( ⋅ ) f_{i,j}(\cdot) is continuously differentiable and strictly increasing; and (ii) f i , j ​ ( 1 ) f_{i,j}(1) is finite and lim p i → 0 ​ f i , j ​ ( p i ) = − ∞ \underset{p_{i}\to 0}{\lim}f_{i,j}(p_{i})=-\infty for all i = 1 , … , I i=1,\ldots,I and j = 0 , … , J j=0,\ldots,J , then the MU function U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) = − ρ 0 ​ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot)=-\rho_{0}(\cdot) satisfies Assumption 1 , the MU functions V j ​ ( ⋅ ) = − ρ j ​ ( ⋅ ) V_{j}(\cdot)=-\rho_{j}(\cdot) , j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J , satisfy Assumption 2 . In addition, both V j ​ ( ⋅ ) V_{j}(\cdot) and U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) satisfy Assumption 3 . Thus, the market constructed by ( 13 ) meets the conditions in Theorem 3.2 and the market state converges to a Pareto optimal allocation. Based on the above result, to check whether the market constructed by the risk measures ( 13 ) converges, we only need to check whether the penalty functions { α j ​ ( ⋅ ) } | j = 0 J \{\alpha_{j}(\cdot)\}|_{j=0}^{J} meet the conditions in Proposition 4.2 . We then focus on characterizing the limiting wealth allocation and the correspondent limiting price. We have the following result. Theorem 4.3. In risk measure-based market which satisfies the condition in Proposition 4.2 , the following results are true: (i) The limiting wealth allocation is the unique solution of problem 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝛎 ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) by setting 𝛎 = 𝐞 \boldsymbol{\nu}=\mathbf{e} . (ii) For any trading sequence 𝒮 ∈ Φ {\mathcal{S}}\in\Phi , the unique limit price generated by Trading Process 1 is 𝐩 ∗ = − ∇ ρ 0 ​ ( 𝐲 ∗ ) \mathbf{p}^{*}=-\nabla\rho_{0}(\mathbf{y}^{*}) where 𝐲 ∗ = w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 − ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j ∗ \mathbf{y}^{*}={w}_{0}^{\textrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{e}-\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{*} and ( 𝐱 1 ∗ , … , 𝐱 J ∗ ) (\mathbf{x}^{*}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{*}_{J}) are the solution of problem 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝐞 ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\mathbf{e}) . Furthermore, the price vector 𝐩 ∗ \mathbf{p}^{*} is given by 𝐩 ∗ = arg ⁡ min 𝐩 ∈ Θ I ​ ∑ j = 0 J α j ​ ( 𝐩 ) \mathbf{p}^{*}=\arg\underset{\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}}{\min}~\sum\limits_{j=0}^{J}~\alpha_{j}(\mathbf{p}) where α j ​ ( ⋅ ) \alpha_{j}(\cdot) is defined in ( 14 ) for j = 0 , 1 , … , J j=0,1,\ldots,J . One surprising result of this theorem is that the limiting price can be independent of the initial wealth allocation. The root cause is the translation invariance property of risk measures. Indeed, considering the j j -th trader’s decision problem, the objective function min 𝐳 ⁡ { ρ j ​ ( w j , 0 ⋅ 𝐞 + 𝐳 ) } \min_{\mathbf{z}}\{\rho_{j}(w_{j,0}\cdot\mathbf{e}+\mathbf{z})\} is equivalent to min 𝐳 ⁡ { ρ j ​ ( 𝐳 ) } \min_{\mathbf{z}}\{\rho_{j}(\mathbf{z})\} . Therefore, the initial wealth w j , 0 w_{j,0} can initially be eliminated from the individual problem. Hence, all subsequent trades become independent of the initial wealth. A similar observation holds for the market maker. As a result, the limiting 𝐲 ∗ \mathbf{y}^{*} , which determines the limiting price 𝐩 ∗ \mathbf{p}^{*} , is independent of the initial wealth allocation. With the help of Theorem 4.3 , it becomes feasible to explicitly compute the limiting price 𝐩 ∗ \mathbf{p}^{*} when the formulation of the risk measures ρ j ​ ( ⋅ ) \rho_{j}(\cdot) is provided. We now consider a particular type of risk measure constructed using a dual formulation. We maintain the same notations as in Section 4.1 , employing { θ i } | i = 1 I \{\theta_{i}\}|_{i=1}^{I} and { π i , j } | i = 1 I \{\pi_{i,j}\}|_{i=1}^{I} to represent the beliefs of the market maker and the j j -th trader, respectively. The following result establishes that the limiting price can be expressed as the power-weighted mean of all participants’ beliefs. Corollary 4.4. Let h j ≥ 0 h_{j}\geq 0 , j = 0 , … , J j=0,\ldots,J , be some parameters. If the penalty functions α j ​ ( ⋅ ) \alpha_{j}(\cdot) take the following form, α 0 ( 𝐩 ) = − 1 γ ∑ i = 1 I p i ( p i / θ i ) γ − 1 h 0 \alpha_{0}(\mathbf{p})=-\frac{1}{\gamma}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I}p_{i}\big(p_{i}/\theta_{i}\big)^{\gamma-1}h_{0} and α j ​ ( 𝐩 ) \alpha_{j}(\mathbf{p}) = = − 1 γ ∑ i = 1 I p i ( p i / π i , j ) γ − 1 h j -\frac{1}{\gamma}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I}p_{i}\big(p_{i}/\pi_{i,j}\big)^{\gamma-1}h_{j} for j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J with some γ &lt; 1 \gamma&lt;1 , then the limiting price is (16) p i ∗ = ( h 0 ⋅ θ i 1 1 − γ + ∑ j = 1 J h j ⋅ π i , j 1 1 − γ ) 1 − γ ∑ k = 1 I ( h 0 ⋅ θ k 1 1 − γ + ∑ j = 1 J h j ⋅ π k , j 1 1 − γ ) 1 − γ \displaystyle p_{i}^{*}=\frac{\left(h_{0}\cdot\theta_{i}^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}h_{j}\cdot\pi_{i,j}^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}\right)^{1-\gamma}}{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}\Big(h_{0}\cdot\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}h_{j}\cdot\pi_{k,j}^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}\Big)^{1-\gamma}}\quad for i = 1 , … , I i=1,\ldots,I . The price formula ( 16 ) shows that the limit price is a weighted power mean of the traders’ beliefs where the weight is a trader dependent parameter h j h_{j} . When γ → 0 \gamma\rightarrow 0 , the penalty function becomes the cross entropy , i.e., α 0 ( 𝐩 ) = − ∑ i = 1 I θ i log ( p i ) h 0 \alpha_{0}(\mathbf{p})=-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}\log(p_{i})h_{0} with α j = − ∑ i = 1 I π i , j log ( p i ) h j \alpha_{j}=-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I}\pi_{i,j}\log(p_{i})h_{j} for j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J , and the corresponding limiting price becomes the arithmetic weighted mean of trader beliefs. Regarding the selection of parameter h j h_{j} , one particular choice is the initial wealth of each trader. In some application, such as the artificial prediction market ( Chakravorti et al. 2023 ) , these parameters can be viewed as the hyper-parameters which control the overall model performance. 5. Limiting Price for CRRA Utility 5.1. Price Variability The preceding section demonstrates that, under certain conditions, the price sequence produced by the exponential utility-based and risk-measure-based markets can converge to a unique limit price for any trading sequence that satisfies the IP property. This motivates us to inquire if a similiar outcome can be extended to more commonly used utility functions. In this section, we mainly explore a market model constructed by the utility with constant relative risk aversion (i.e., CRRA utility). The CRRA utility enjoys widespread usage in economic modeling and analysis. In the CRRA utility-based market, we assume the market maker’s and the j j -th trader’s MU functions are (17) U ( 𝐲 ) = 1 γ m ∑ i = 1 I θ i ⋅ y i γ m and V j ( 𝐱 j ) = 1 γ ∑ i = 1 I π i , j ⋅ x i , j γ , j = 1 , … , J , \displaystyle U(\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{\gamma_{m}}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}\cdot y_{i}^{\gamma_{m}}~~\textrm{and}~~V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j})=\frac{1}{\gamma}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\pi_{i,j}\cdot x_{i,j}^{\gamma},~~j=1,\ldots,J, respectively, where γ &lt; 1 \gamma&lt;1 and γ m &lt; 1 \gamma_{m}&lt;1 are the traders’ and market maker’s risk aversion parameters, respectively; 𝐲 ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} and 𝐱 j ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{x}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} are the wealth vectors; ( θ 1 , … , θ I ) ∈ Θ I (\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{I})\in\Theta_{I} and ( π 1 , j , … , π I , j ) ∈ Θ I (\pi_{1,j},\ldots,\pi_{I,j})\in\Theta_{I} are the associated subjective beliefs as we defined in previous sections. Under the above setting, the trader’s decision problem 𝒫 j ​ ( 𝐱 j ) \mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j}) generally lacks a closed-form solution. Nonetheless, when all participants have identical risk parameters, the trader’s decision can be solved as follows. Proposition 5.1. In the CRRA uitlity-based market definied by ( 17 ) with γ = γ m \gamma=\gamma_{m} , given { 𝐱 j , t } | j = 1 J \{\mathbf{x}_{j,t}\}|_{j=1}^{J} and 𝐲 t \mathbf{y}_{t} at time t t , the solution of the j j -th trader’s decision problem 𝒫 j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) \mathcal{P}_{j}{(\mathbf{x}_{j,t})} is z i ∗ = ( y i , t ⋅ κ i , j − x i , j , t ⋅ λ j ∗ ) / ( λ j ∗ + κ i , j ) z^{*}_{i}=(y_{i,t}\cdot\kappa_{i,j}-x_{i,j,t}\cdot\lambda^{*}_{j})/(\lambda^{*}_{j}+\kappa_{i,j}) for i = 1 , … , I i=1,\ldots,I , and the market states are updated to (18) y i , t + 1 = λ j ∗ κ i , j + λ j ∗ ​ ( x i , j , t + y i , t ) , x i , j , t + 1 = κ i , j κ i , j + λ j ∗ ​ ( x i , j , t + y i , t ) , \displaystyle y_{i,t+1}=\frac{\lambda_{j}^{*}}{\kappa_{i,j}+\lambda_{j}^{*}}\big(x_{i,j,t}+y_{i,t}\big),~~x_{i,j,t+1}=\frac{\kappa_{i,j}}{\kappa_{i,j}+\lambda_{j}^{*}}\big(x_{i,j,t}+y_{i,t}\big), where κ i , j ≜ ( π i , j / θ i ) 1 1 − γ \kappa_{i,j}\triangleq\big(\pi_{i,j}\big/\theta_{i}\big)^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}} , for i = 1 , … , I i=1,\ldots,I and j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J , and λ j ∗ &gt; 0 \lambda_{j}^{*}&gt;0 is the solution to the following equation, (19) ∑ i = 1 I ( λ j ∗ λ j ∗ + κ i , j ​ ( x i , j , t + y i , t ) ) γ ​ θ i = W 0 γ . \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{I}\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}^{*}}{\lambda_{j}^{*}+\kappa_{i,j}}(x_{i,j,t}+y_{i,t})\right)^{\gamma}\theta_{i}=W_{0}^{\gamma}. This proposition shows that in a CRRA-utility-based market, the initial wealth of each trader is recursively adapted into the wealth vector 𝐲 t \mathbf{y}_{t} of the market maker, hence the limiting 𝐲 ∗ \mathbf{y}^{*} may be affected by the initial wealth allocation, as opposed to the risk measure-based markets. The following example demonstrates how the initial wealth, risk parameters, and trading sequence could affect limiting price. Example 5.2. We consider a CRRA utility-based market with three assets ( I = 3 I=3 ) and two traders ( J = 2 J=2 ) whose belief are 𝝅 1 = ( 0.2 , 0.2 , 0.6 ) \boldsymbol{\pi}_{1}=(0.2,0.2,0.6) and 𝝅 2 = ( 0.6 , 0.1 , 0.3 ) \boldsymbol{\pi}_{2}=(0.6,0.1,0.3) , respectively. The market maker’s parameters are θ 1 = θ 2 = θ 3 = 1 / 3 \theta_{1}=\theta_{2}=\theta_{3}=1/3 , W 0 = 5 W_{0}=5 and γ m = γ \gamma_{m}=\gamma . The two traders’ initial wealth are w 1 , 0 = w 1 w_{1,0}=w^{1} and w 2 , 0 = w 2 w_{2,0}=w^{2} where we vary the parameters ( w 1 , w 2 ) (w^{1},w^{2}) to examine their impact on the limiting prices. In Trading Process 1 , we consider two trading sequences: 𝒮 1 = { 1 , 2 , 1 , 2 , … } \mathcal{S}_{1}=\{1,2,1,2,\ldots\} and 𝒮 2 = { 2 , 1 , 2 , 1 , … } \mathcal{S}_{2}=\{2,1,2,1,\ldots\} . 14 14 14 Note that if the same trader arrives twice in a row, the second trade is vacuous because the first one is already utility maximizing. Figure 1(a) depicts the utility value trajectories generated by these trading processes for different ( w 1 , w 2 ) (w^{1},w^{2}) when γ = 0.5 \gamma=0.5 . The Pareto efficient frontier (represented by the solid line) is obtained by solving problem 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) for different 𝝂 \boldsymbol{\nu} . This figure illustrates that, for a given wealth distribution and fixed trading sequence (either 𝒮 1 \mathcal{S}_{1} or 𝒮 2 \mathcal{S}_{2} ), the utility value trajectories indeed converge to points on the Pareto efficient frontier, confirming the result in Theorem 3.2 . However, even for identical initial wealth, different trading sequences may generate different utility paths (and hence different limiting prices). Figure 1(b) demonstrates the impact of the risk parameter. It shows the price trajectories of p 1 , t p_{1,t} and p 3 , t p_{3,t} when w 1 = w 2 = 10 w^{1}=w^{2}=10 for the trading sequence S 1 S_{1} . This illustration makes it clear that a change in the risk aversion parameter, e.g., from γ = 0.5 \gamma=0.5 to γ = 0.7 \gamma=0.7 , has an effect on the limiting prices. Table 1 provides details of the limiting prices for different ( w 1 , w 2 ) (w^{1},w^{2}) and γ \gamma . A closer examination reveals that even with fixed initial wealth, the limiting price may vary under different trading sequences, although both 𝒮 1 \mathcal{S}_{1} and 𝒮 2 \mathcal{S}_{2} satisfy the IP property. 15 15 15 It is worth noting that when there are only two traders, 𝒮 1 \mathcal{S}_{1} and 𝒮 2 \mathcal{S}_{2} are effectively the only possible sequences that satisfy the IP property. The impact of the trading sequence is also discussed in ( Sethi and Vaughan 2016 ) . However, among these factors, the trading sequence has less impact compared to the initial wealth and the risk parameter. Figure 1 . The utility and price trajectories in Example 5.2 (a) Trajectory of the utility (b) Trajectory of the price Table 1. Limiting wealth allocation and price in Example 5.2 Wealth Risk Limiting Price w 1 w^{1} w 2 w^{2} γ \gamma p 1 ∗ p_{1}^{*} p 2 ∗ p_{2}^{*} p 3 ∗ p_{3}^{*} 10 10 0.5 (0.7) 𝒮 1 : \mathcal{S}_{1}: 0.4044 (0.3959) 0.1839 (0.1849) 0.4117 (0.4192) 𝒮 2 : \mathcal{S}_{2}: 0.4045 (0.4004) 0.1838 (0.1841) 0.4117 (0.4155) 8 12 0.5 (0.7) 𝒮 1 : \mathcal{S}_{1}: 0.4436 (0.4449) 0.1745 (0.1726) 0.3819 (0.3825) 𝒮 2 : \mathcal{S}_{2}: 0.4440 (0.4800) 0.1743 (0.1741) 0.3817 (0.3459) 12 8 0.5 (0.7) 𝒮 1 : \mathcal{S}_{1}: 0.3624 (0.3463) 0.1933 (0.1959) 0.4443 (0.4578) 𝒮 2 : \mathcal{S}_{2}: 0.3621 (0.3776) 0.1933 (0.2044) 0.4446 (0.4180) As demonstrated in Example 5.2 , the trading sequence can impact the limiting price. A key observation for understanding this phenomenon is that 𝒮 1 \mathcal{S}_{1} can be equivalently viewed as 𝒮 2 \mathcal{S}_{2} preceded by an extra Trader 1 action. Such an action results in an initial market state different from that of the original 𝒮 2 \mathcal{S}_{2} . As Proposition 5.1 suggests, different initial wealth results in a different limiting price; thus the limiting prices for the two sequences 𝒮 1 \mathcal{S}_{1} and 𝒮 2 \mathcal{S}_{2} are different. To illustrate, we consider Example 5.2 with w 1 = w 2 = 10 w^{1}=w^{2}=10 . The extra action of 𝒮 1 \mathcal{S}_{1} (the first trade) yields market states 𝐱 1 = ( 7.49 , 7.49.13.50 ) \mathbf{x}_{1}=(7.49,7.49.13.50) , 𝐱 2 = ( 10 , 10 , 10 ) \mathbf{x}_{2}=(10,10,10) , and 𝐲 = ( 7.51 , 7.51 , 1.50 ) \mathbf{y}=(7.51,7.51,1.50) . After the first trade, sequence 𝒮 1 \mathcal{S}_{1} becomes identical to 𝒮 2 \mathcal{S}_{2} . However, for 𝒮 2 \mathcal{S}_{2} , the initial condition is 𝐱 1 = ( 10 , 10 , 10 ) \mathbf{x}_{1}=(10,10,10) , 𝐱 2 = ( 10 , 10 , 10 ) \mathbf{x}_{2}=(10,10,10) , and 𝐲 = ( 5 , 5 , 5 ) \mathbf{y}=(5,5,5) , which is different from the states yield from 𝒮 1 \mathcal{S}_{1} after the first trading, thus the two sequences result in different limiting prices. Practically, traders interact with the market maker randomly, leading to a random trading path. Consequently, the limiting price becomes a trading-sequence-dependent random variable. However, our next result characterizes the limiting price for a given trading sequence and demonstrates that price invariance can be achieved when the trader population is large. Theorem 5.3. For the CRRA-utility-based market, we have the following results: (i) Given a trading sequence 𝒮 ∈ Φ \mathcal{S}\in\Phi , the Trading Process 1 converges to the limiting wealth allocation { 𝐱 j ∗ } \{\mathbf{x}_{j}^{*}\} characterized by problem 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝛎 ∗ ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{*}) with some weights 𝛎 ∗ = ( ν 1 ∗ , … , ν J ∗ ) ∈ ℝ + J ∖ { 𝟎 } \boldsymbol{\nu}^{*}=(\nu_{1}^{*},\ldots,\nu_{J}^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{J}_{+}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\} ; and the limiting price is given by (20) p i ∗ = ξ ∗ i / ∑ k = 1 I ξ k ∗ , i = 1 , … , I , \displaystyle p_{i}^{*}=\xi^{*}_{i}\Big/\sum_{k=1}^{I}\xi_{k}^{*},~~~i=1,\ldots,I, where 𝛏 ∗ = ( ξ 1 ∗ , … , ξ I ∗ ) \boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}=(\xi_{1}^{*},\ldots,\xi_{I}^{*}) is the unique solution of the following equations: (21) ξ i ∗ \displaystyle\xi_{i}^{*} = ( ∑ j = 1 J ( ν j ∗ ) 1 1 − γ ⋅ π i , j 1 1 − γ + ( ξ i ∗ ) γ − γ m ( 1 − γ ) ​ ( 1 − γ m ) ⋅ ℒ ⁡ ( 𝝃 ∗ , 𝜽 ) ⋅ W 0 ⋅ θ i 1 1 − γ m w 0 all ) 1 − γ , i = 1 , … , I \displaystyle=\Bigg(\frac{\sum\limits^{J}_{j=1}(\nu_{j}^{*})^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}\cdot\pi_{i,j}^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}+(\xi_{i}^{*})^{\frac{\gamma-\gamma_{m}}{(1-\gamma)(1-\gamma_{m})}}\cdot\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta})\cdot W_{0}\cdot\theta_{i}^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma_{m}}}}{w_{0}^{\textrm{all}}}\Bigg)^{1-\gamma},~~i=1,\ldots,I where ℒ ⁡ ( 𝛏 , 𝛉 ) ≜ ( ∑ k = 1 I θ k ​ ( θ k / ξ k ) γ m 1 − γ m ) − 1 γ m \mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\theta})\triangleq\left(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}\theta_{k}(\theta_{k}/\xi_{k})^{\frac{\gamma_{m}}{1-\gamma_{m}}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\gamma_{m}}} . (ii) In Trading Process 1 , if traders’ beliefs 𝛑 j \boldsymbol{\pi}_{j} and initial wealth w j , 0 w_{j,0} are independently and identically distributed random variables for j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J ; with each trader having an equal probability of trading with the market maker at each trading instance, then the solution { ξ i ∗ } | i = 1 I \{\xi_{i}^{*}\}|_{i=1}^{I} defined in Eq. ( 21 ) and the associated limiting price { p i ∗ } | i = 1 I \{p_{i}^{*}\}|_{i=1}^{I} generated by each trading sample path converge almost surely to a pair of constant vectors as the trader population J → ∞ J\rightarrow\infty . The first result in Theorem 5.3 provides an explicit method for computing the limiting price 𝐩 ∗ \mathbf{p}^{*} for a sepecified trading sequence with the associated weighting parameters 𝝂 ∗ \boldsymbol{\nu}^{*} . It is worth noting that the functional form of the limiting price ( 20 ) and ( 21 ) remain consistent, as the influence of the trading sequence is solely reflected through the weights 𝝂 ∗ \boldsymbol{\nu}^{*} . The second result in Theorem 5.3 is an interesting product of the price formula. One can consider it a prediction market analogue of the “Law of Large Numbers” . It implies that in a randomly trading market with a large trader population, the variation of limiting price induced by different trading sequences becomes negligible. This underscores the significance of trader participation in prediction markets, as it not only enhances liquidity but also increases the reliability of limiting prices. Imagine a prediction market that yields predictions (limiting price) that are heavily dependent on the trading sequence. In such a case, one cannot know whether its prediction truly represents the overall beliefs or only happens to be a biased result of a specific trading sequence. Such uncertainty will undermine the interpretability of prices and jeopardize the prediction accuracy. Theorem 5.3 also provides several potentially useful insights for market maker designing. Combining Eqs. ( 20 ) and ( 21 ), the limiting price has the following decomposition: (22) p i ∗ = ( ∑ j = 1 J ( ν j ∑ k = 1 I ξ k ∗ ) 1 ( 1 − γ ) w 0 all ( π i , j ) 1 1 − γ + ( p i ∗ ) γ − γ m ( 1 − γ ) ​ ( 1 − γ m ) ⋅ ℒ ( 𝐩 ∗ , 𝜽 ) W 0 w 0 all θ i 1 1 − γ m ) 1 − γ , i = 1 , … , I . \displaystyle p_{i}^{*}=\Bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{\left(\frac{\nu_{j}}{\sum_{k=1}^{I}\xi_{k}^{*}}\right)^{\frac{1}{(1-\gamma)}}}{w_{0}^{\textrm{all}}}\left(\pi_{i,j}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}+(p_{i}^{*})^{\frac{\gamma-\gamma_{m}}{(1-\gamma)(1-\gamma_{m})}}\cdot\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{p}^{*},\bm{\theta})\frac{W_{0}}{w_{0}^{\textrm{all}}}\theta_{i}^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma_{m}}}\Bigg)^{1-\gamma},\quad i=1,\ldots,I. This decomposition can be explained as follows. First, a smaller initial wealth W 0 W_{0} can reduce the market maker’s impact on the limiting price. Second, a market maker with higher risk aversion (smaller γ m \gamma_{m} ) also diminishes its influence on the limiting price because ( p i ∗ ) γ − γ m ( 1 − γ ) ​ ( 1 − γ m ) (p_{i}^{*})^{\frac{\gamma-\gamma_{m}}{(1-\gamma)(1-\gamma_{m})}} is a decreasing function of γ m \gamma_{m} for p i ∗ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) p_{i}^{*}\in(0,1) . Third, the function ℒ ⁡ ( 𝐩 ∗ , 𝜽 ) \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{p}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}) exhibits an intriguing suppressive effect designed to mitigate the price distortion caused by the market maker’ externality. To explain further, ℒ ⁡ ( 𝐩 ∗ , 𝜽 ) \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{p}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}) reaches its maximum value of 1 1 when 𝐩 ∗ = 𝜽 \mathbf{p}^{*}=\boldsymbol{\theta} . When the market consensus 𝐩 ∗ \mathbf{p}^{*} significantly deviates from the market maker’s belief 𝜽 \boldsymbol{\theta} , the term ℒ ⁡ ( 𝐩 ∗ , 𝜽 ) \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{p}^{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}) lessens its impact, effectively reducing the market maker’s influence on the limiting price. It is worth noting that such a suppressive effect stems from the utility preserving condition ( 8 ), underscoring the additional benefits of the MU-based mechanism. With the above results, in the next section, we investigate how to approximate the limiting price under a finite trader scenario through choosing an appropriate weight 𝝂 ∗ \boldsymbol{\nu}^{*} . 5.2. Heuristic Weighting Parameters and Price Formula The previous section has emphasized the vital role of determining the weighting parameters, 𝝂 ∗ \boldsymbol{\nu}^{*} , in solving problem 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ∗ ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{*}) to establish the limiting price. Given the practical difficulty of obtaining the “true” weighting parameters 𝝂 ∗ \boldsymbol{\nu}^{*} before trading converges, we propose an approximation using deterministic weights. Drawing inspiration from previous analysis, which highlights Pareto optimality of the limiting wealth allocation and underscores the significance of both the initial wealth distribution w j , 0 w_{j,0} and the risk parameter γ \gamma in shaping the limiting price (e.g., see Table 1 ), we introduce the concept of Pareto Optimal Induced (POI) weights, denoted as 𝝂 ^ = ( ν ^ 1 , … , ν ^ J ) \hat{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=(\hat{\nu}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\nu}_{J}) defined as: (23) ν ^ j = ( w j , 0 ) 1 − γ , j = 1 , … , J . \displaystyle\hat{\nu}_{j}=\big(w_{j,0}\big)^{1-\gamma},~~j=1,\ldots,J. Applying Theorem 5.3 , we replace 𝝂 ∗ \boldsymbol{\nu}^{*} with the POI weights 𝝂 ^ \hat{\boldsymbol{\nu}} in Eq. ( 21 ) to calculate the associated POI limiting price, denoted as 𝐩 ^ = ( p ^ 1 , … , p ^ I ) \hat{\mathbf{p}}=(\hat{p}_{1},\ldots,\hat{p}_{I}) , with its components given as follows: (24) p ^ i = ξ ^ i / ∑ k = 1 I ξ ^ k , i = 1 , … , I , \displaystyle\hat{p}_{i}=\hat{\xi}_{i}\Big/\sum_{k=1}^{I}\hat{\xi}_{k},~~~i=1,\ldots,I,\ where 𝝃 ^ = ( ξ ^ 1 , … , ξ ^ I ) \hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\hat{\xi}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\xi}_{I}) is the unique solution of the following equation, (25) ξ ^ i = ( ∑ j = 1 J w j , 0 ⋅ π i , j 1 1 − γ + ξ ^ i γ − γ m ( 1 − γ ) ​ ( 1 − γ m ) ⋅ ℒ ⁡ ( 𝝃 ^ , 𝜽 ) ⋅ W 0 ⋅ θ i 1 1 − γ m w 0 all ) 1 − γ , i = 1 , … , I . \displaystyle\hat{\xi}_{i}=\Bigg(\frac{\sum\limits^{J}_{j=1}w_{j,0}\cdot\pi_{i,j}^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}+\hat{\xi}_{i}^{\frac{\gamma-\gamma_{m}}{(1-\gamma)(1-\gamma_{m})}}\cdot\mathcal{L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\theta})\cdot W_{0}\cdot\theta_{i}^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma_{m}}}}{w_{0}^{\textrm{all}}}\Bigg)^{1-\gamma},~~i=1,\ldots,I. Now we provide some interpretation for the POI limiting price. Eq ( 25 ) implies that the price p ^ i \hat{p}_{i} is directly linked to a weighted average that considers all participants’ wealth and risk-adjusted beliefs regarding the i i -th asset. More specifically, the term ∑ j = 1 J w j , 0 ⋅ π i , j 1 1 − γ \sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}w_{j,0}\cdot\pi_{i,j}^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}} represents the avarage risk-adjusted belief contributed from all the traders weighted by their own wealth. In contrast, the term ξ ^ i γ − γ m ( 1 − γ ) ​ ( 1 − γ m ) ⋅ ℒ ⁡ ( 𝝃 ^ , 𝜽 ) ⋅ W 0 ⋅ θ i 1 1 − γ m \hat{\xi}_{i}^{\frac{\gamma-\gamma_{m}}{(1-\gamma)(1-\gamma_{m})}}\cdot\mathcal{L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\theta})\cdot W_{0}\cdot\theta_{i}^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma_{m}}} which combines the risk difference and belief divergence, controls the contribution of market maker’s risk-adjusted belief in such a price. The POI price formula ( 24 ) and ( 25 ) encompass specific market scenarios as special cases. When both market maker and traders adopt the logarithmic utilities, i.e., γ = γ m = 0 \gamma=\gamma_{m}=0 in ( 17 ), Eq. ( 25 ) will simplify to: (26) ξ ^ i = ∑ j = 1 J w j , 0 ⋅ π i , j + ℒ 0 ​ ( 𝝃 ^ , 𝜽 ) ⋅ W 0 ⋅ θ i w 0 all , i = 1 , … , I , \displaystyle\hat{\xi}_{i}=\frac{\sum\limits^{J}_{j=1}w_{j,0}\cdot\pi_{i,j}+\mathcal{L}_{0}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\theta})\cdot W_{0}\cdot\theta_{i}}{w_{0}^{\textrm{all}}},~~~i=1,\ldots,I, where ℒ 0 ( 𝝃 ^ , 𝜽 ) = exp ( − ∑ k = 1 I θ k log ( θ k / ξ ^ k ) ) \mathcal{L}_{0}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\theta})=\exp(-\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}\theta_{k}\log(\theta_{k}/\hat{\xi}_{k})) representing the exponential Kullback-Leibler Divergence (KLD) between 𝜽 \boldsymbol{\theta} and 𝝃 ^ \hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}} . If we further set ℒ 0 ​ ( 𝝃 ^ , 𝜽 ) ≡ 1 \mathcal{L}_{0}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}},\boldsymbol{\theta})\equiv 1 then the price formula ( 24 ) becomes the wealth-weighted average of market beilefs discussed in ( Sethi and Vaughan 2016 ) . We would like to highlight that the solution methodology for deriving the CRRA-utility based limiting price, e.g., Eq. ( 21 ) and the POI weights ( 23 ) remain applicable even when market participant’s MU functions extend beyond the CRRA family of utilities. One example is the hybrid market model where the market maker adopts exponential utility, as given by Eq. ( 10 ), while the traders adopt logarithmic utility. In this scenario, the POI limiting price remains the same as ( 24 ), but the equations for 𝝃 ^ \hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}} become: (27) ξ ^ i = ∑ j = 1 J w j , 0 ⋅ π i , j + 1 β ​ ξ ^ i ​ log ⁡ ( θ i / ξ ^ i ∑ k = 1 I ξ ^ k ) ∑ j = 1 J w j , 0 , i = 1 , 2 , … , I . \displaystyle\hat{\xi}_{i}=\frac{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}w_{j,0}\cdot\pi_{i,j}+\frac{1}{\beta}\hat{\xi}_{i}\log\left(\theta_{i}\Big/\frac{\hat{\xi}_{i}}{\sum_{k=1}^{I}\hat{\xi}_{k}}\right)}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}w_{j,0}},\quad i=1,2,...,I. We provide more details of Eq. ( 27 ) in Appendix A.5 . Our numerical experiments (given in Appendix A.5 ) demonstrate that the price formula ( 27 ) outperforms the conventional wealth-weighted average price reported in ( Sethi and Vaughan 2016 ) . The CRRA-utility-based pricing formula can also be extended to a more comprehensive market model where all participants adopt utilities with hyperbolic absolute risk aversion (HARA utility), which finds wide applications in economic modeling. A brief discussion of this model is presented in Appendix A.6 . 5.3. Evaluation of Approximation Scheme In this section, we first employ simulation to validate the accuracy of the POI weights ( 23 ) and the price formula ( 24 ) in estimating the true weighting coefficients and the true limiting price, respectively. Following this validation, we proceed to examine how the risk parameters and initial wealth impact the accuracy of our approximation. We consider a CRRA-utility-based market with I = 3 I=3 , W 0 = 10 W_{0}=10 , and θ 1 = θ 2 = θ 3 = 1 / 3 \theta_{1}=\theta_{2}=\theta_{3}=1/3 . Traders are generated randomly as follows. The j j -th trader’s belief is sampled from 𝝅 j = α ⋅ 𝝅 ¯ + ( 1 − α ) ⋅ ϵ / | ϵ | \boldsymbol{\pi}_{j}=\alpha\cdot\bar{\boldsymbol{\pi}}+(1-\alpha)\cdot\boldsymbol{\epsilon}/|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}| , where 𝝅 ¯ \bar{\boldsymbol{\pi}} is the baseline belief evenly sampled from ( 0.6 , 0.2 , 0.2 ) (0.6,0.2,0.2) and ( 0.2 , 0.2 , 0.6 ) (0.2,0.2,0.6) ; ϵ \boldsymbol{\epsilon} is uniformly distributed random noise on [ 0 , 1 ] 3 [0,1]^{3} , and α = 0.5 \alpha=0.5 controls the market belief structure. We conduct simulations according to Trading Process 1 , with each trader randomly selected with equal probability to interact with the market maker at each time period. For each set of parameters, we perform N = 5 × J N=5\times J rounds of simulation, and we denote the k k -th sample of the limiting wealth allocation as { 𝐱 1 sim ​ ( k ) , … , 𝐱 J sim ​ ( k ) } | k = 1 N \{\mathbf{x}_{1}^{\textrm{sim}}(k),\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}^{\textrm{sim}}(k)\}|_{k=1}^{N} . • Evaluation by limiting wealth: To verify the accuracy of the POI weights ( 23 ), we first solve the problem 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ^ ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\nu}}) whose solution is denoted as { 𝐱 ^ 1 , … , 𝐱 ^ J } \{\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{J}\} . If the heuristic weight 𝝂 ^ \hat{\boldsymbol{\nu}} provides an accurate approximation for the “true” weighting parameter, then the wealth allocation { 𝐱 ^ 1 , … , 𝐱 ^ J } \{\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{J}\} should be close to the sample limiting wealth allocation { 𝐱 1 sim ​ ( k ) , … , 𝐱 J sim ​ ( k ) } | k = 1 N \{\mathbf{x}_{1}^{\textrm{sim}}(k),\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}^{\textrm{sim}}(k)\}|_{k=1}^{N} generated by the simulation. We introduce the following quantity to measure the difference for each sample, δ 𝐱 ​ ( k ) ≜ ∑ j = 1 J ‖ 𝐱 j sim ​ ( k ) − 𝐱 ^ j ‖ / ∑ j = 1 J ‖ 𝐱 j sim ​ ( k ) ‖ , \displaystyle\delta_{\mathbf{x}}(k)\triangleq\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}\|\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\textrm{sim}}(k)-\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}\|\big/\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}\|\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\textrm{sim}}(k)\|, for k = 1 , … , N k=1,\ldots,N . The sample mean and variance of δ 𝐱 ​ ( k ) \delta_{\mathbf{x}}(k) is denoted by E ​ [ δ 𝐱 ] \textrm{E}[\delta_{\mathbf{x}}] and Var ​ [ δ 𝐱 ] \textrm{Var}[\delta_{\mathbf{x}}] , respectively. • Evaluation by limiting price: We denote the price associated with the k k -th sample of limting wealth as 𝐩 sim ​ ( k ) \mathbf{p}^{\textrm{sim}}(k) and we compute the POI price 𝐩 ^ \hat{\mathbf{p}} by using ( 24 ). To measure the difference between these prices, we employ the Kullback-Leibler Divergence (KLD), i.e., given two prices 𝐩 a , 𝐩 b ∈ Δ I \mathbf{p}^{a},\mathbf{p}^{b}\in\Delta_{I} , the KLD of these prices is defined as 𝒟 ⁡ ( 𝐩 a , 𝐩 b ) ≜ ∑ i = 1 I p i a ​ ln ⁡ ( p i a / p i b ) \mathcal{D}(\mathbf{p}^{a},\mathbf{p}^{b})\triangleq\sum_{i=1}^{I}p^{a}_{i}\ln\left(p^{a}_{i}/p^{b}_{i}\right) . We then denote the KLD between the k k -th sample price 𝐩 sim ​ ( k ) \mathbf{p}^{\textrm{sim}}(k) and the POI price 𝐩 ^ \hat{\mathbf{p}} as δ 𝐩 ​ ( k ) ≜ 𝒟 ⁡ ( 𝐩 sim ​ ( k ) , 𝐩 ^ ) \delta_{\mathbf{p}}(k)\triangleq\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{p}^{\textrm{sim}}(k),\hat{\mathbf{p}}) for k = 1 , … , N k=1,\ldots,N . We then compute the sample mean and sample variance of δ 𝐩 ​ ( k ) \delta_{\mathbf{p}}(k) as E ​ [ δ 𝐩 ] \text{E}[\delta_{\mathbf{p}}] and Var ​ [ δ 𝐩 ] \textrm{Var}[\delta_{\mathbf{p}}] , respectively. Table 2 reports the comparative results for different setups ( γ m , γ ∈ { − 2 , 0.5 } \gamma_{m},\gamma\in\{-2,0.5\} and J ∈ { 50,100,200,400 } J\in\{50,100,200,400\} ). For each set of risk parameters, denoted as ( γ m \gamma_{m} , γ \gamma ), we list the results for different values of J J . The results clearly show that, regardless of the different settings, the POI weights ( 23 ) and the POI price ( 24 ) perform exceptionally well, exhibiting minimal disparities in both wealth and price. A closer examination reveals several noteworthy patterns. Firstly, for a fixed set of risk parameters γ m \gamma_{m} and γ \gamma , increasing the trader population J J enhances the accuracy of our approximation. This enhancement is evident as all error and variation indicators decrease with the increase in J J . Of particular significance is the observation that Table 2 also highlights a notable decrease in approximation variances, Var ​ [ δ 𝐱 ] \textrm{Var}[\delta_{\mathbf{x}}] and Var ​ [ δ 𝐩 ] \textrm{Var}[\delta_{\mathbf{p}}] , with a larger population. This reduction in variance can be attributed to the decrease in variation stemming from the trading sequence. Consequently, this pattern confirms the second result outlined in Theorem 5.3 , which posits that a larger population leads to a more stable limit price. Secondly, for a given trader population, the approximation error is smaller in scenarios with γ m = − 2 \gamma_{m}=-2 compared to those with γ m = 0.5 \gamma_{m}=0.5 , suggesting that our approximation is more favorable for higher levels of risk aversion.
