To better illustrate the impact of these parameters, we introduce the conventional wealth-weighted price formula from ( Sethi and Vaughan 2016 ) as a benchmark, denoted as 𝐩 ˘ = ( p ˘ 1 , … , p ˘ I ) \breve{\mathbf{p}}=(\breve{p}_{1},\ldots,\breve{p}_{I}) , where (28) p ˘ i = θ i ⋅ W 0 + ∑ j = 1 J w j , 0 ​ π i , j W 0 + ∑ j = 1 J w j , 0 , i = 1 , … , I . \displaystyle\breve{p}_{i}=\frac{\theta_{i}\cdot W_{0}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}w_{j,0}\pi_{i,j}}{W_{0}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}w_{j,0}},~~i=1,\ldots,I. We follow a simulation procedure similar to that in Table 2 , using a fixed J = 50 J=50 and γ m = − 0.5 \gamma_{m}=-0.5 . To highlight the impact of traders’ risk parameter γ \gamma , we vary its values and compare the resulting limiting prices obtained from the simulation with two heuristic price formulas: ( 24 ) and ( 28 ). Figure 2 . Comparison of two heuristics for different risk parameters (a) Price p 1 ∗ p_{1}^{*} and γ \gamma (b) KLD and γ \gamma In Figure 2(a) , the red curve represents the average price of the first security resulting from N = 250 N=250 rounds of simulations, which exhibits an increasing trend as γ \gamma increases. Importantly, our POI price formula ( 24 ) effectively captures this trend, as indicated by the solid blue curve. In contrast, the conventional wealth-weighted formula ( 28 ) fails to exhibit such a distinct pattern as it does not account for the risk parameter. Figure 2(b) illustrates the approximation errors of the two heuristic prices where 𝒟 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ¯ sim , 𝐩 ^ ) \mathcal{D}(\bar{\mathbf{p}}^{\textrm{sim}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}) and 𝒟 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ¯ sim , 𝐩 ˘ ) \mathcal{D}(\bar{\mathbf{p}}^{\textrm{sim}},\breve{\mathbf{p}}) measure the KLD between the simulated average price and the two heuristic prices, respectively. Notably, when γ \gamma deviates from 0 0 , especially when it is below zero, our price formula ( 24 ) significantly outperforms the conventional wealth-weighted formula ( 28 ). Figure 3 . Comparison of two heuristics for different initial wealth (a) Price and market maker’s wealth (b) KLD and market maker’s wealth We then investigate the impact of the market maker’s initial wealth W 0 W_{0} , which is a key design variable for providing liquidity. Holding γ m = γ = − 2 \gamma_{m}=\gamma=-2 , we incrementally raise W 0 W_{0} from 5 5 to 100 100 . In Figure 3(a) , the limiting price decreases as W 0 W_{0} increases. This is because the price becomes increasingly biased towards the market maker’s own belief (i.e., θ i = 1 / 3 \theta_{i}=1/3 ) as its wealth grows. Both formulas ( 24 ) and ( 28 ) display a similar trend. However, our price formula ( 24 ) closely tracks the limiting price, while the conventional one ( 28 ) tends to overestimate it significantly. Figure 3(b) provides a more detailed comparison of these approximation errors by evaluating the KLD. It becomes evident that our POI price formula ( 24 ) not only demonstrates greater accuracy but also exhibits much higher stability than formula ( 28 ) when varying the market maker’s wealth W 0 W_{0} . This characteristic highlights that the quality of our heuristic price formula ( 24 ) is not sensitive to fluctuations in the market maker’s wealth level. Such a property may benefit the market maker in customizing the market-making mechanism. In summary, the combination of the POI weights ( 23 ) with Eq. ( 21 ) offers an accurate estimation of the limiting price across various market scenarios. Although such a pricing formula is not entirely analytical, the parameters ξ i ∗ \xi_{i}^{*} , given in Eqs. ( 21 ) provide comprehensive information on how the price is aggregated based on participants’ beliefs, risk attitudes, and wealth levels. 6. Price-Volume Relationship and Nonstationary Market In this section, we provide analysis of the price-volume relationship resulted from our model and discuss the market behavior in a non-stationary market. The price-volume relationship: Like microstructure analysis in the financial market, studying the relationship between the price and trading volume reveals how prices evolve during trading. To examine this, we consider the t t -th round of trading where the j j -th trader interacts with the market maker in Trading Process 1 . Although it might seem like only one trader is trading at a time, we can think of this as a group of traders. For instance, the j j -th trader represents a group of traders holding the similar beliefs with the wealth being the aggregate wealth. Therefore, the order size in each round of trading represents an aggregate order for a group of traders. For simplicity, we consider a market with binary securities ( I = 2 I=2 ). The j j -th trader solves problem 𝒫 j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) \mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}) to determine the optimal decision 𝐳 ∗ = ( z 1 ∗ , z 2 ∗ ) \mathbf{z}^{*}=(z_{1}^{*},z_{2}^{*}) . Since the two securities are mutually exclusive, the trading volume is only related to the net demand D t ≜ Δ ​ q 1 ∗ − Δ ​ q 2 ∗ = z 1 ∗ − z 2 ∗ \textrm{D}_{t}\triangleq\Delta q_{1}^{*}-\Delta q_{2}^{*}=z^{*}_{1}-z^{*}_{2} where q i ∗ q_{i}^{*} is the amount of the order. 16 16 16 The last equality is from the definition of z ∗ z^{*} . The mutual exclusiveness also implies the prices 𝐩 t = ( p 1 , t , p 2 , t ) \mathbf{p}_{t}=(p_{1,t},p_{2,t}) satisfies p 1 , t = 1 − p 2 , t p_{1,t}=1-p_{2,t} . We then examine the relationship between the net demand D t \text{D}_{t} and the security’s price p 1 , t p_{1,t} for different market settings. As the exponential MU-function-based market admits a closed-form solution for trading problem 𝒫 j ​ ( 𝐱 j ) \mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j}) , it is an ideal vehicle to study the price-volume relationship. Using the expression ( 11 ) in Proposition 4.1 , we can compute the net demand as, (29) D t = z 1 ∗ − z 2 ∗ \displaystyle\textrm{D}_{t}=z^{*}_{1}-z^{*}_{2} = 1 α j + β ​ ln ⁡ ( π ^ 1 , j , t ​ ( 1 − p 1 , t ) ( 1 − π ^ 1 , j , t ) ​ p 1 , t ) = 1 α j + β ​ ( ln ⁡ ( π ^ 1 , j , t 1 − π ^ 1 , j , t ) − ln ⁡ ( p 1 , t 1 − p 1 , t ) ) , \displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha_{j}+\beta}\ln\left(\frac{\hat{\pi}_{1,j,t}(1-p_{1,t})}{(1-\hat{\pi}_{1,j,t})p_{1,t}}\right)=\frac{1}{\alpha_{j}+\beta}\left(\ln\left(\frac{\hat{\pi}_{1,j,t}}{1-\hat{\pi}_{1,j,t}}\right)-\ln\left(\frac{p_{1,t}}{1-p_{1,t}}\right)\right), where π ^ i , j , t \hat{\pi}_{i,j,t} is defined in Proposition 4.1 . We observe that the volume D t \textrm{D}_{t} is proportional to the difference between the log odds of the j j -th trader’s belief and the security price. The demand D t \text{D}_{t} decreases as the price p 1 , t p_{1,t} increases, indicating a higher demand for the security at a lower price. Conversely, for a fixed price p 1 , t p_{1,t} , the demand D t \text{D}_{t} depends on the trader’s utility-adjusted belief π ^ 1 , j , t \hat{\pi}_{1,j,t} . A trader trades more security if π ^ 1 , j , t \hat{\pi}_{1,j,t} deviates further from the price. It is also evident that the net trade volume is discounted by a factor 1 α j + β \frac{1}{\alpha_{j}+\beta} . That is to say, even if two traders have identical subjective beliefs, the trader who is more risk-averse trades less security than the one with less risk aversion. This finding further demonstrates the importance of including the risk parameter in our approximated pricing formula ( 25 ). The above price-volume relationship also holds for the CRRA utility-based market. We slightly modify the market in Example 5.2 by aggregating the first two securities as a single security, which gives a binary market. That is, the two traders’ beliefs become π 1 = ( 0.4 , 0.6 ) \pi_{1}=(0.4,0.6) and π 2 = ( 0.7 , 0.3 ) \pi_{2}=(0.7,0.3) . Figure 4 plots trader 2’s net demand as a function of p 1 , t p_{1,t} for diffferent paramters, i.e., γ m , γ ∈ { 0.5 , − 0.5 } \gamma_{m},\gamma\in\{0.5,-0.5\} and W 0 ∈ { 5 , 10 } W_{0}\in\{5,10\} . Clearly, we can observe the pattern that the net demand is negatively correlated with the price and the risk aversion parameter. In addition, this figure also indicates that when the market maker has larger wealth, it may provide higher trading volume (higher liquidity). Figure 4 . Demand as function of price for CRRA utility Nonstationary market: All the previous analysis is based on the stationary assumption, i.e., we assume that no new information comes to the market during trading. However, in reality, most prediction markets run for several months, and when an event shocks the market, traders’ beliefs are significantly changed. To show how new information affects security prices, we consider two situations: the shock happens at an early stage (before price convergence), and the shock happens at a late stage. We adopt Example 5.2 for illustration. The shock changes the traders’ beliefs on the second security, i.e., the original beliefs, π 1 = ( 0.2 , 0.2 , 06 ) \pi_{1}=(0.2,0.2,06) and π 2 = ( 0.6 , 0.1 , 0.3 ) \pi_{2}=(0.6,0.1,0.3) are modified to π ^ 1 = normalize ​ { ( 0.2 , 0.2 , 0.6 ) + ( 0 , 1 , 0 ) } = ( 0.1 , 0.6 , 0.3 ) \hat{\pi}_{1}=\text{normalize}\{(0.2,0.2,0.6)+(0,1,0)\}=(0.1,0.6,0.3) and π ^ 2 = normalize ​ { ( 0.6 , 0.1 , 0.3 ) + ( 0 , 1 , 0 ) } = ( 0.3 , 0.55 , 0.15 ) \hat{\pi}_{2}=\text{normalize}\{(0.6,0.1,0.3)+(0,1,0)\}=(0.3,0.55,0.15) , respectively. Figure 5(a) displays the trajectories of two traders’ utilities. The solid line and dash-dot line indicate the Pareto optimal frontiers of utility values generated by the original and modified beliefs, respectively. We can observe that, regardless of when the shock occurs, the utilities ultimately converge to the new Pareto-optimal set (i.e., the solid curve and the dashed curve indicate the Pareto-optimal sets for the market with and without the shock, respectively). However, the position of the limiting points varies. Figure 5(b) illustrates the trajectory of the first security’s price, p 1 , t p_{1,t} . Similar to the utility values, when new information enters the market, the price p 1 , t p_{1,t} responds rapidly to this new information. It is important to note that the limiting prices resulting from early and late shocks are different. This is a straightforward consequence of Theorem 5.3 , which indicates that all traders’ initial conditions and beliefs determine the limiting price. If we consider the time at which the shock occurs as the new starting point, early and late shocks produce different initial conditions, thus potentially leading to different limiting prices. Figure 5 . Utility and price trajectories after shock (a) Utility trajectories (b) Price trajectories 7. Conclusion This paper studies the prediction market convergence properties through the utilization of the multivariate utility (MU)-based market-making mechanism. This mechanism not only consolidates various existing market-making methods under specific conditions but also establishes a framework for examining the dynamic trading process within a market featuring a single market maker and a finite number of traders. Within this framework, we address one fundamental question arising in the prediction market: the formation of the limiting price (final price) by all market participants. We establish, under mild conditions, that traders’ wealth processes converge to a limiting wealth distribution, resulting in Pareto optimal utility levels for all participants. This outcome enables us to explore the limiting price across different market types. In exponential utility markets, we present an explicit formula for the limiting price. For convex risk-measure-based markets, we introduce a method to design MU functions via penalty functions and demonstrate that the limiting price is uniquely determined as a weighted power mean of trader beliefs. Regarding CRRA utility-based markets, we characterize the limiting price through a system of equations. Additionally, we present a theoretical result indicating that the impact of trading sequence variation becomes negligible as the trader population increases. This novel finding enables us to formulate an approximate price formula, suggesting that the limiting price can be construed as a wealth-weighted risk-adjusted average of all participants’ beliefs. Numerical experiments validate the high accuracy and robustness of this approximation. There are several promising directions for future research. Firstly, although this work has derived price formulas under various theoretical settings, a critical next step is the empirical validation of these results using real market data. Secondly, our model is built on the stationary assumption that participants’ beliefs remain constant. In real-world trading, traders and market maker typically adaptively revise their beliefs based on new information. While our preliminary analysis in Section 6 indicates that changes in traders’ beliefs can result in significant shifts in the limiting price, it remains unknown how traders and market maker should incorporate this new information into their decision-making process as part of a learning process. A recent study by ( Birge et al. 2021 ) analyzes the profit maximization problem for market maker in the spread betting market, where market maker may learn the distribution of event outcomes during trading. To address the potential profit loss that Bayesian policies may suffer in the presence of strategic bettors, they propose a new policy that balances learning with bluff-proofing. Building on this line of research, our model has the potential to incorporate learning features for both traders and market maker. Thirdly, in our model, we assume that traders’ decision problems are myopic, meaning that they focus on single-period decision problems. However, in the actual market, some traders exhibit forward-looking behavior. For example, some professional traders may possess significant insights into future market movements. Therefore, it is worthwhile to explore a model that incorporates traders who tackle forward-looking decision problems involving multi-period considerations. In such a scenario, the decision horizon spans multiple periods, which introduces more price uncertainty resulting from the trading activities of other market participants. Establishing a tractable multi-period decision problem for traders is a crucial step in exploring this forward-looking aspect in the market. Lastly, it is well-known that classical risk-averse preferences cannot fully explain certain decision-making behaviors observed in empirical studies and psychological experiments ( Tversky and Kahneman 1992 ) . Prospect theory (PT) is an influential alternative theory that accounts for psychological factors such as loss aversion, reference points, and probability distortion in individual decision-making ( ( Kahneman and Tversky 2013 ; Barberis 2013 ) ). Recently, ( Yu et al. 2022 ) adopt a PT-based utility and construct an equilibrium model to explain pricing anomalies in betting markets. In the context of pricing models, ( den Boer and Keskin 2022 ) incorporate the PT-based demand function into a dynamic pricing model with demand learning and propose an asymptotically optimal dynamic pricing policy. Incorporating PT-based traders into the current analysis could provide a more realistic representation of real-world trading. Furthermore, it would be intriguing to explore how such traders influence the limiting price. However, establishing market convergence could be challenging due to the non-convex nature of the PT-based model. To address this challenge, several key assumptions made in this work may require refinement. References Abbott [2001] Stephen Abbott. Understanding Analysis , volume 2. Springer, 2001. On the other hand, Assumption 2 implies that h V ​ ( w ) h_{V}(w) is a strictly decreasing function of w w . Based on these properties, when Δ ​ 𝐪 \Delta\mathbf{q} is fixed, replacing the objective function “ min w ⁡ w \min_{w}~w ” by “ max w ⁡ h V ​ ( w ) \max_{w}~h_{V}(w) ” in the lower-level problem defined in constraint ( 3 ) does not change its optimal solution for Δ ​ w \Delta w . After the replacement, the subproblem can be viewed as a partial optimization of the master problem for a given Δ ​ 𝐪 \Delta\mathbf{q} , therefore, it can be viewed as the following problem which optimizes Δ ​ w \Delta w and Δ ​ 𝐪 \Delta\mathbf{q} simultaneously, max Δ ​ w ∈ ℝ , Δ ​ 𝐪 ∈ ℝ I \displaystyle\max_{\Delta w\in\mathbb{R},\Delta\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{I}}~ V j ​ ( ( w j , t − Δ ​ w ) ⋅ 𝐞 + ( 𝐪 j , t + Δ ​ 𝐪 ) ) \displaystyle~V_{j}((w_{j,t}-\Delta w)\cdot\mathbf{e}+(\mathbf{q}_{j,t}+\Delta\mathbf{q})) subject to U ⁡ ( ( W t + Δ ​ w ) ⋅ 𝐞 − ( 𝐐 t + Δ ​ 𝐪 ) ) ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ​ 𝐞 ) , \displaystyle~U\big((W_{t}+\Delta w)\cdot\mathbf{e}-(\mathbf{Q}_{t}+\Delta\mathbf{q})\big)\geq U(W_{0}\mathbf{e}), ( W t + Δ ​ w ) ⋅ 𝐞 − ( 𝐐 t + Δ ​ 𝐪 ) ∈ dom ​ ( U ) , \displaystyle~(W_{t}+\Delta w)\cdot\mathbf{e}-(\mathbf{Q}_{t}+\Delta\mathbf{q})\in\textbf{{dom}}(U), ( w j , t − Δ ​ w ) ⋅ 𝐞 + ( 𝐪 j , t + Δ ​ 𝐪 ) ∈ dom ​ ( V j ) . \displaystyle~(w_{j,t}-\Delta w)\cdot\mathbf{e}+(\mathbf{q}_{j,t}+\Delta\mathbf{q})\in\textbf{{dom}}(V_{j}). Letting 𝐱 j , t = w j , t ⋅ 𝐞 + 𝐪 j , t \mathbf{x}_{j,t}=w_{j,t}\cdot\mathbf{e}+\mathbf{q}_{j,t} , 𝐲 t = W t ⋅ 𝐞 − 𝐐 t \mathbf{y}_{t}=W_{t}\cdot\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{Q}_{t} and 𝐳 = Δ ​ 𝐪 − Δ ​ w ⋅ 𝐞 \mathbf{z}=\Delta\mathbf{q}-\Delta w\cdot\mathbf{e} in above problem gives problem 𝒫 j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) \mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}) . The uniqueness of the solution follows from the strict concavity of V j ​ ( ⋅ ) V_{j}(\cdot) . □ \square ∙ \bullet Proof of Proposition 2.3 : Proof. Thus, the proof for this condition is similar to condition (i). □ \square A.2. Proof of Theorem 3.2 Before we prove Theorem 3.2 , we need the following result. Lemma A.1. Under Assumptions 1 and 2 , the feasible market states set 𝒜 \mathcal{A} is a compact set. Proof. The set 𝒜 \mathcal{A} is closed as it results from the intersection of closed sets, and it’s convex due to the convex nature of U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) and V j ​ ( ⋅ ) V_{j}(\cdot) . To prove the compactness, we only need to demonstrate that 𝒜 \mathcal{A} is bounded. Suppose 𝒜 \mathcal{A} is unbounded, which implies the existence of a recession direction ( 𝐝 1 , 𝐝 2 , … , 𝐝 J ) (\mathbf{d}_{1},\mathbf{d}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{d}_{J}) such that 𝐝 j ′ ≠ 0 \mathbf{d}_{j^{\prime}}\neq 0 for some j ′ j^{\prime} . This means that for any ( 𝐱 ^ 1 , … , 𝐱 ^ J ) ∈ 𝒜 (\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{J})\in\mathcal{A} and any large number β &gt; 0 \beta&gt;0 , it follows that ( 𝐱 ^ 1 + β ⋅ 𝐝 1 , … , 𝐱 ^ J + β ⋅ 𝐝 J ) ∈ 𝒜 (\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1}+\beta\cdot\mathbf{d}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{J}+\beta\cdot\mathbf{d}_{J})\in\mathcal{A} . However, due to Assumption 2 , it has recc ⁡ ( V j ) ⊆ ℝ + I \mathrm{recc}(V_{j})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{I}_{+} , for all j j , which implies 𝐝 j ≥ 𝟎 \mathbf{d}_{j}\geq\mathbf{0} for j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J . Let 𝐱 ^ j = w j , 0 ​ 𝐞 \hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}=w_{j,0}\mathbf{e} . Assumption 1 for U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) implies that, for some β &gt; 0 \beta&gt;0 , it has U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 − β ⋅ ∑ j = 1 J 𝐝 j ) &lt; U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}-\beta\cdot\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{d}_{j})&lt;U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}) , which contradicts with the assumption ( 𝐱 ^ 1 + β ⋅ 𝐝 1 , … , 𝐱 ^ J + β ⋅ 𝐝 J ) ∈ 𝒜 (\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1}+\beta\cdot\mathbf{d}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{J}+\beta\cdot\mathbf{d}_{J})\in\mathcal{A} for all β &gt; 0 \beta&gt;0 . Thus, the set 𝒜 \mathcal{A} is bounded. Note that, if the no-bankruptcy constraint 𝐱 j ≥ 𝟎 \mathbf{x}_{j}\geq\mathbf{0} is involved, the compactness of the set 𝒜 \mathcal{A} is straightforward result. We then proceed to prove Theorem 3.2 through three steps: (I) Firstly, we prove the existence of a sub-sequence within the utility values generated by the trading sequence that converges to a specific limiting utility level. (II) We then establish that the limiting allocation corresponding to such a convergent subsequence achieves Pareto optimality. (III) Finally, we show that any convergent subsequence derived from the original bounded sequence converges to the same limit. To ensure this proof fit more general situation, we consider the no-bankruptcy constraints, 𝐱 j ≥ 0 \mathbf{x}_{j}\geq 0 for all j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J , in the Trading Process 1 . In other words, we solve problem 𝒫 + ​ j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) \mathcal{P}^{+}j(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}) in each round of trading. It is worth noting that this proof can be readily adapted for cases without the no-bankruptcy constraint. The detailed proof unfolds as follows. (I) Given a trading sequence 𝒮 ∈ Φ \mathcal{S}\in\Phi , the wealth position ( 𝐱 1 , t , … , 𝐱 J , t ) (\mathbf{x}_{1,t},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J,t}) for t = 1 , 2 , … t=1,2,\ldots generated from the Trading Process 1 is confined in the compact feasible set 𝒜 \mathcal{A} (see Lemma A.1 ). From Bolzano-Weierstrass theorem, we know there exists a subsequence ( 𝐱 1 , t n CLOSE (\mathbf{x}_{1,t_{n}} , … \ldots , OPEN 𝐱 J , t n ) \mathbf{x}_{J,t_{n}}) for some { t 1 , … , t n , … } \{t_{1},\ldots,t_{n},\ldots\} ⊆ \subseteq { 1 , 2 , 3 ​ … } \{1,2,3...\} that converges to a limiting wealth distribution ( 𝐱 ^ 1 CLOSE (\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1} , … \ldots , OPEN 𝐱 ^ J ) \hat{\mathbf{x}}_{J}) , i.e., it has lim n → ∞ 𝐱 j , t n = 𝐱 ^ j \lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{x}_{j,t_{n}}=\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j} for j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J . (II) We then show that the limiting allocation ( 𝐱 ^ 1 , … , 𝐱 ^ J ) (\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{J}) is a Pareto optimal allocation. As there are two conditions to guarantee the convergence, we treat them seqerately. We first consider the case (i) (i.e., the case dom ​ ( U ) = ℝ I \textbf{{dom}}(U)=\mathbb{R}^{I} ). The argument for case (ii) is identical to case (i), once we show that 𝐲 t \mathbf{y}_{t} will be bounded away from the domain boundary for all t t . Given limiting allocation ( 𝐱 ^ 1 CLOSE (\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1} , … \ldots , OPEN 𝐱 ^ J ) \hat{\mathbf{x}}_{J}) , the market maker’s limiting wealth position is 𝐲 ^ = w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 − ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 ^ j \hat{\mathbf{y}}={w}_{0}^{\textrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{e}-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j} . We first prove the following claim. For any j ∈ { 1 , … , J } j\in\{1,\ldots,J\} , given j j -th trader’s wealth 𝐱 ^ j = ( x ^ 1 , j , … , x ^ I , j ) \hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}=(\hat{x}_{1,j},\ldots,\hat{x}_{I,j}) , there does not exist vector 𝐝 ^ = ( d ^ 1 , … , d ^ I ) ∈ ℝ I \hat{\mathbf{d}}=(\hat{d}_{1},\ldots,\hat{d}_{I})\in\mathbb{R}^{I} which satisfies the following system of inequalities, (33) { ∇ V j ( 𝐱 ^ j ) ⊤ 𝐝 ^ &gt; 0 , ∇ U ( 𝐲 ^ ) ⊤ 𝐝 ^ ≤ 0 , 𝐇 ^ j ​ 𝐝 ^ ≥ 𝟎 , \displaystyle\begin{cases}~\nabla V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j})^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{d}}&gt;0,\\ ~\nabla U(\hat{\mathbf{y}})^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{d}}\leq 0,\\ ~\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{j}\hat{\mathbf{d}}\geq\mathbf{0},\\ \end{cases} where ∇ V j ​ ( 𝐱 ^ j ) \nabla V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}) = = ( ∂ V j ​ ( 𝐱 ) ∂ x 1 , … , ∂ V j ​ ( 𝐱 ) ∂ x I ) | 𝐱 = 𝐱 ^ j \left(\frac{\partial V_{j}(\mathbf{x})}{\partial x_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\partial V_{j}(\mathbf{x})}{\partial x_{I}}\right)\big|_{\mathbf{x}=\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}} , ∇ U ​ ( 𝐲 ^ ) \nabla U(\hat{\mathbf{y}}) = = ( ∂ U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) ∂ y 1 , … , ∂ U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) ∂ y I ) | 𝐲 = 𝐲 ^ \left(\frac{\partial U(\mathbf{y})}{\partial y_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\partial U(\mathbf{y})}{\partial y_{I}}\right)\big|_{\mathbf{y}=\hat{\mathbf{y}}} , and 𝐇 ^ j = diag ​ ( H ^ 1 , j , … , H ^ i , j , … , H ^ I , j ) ∈ ℝ I × I \displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{j}=\textrm{diag}\big(\hat{H}_{1,j},\ldots,\hat{H}_{i,j},\ldots,\hat{H}_{I,j}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{I\times I} is a diagonal matrix with diagonal elements being H ^ i , j = 0 \hat{H}_{i,j}=0 if x ^ i , j &gt; 0 \hat{x}_{i,j}&gt;0 and H ^ i , j = 1 \hat{H}_{i,j}=1 if x ^ i , j = 0 \hat{x}_{i,j}=0 . We then prove the following claim. If there exists 𝐝 ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} which satisfies ( 33 ) for some j ∈ { 1 , … , J } j\in\{1,\ldots,J\} , then 𝐝 \mathbf{d} must satisfy the following condition, (34) { ∇ V j ( 𝐱 ^ j ) ⊤ 𝐝 &gt; 0 , ∇ U ( 𝐲 ^ ) ⊤ 𝐝 &lt; 0 , 𝐇 ^ j ​ 𝐝 ≥ 𝟎 , d i &gt; 0 , for all i such that H ^ i , j = 1 . \displaystyle\begin{cases}~\nabla V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j})^{\top}\mathbf{d}&gt;0,\\ ~\nabla U(\hat{\mathbf{y}})^{\top}\mathbf{d}&lt;0,\\ ~\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{j}\mathbf{d}\geq\mathbf{0},\\ ~d_{i}&gt;0,\quad\mbox{for all }i\mbox{ such that }\hat{H}_{i,j}=1.\\ \end{cases} First, we require the following result: for any 𝐲 ∈ dom ​ ( U ) \mathbf{y}\in\textbf{{dom}}(U) , it holds that ∂ U ∂ y i &gt; 0 \frac{\partial U}{\partial y_{i}}&gt;0 for all i i . To establish this fact, consider an arbitrary 𝐲 ∈ dom ​ ( U ) \mathbf{y}\in\textbf{{dom}}(U) . We define a function f ⁡ ( ⋅ ) : ℝ → ℝ f(\cdot):\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} as f ⁡ ( t , 𝐲 , 𝐞 i ) ≜ U ⁡ ( 𝐲 + t ​ 𝐞 i ) f(t;\mathbf{y},\mathbf{e}_{i})\triangleq U(\mathbf{y}+t\mathbf{e}_{i}) . Since U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) is concave and monotonic, f ⁡ ( t , 𝐲 , 𝐞 i ) f(t;\mathbf{y},\mathbf{e}_{i}) is also concave and strictly increasing with respect to t t . Therefore, we have f ′ ​ ( t , 𝐲 , 𝐞 i ) &gt; 0 f^{\prime}(t;\mathbf{y},\mathbf{e}_{i})&gt;0 for all t ∈ ℝ t\in\mathbb{R} , and thus ∂ U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) ∂ y i = f ′ ​ ( 0 , 𝐲 , 𝐞 i ) &gt; 0 \frac{\partial U(\mathbf{y})}{\partial y_{i}}=f^{\prime}(0;\mathbf{y},\mathbf{e}_{i})&gt;0 . This establishes the monotonicity result, ∂ U ∂ y i &gt; 0 \frac{\partial U}{\partial y_{i}}&gt;0 for all i i , as the choice of 𝐲 ∈ dom ​ ( U ) \mathbf{y}\in\textbf{{dom}}(U) is arbitrary. We proceed to establish the claim presented in ( 34 ), which leads to three cases: (a) ∇ U ( 𝐲 ^ ) ⊤ 𝐝 ^ = 0 \nabla U(\hat{\mathbf{y}})^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{d}}=0 and H ^ i , j ​ d i &gt; 0 \hat{H}_{i,j}d_{i}&gt;0 for some i i , (b) ∇ U ( 𝐲 ^ ) ⊤ 𝐝 ^ &gt; 0 \nabla U(\hat{\mathbf{y}})^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{d}}&gt;0 and 𝐇 ^ j ​ 𝐝 ^ = 0 \hat{\mathbf{H}}_{j}\hat{\mathbf{d}}=0 , and (c) ∇ U ( 𝐲 ^ ) ⊤ 𝐝 ^ = 0 \nabla U(\hat{\mathbf{y}})^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{d}}=0 and 𝐇 ^ j ​ 𝐝 ^ = 0 \hat{\mathbf{H}}_{j}\hat{\mathbf{d}}=0 . The first two cases can be easily addressed using the fact that ∂ U ∂ y i &gt; 0 \frac{\partial U}{\partial y_{i}}&gt;0 for i i . We will focus on the case where ∇ U ( 𝐲 ^ ) ⊤ 𝐝 ^ = 0 \nabla U(\hat{\mathbf{y}})^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{d}}=0 and 𝐇 ^ j ​ 𝐝 ^ = 𝟎 \hat{\mathbf{H}}_{j}\hat{\mathbf{d}}=\mathbf{0} . In this scenario, we can construct a vector 𝐝 = 𝐝 ^ + ϵ \mathbf{d}=\hat{\mathbf{d}}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon} , where ϵ = l ⋅ [ ( 𝐇 ^ j − 𝐈 ) + k ⋅ 𝐇 ^ j ] ⋅ 𝐞 \boldsymbol{\epsilon}=l\cdot\big[(\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{j}-\mathbf{I})+k\cdot\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{j}\big]\cdot\mathbf{e} for some small positive numbers k k and l l . Clearly, 𝐝 \mathbf{d} satisfies the condition ( 34 ). Given that 𝐲 ^ \hat{\mathbf{y}} lies within the interior of dom ​ ( U ) \textbf{{dom}}(U) , we can utilize the vector 𝐝 \mathbf{d} to enhance the expected utility for trader j j in Trading Process 1 while maintaining feasibility. Specifically, for some δ &gt; 0 \delta&gt;0 and small number α \alpha , it has { V j ( 𝐱 ^ j + α 𝐝 ) − V j ( 𝐱 ^ j ) = α ∇ V j ( 𝐱 ^ j ) ⊤ 𝐝 + o ( α ) &gt; 2 δ , U ( 𝐲 ^ − α 𝐝 ) − U ( 𝐲 ^ ) = − α ∇ U ( 𝐲 ^ ) ⊤ 𝐝 + o ( α ) &gt; 0 , 𝐱 ^ j + α 𝐝 &gt; 𝟎 , 𝐱 ^ j + α 𝐝 ∈ dom ( V j ) , 𝐲 ^ − α ​ 𝐝 ∈ dom ​ ( U ) , \displaystyle\begin{cases}~V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}+\alpha\mathbf{d})-V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j})=\alpha\nabla V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j})^{\top}\mathbf{d}+o(\alpha)&gt;2\delta,\\ ~U(\hat{\mathbf{y}}-\alpha\mathbf{d})-U(\hat{\mathbf{y}})=-\alpha\nabla U(\hat{\mathbf{y}})^{\top}\mathbf{d}+o(\alpha)&gt;0,\\ ~\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}+\alpha\mathbf{d}&gt;\mathbf{0},~\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}+\alpha\mathbf{d}\in\textbf{{dom}}(V_{j}),\\ ~\hat{\mathbf{y}}-\alpha\mathbf{d}\in\textbf{{dom}}(U),\end{cases} where the above inequalities can be derived from ( 34 ) and by noting that the linear term dominates the higher-order term in the Taylor expansion when α \alpha is sufficiently small. Next, we demonstrate that α ​ 𝐝 \alpha\mathbf{d} is a feasible direction that can drive the utility of trader j j to surpass their limit V j ​ ( 𝐱 ^ ​ j ) V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}j) at some time t N t_{N} . Indeed, there exists some positive integer N N , such that the wealth vector 𝐱 ​ j , t N \mathbf{x}{j,t_{N}} is sufficiently close to 𝐱 ^ j \hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j} . Then, we have: { V j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t N + α ​ 𝐝 ) − V j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t N ) &gt; δ , U ⁡ ( 𝐲 t N − α ​ 𝐝 ) − U ⁡ ( 𝐲 t N ) &gt; 0 , 𝐱 j , t N + α 𝐝 &gt; 𝟎 , 𝐱 j , t N + α 𝐝 ∈ dom ( V j ) , 𝐲 t N − α ​ 𝐝 ∈ dom ​ ( U ) , V j ​ ( 𝐱 ^ j ) − V j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t N ) &lt; δ / 2 , \displaystyle\begin{cases}~V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t_{N}}+\alpha\mathbf{d})-V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t_{N}})&gt;\delta,\\ ~U(\mathbf{y}_{t_{N}}-\alpha\mathbf{d})-U(\mathbf{y}_{t_{N}})&gt;0,\\ ~\mathbf{x}_{j,t_{N}}+\alpha\mathbf{d}&gt;\mathbf{0},~{\mathbf{x}}_{j,t_{N}}+\alpha\mathbf{d}\in\textbf{{dom}}(V_{j}),\\ ~\mathbf{y}_{t_{N}}-\alpha\mathbf{d}\in\textbf{{dom}}(U),\\ ~V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j})-V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t_{N}})&lt;\delta/2,\end{cases} where the first two inequalities are a result of the continuity of V j ​ ( ⋅ ) V_{j}(\cdot) and U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) , and the last inequality arises from the convergence property: lim n → V j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t n ) → V j ​ ( 𝐱 ^ j ) \lim_{n\rightarrow}V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t_{n}})\rightarrow V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}) . TTherefore, α ​ 𝐝 \alpha\mathbf{d} represents a feasible trade for trader j j at time t N t_{N} , causing the post-trade utility to exceed their limit, as demonstrated below: V j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t N + α ​ 𝐝 ) − V j ​ ( 𝐱 ^ j ) = V j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t N + α ​ 𝐝 ) − V j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t N ) + V j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t N ) − V j ​ ( 𝐱 ^ j ) &gt; δ − δ / 2 = δ / 2 &gt; 0 . \displaystyle V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t_{N}}+\alpha\mathbf{d})-V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j})=V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t_{N}}+\alpha\mathbf{d})-V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t_{N}})+V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t_{N}})-V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j})&gt;\delta-\delta/2=\delta/2&gt;0. This inequality contradicts the fact that V j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}) should be upper-bounded by the limit V j ​ ( 𝐱 ^ j ) V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}) for all t t . We then show that if the condition (ii) in Theorem 3.2 is met, then the subsequential limit of market maker wealth 𝐲 ^ \hat{\mathbf{y}} is bounded away from the domain boundary. Indeed, assume 𝐲 ^ \hat{\mathbf{y}} lies on the boundary of dom ​ ( U ) \textbf{{dom}}(U) , then ∂ U ∂ y i | 𝐲 ^ = ∞ \frac{\partial U}{\partial y_{i}}\big|_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}=\infty for some i i while ∂ U ∂ y i ′ | 𝐲 ^ &lt; + ∞ \frac{\partial U}{\partial y_{i^{\prime}}}\big|_{\hat{\mathbf{y}}}&lt;+\infty , y i ′ &gt; 0 y_{i^{\prime}}&gt;0 for some i ′ ≠ i i^{\prime}\neq i . Otherwise, 𝐲 ^ = 𝐋 ⟹ U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ^ ) &lt; U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) \hat{\mathbf{y}}=\mathbf{L}\implies U(\hat{\mathbf{y}})&lt;U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}) , which is a contradiction. Since y ^ i = L i &lt; W 0 \hat{y}_{i}=L_{i}&lt;W_{0} and 𝐲 ^ + ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 ^ j = ( W 0 + ∑ j = 1 J w j , 0 ) ⋅ 𝐞 \hat{\mathbf{y}}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}=\big(W_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}w_{j,0}\big)\cdot\mathbf{e} it has x ^ i , j &gt; w j , 0 &gt; 0 \hat{x}_{i,j}&gt;w_{j,0}&gt;0 for at least one j j . In other words, x ^ i , j \hat{x}_{i,j} is bounded away from trader’s utility boundary. Let 𝐝 = M ⋅ 𝐞 i ′ − 𝐞 i \mathbf{d}=M\cdot\mathbf{e}_{i^{\prime}}-\mathbf{e}_{i} , then for trader j j and some M M sufficiently large, it has { ∇ V j ( 𝐱 ^ j ) ⊤ 𝐝 &gt; 0 , ∇ U ( 𝐲 ^ ) ⊤ 𝐝 &lt; 0 . \displaystyle\begin{cases}~\nabla V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j})^{\top}\mathbf{d}&gt;0,\\ ~\nabla U(\hat{\mathbf{y}})^{\top}\mathbf{d}&lt;0.\\ \end{cases} Then, for some δ &gt; 0 \delta&gt;0 and α \alpha which are sufficiently small, it has { V j ( 𝐱 ^ j + α 𝐝 ) − V j ( 𝐱 ^ j ) = α ∇ V j ( 𝐱 ^ j ) ⊤ 𝐝 + o ( α ) &gt; 2 δ , U ( 𝐲 ^ − α 𝐝 ) − U ( 𝐲 ^ ) = − α ∇ U ( 𝐲 ^ ) ⊤ 𝐝 + o ( α ) &gt; 0 , x ^ i , j + α ​ d i &gt; 0 , x ^ i , j + α ​ d i ∈ dom ​ ( V j ) 𝐲 ^ − α ​ 𝐝 ∈ dom ​ ( U ) . \displaystyle\begin{cases}~V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}+\alpha\mathbf{d})-V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j})=\alpha\nabla V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j})^{\top}\mathbf{d}+o(\alpha)&gt;2\delta,\\ ~U(\hat{\mathbf{y}}-\alpha\mathbf{d})-U(\hat{\mathbf{y}})=-\alpha\nabla U(\hat{\mathbf{y}})^{\top}\mathbf{d}+o(\alpha)&gt;0,\\ ~\hat{x}_{i,j}+\alpha d_{i}&gt;0,~\hat{x}_{i,j}+\alpha d_{i}\in\textbf{{dom}}(V_{j})\\ ~\hat{\mathbf{y}}-\alpha\mathbf{d}\in\textbf{{dom}}(U).\end{cases} Since 𝐱 j , t n → 𝐱 ^ j \mathbf{x}_{j,t_{n}}\to\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j} for all j j , we can find a N N sufficiently large such that, { V j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t N + α ​ 𝐝 ) − V j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t N ) &gt; δ , U ⁡ ( 𝐲 t N − α ​ 𝐝 ) − U ⁡ ( 𝐲 t N ) &gt; 0 , x i , j , t N − α d i &gt; 0 , x i , j , t N − α d i ∈ dom ( V j ) , 𝐲 t N − α ​ 𝐝 ∈ dom ​ ( U ) , V j ​ ( 𝐱 ^ j ) − V j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t N ) &lt; δ / 2 . \displaystyle\begin{cases}~V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t_{N}}+\alpha\mathbf{d})-V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t_{N}})&gt;\delta,\\ ~U(\mathbf{y}_{t_{N}}-\alpha\mathbf{d})-U(\mathbf{y}_{t_{N}})&gt;0,\\ ~x_{i,j,t_{N}}-\alpha d_{i}&gt;0,x_{i,j,t_{N}}-\alpha d_{i}\in\textbf{{dom}}(V_{j}),\\ ~\mathbf{y}_{t_{N}}-\alpha\mathbf{d}\in\textbf{{dom}}(U),\\ ~V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j})-V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t_{N}})&lt;\delta/2.\end{cases} Again, we achieve a non-trivial utility gain for trader j j using feasible trade α ​ 𝐝 \alpha\mathbf{d} . As a summary at this stage, we’ve demonstrated that the existence of a vector 𝐝 \mathbf{d} satisfying condition ( 33 ) leads to condition ( 34 ). This, in turn, results in an improvement in the utility value for V j ​ ( 𝐱 ^ j ) , V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}), contradicting the fact that 𝐱 ^ j {\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}} is the limiting wealth allocation. Therefore, at this stage, we conclude that condition ( 33 ) must be infeasible. Now we reformulate the infeasibility condition given by ( 33 ) as the alternative of Farkas’ Lemma [ Bertsekas et al. 2003 ] . Let 𝐀 j = ( − ∇ U ​ ( 𝐲 ^ ) , 𝐇 ^ j ) ⊤ \mathbf{A}_{j}=\big(-\nabla U(\hat{\mathbf{y}}),~\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{j}\big)^{\top} . Recall our previous claim which says that there does not exist 𝐝 \mathbf{d} satisfying the following conditions (35) 𝐀 j 𝐝 ≥ 𝟎 , − ∇ V j ( 𝐱 ^ j ) ⊤ 𝐝 &lt; 0 . \displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{j}\mathbf{d}\geq\mathbf{0},~~-\nabla V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j})^{\top}\mathbf{d}&lt;0. Applying the Farkas’ Lemma gives the alternative of the claim, i.e., there exists a vector ( a j 𝐠 j ) ∈ ℝ + 1 + I \left(\begin{array}[]{c}a_{j}\\ \mathbf{g}_{j}\\ \end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{1+I}_{+} which satisfies 𝐀 j ⊤ ( a j 𝐠 j ) = − ∇ V j ( 𝐱 ^ ) ⇒ ∇ V j ( 𝐱 ^ j ) + 𝐇 ^ j 𝐠 j − a j ∇ U ( 𝐲 ∗ ) = 𝟎 \displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{j}^{\top}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}a_{j}\\ \mathbf{g}_{j}\\ \end{array}\right)=-\nabla V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})~\Rightarrow~\nabla V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j})+\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{j}\mathbf{g}_{j}-a_{j}\nabla U(\mathbf{y}^{*})=\mathbf{0} for all j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J . We then consider the aggregate optimization problem, 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ) : \displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}):~ max ⁡ ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 1 , … , 𝐱 J ⁡ ν j ​ V j ​ ( 𝐱 j ) \displaystyle~\max_{\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}}~~\sum_{j=1}^{J}\nu_{j}V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j}) Subject to: 𝐲 + ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j = w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 , \displaystyle~\mathbf{y}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j}={w}_{0}^{\textrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{e}, U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) , \displaystyle~U(\mathbf{y})\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}), 𝐱 j ≥ 𝟎 , j = 1 , … , J ( if bankruptcy is prohibitted ) , \displaystyle~\mathbf{x}_{j}\geq\mathbf{0},\quad j=1,\ldots,J~~(\textrm{if bankruptcy is prohibitted}), where 𝝂 = ( ν 1 , … , ν J ) ⊤ ∈ ℝ + I ∖ { 𝟎 } \boldsymbol{\nu}=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{I}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\} is some weighting parameter. Because V j ​ ( ⋅ ) V_{j}(\cdot) ’s are strictly concave, it is well known that a wealth allocation ( 𝐱 1 ∗ , 𝐱 2 ∗ , … , 𝐱 J ∗ ) ∈ 𝒜 (\mathbf{x}^{*}_{1},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{*}_{J})\in\mathcal{A} is Pareto optimal if and only if it is a solution to 𝒫 po \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}} for certain 𝝂 = ( ν 1 , … , ν J ) ⊤ &gt; 0 \boldsymbol{\nu}=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})^{\top}&gt;0 . We now show that allocation ( 𝐱 ^ 1 , ⋯ , 𝐱 ^ J ) (\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\cdots,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{J}) is a solution of problem 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) . Since problem 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) is a convex optimization problem, its optimal solution ( 𝐱 1 ∗ , … , 𝐱 J ∗ ) (\mathbf{x}_{1}^{*},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}^{*}) is completely characterized by the Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) conditions as follow, (38) { ν j ∇ V j ( 𝐱 ∗ j ) + 𝜼 j − μ ∇ U ( 𝐲 ∗ ) = 𝟎 , j = 1 , ⋯ , J , diag ( 𝜼 j ) 𝐱 ∗ j = 𝟎 , j = 1 , 2 , … , J , μ ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ∗ ) − U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) ) = 0 , ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j ∗ + 𝐲 ∗ = 𝐰 0 , \displaystyle\begin{cases}\nu_{j}\nabla V_{j}(\mathbf{x}^{*}_{j})+\boldsymbol{\eta}_{j}-\mu\nabla U(\mathbf{y}^{*})=\mathbf{0},~~~j=1,\cdots,J,\\ \textrm{diag}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{j})\mathbf{x}^{*}_{j}=\mathbf{0},~~j=1,2,\ldots,J,\\ \mu\big(U(\mathbf{y}^{*})-U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e})\big)=0,\\ \sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}^{*}_{j}+\mathbf{y}^{*}=\mathbf{w}_{0},\end{cases} for some μ &gt; 0 \mu&gt;0 and 𝜼 j ∈ ℝ + I \boldsymbol{\eta}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{I} , j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J . Noticing the relationship ( A.2 ) holds for all j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J . Multiplying ( A.2 ) by ν j \nu_{j} , it is not hard to verify the limit allocation ( 𝐱 ^ 1 , … , 𝐱 ^ J ) (\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{J}) together with the following Lagrange multipliers, μ = 1 \mu=1 , ν j = 1 a j \nu_{j}=\frac{1}{a_{j}} and 𝜼 j = 1 a j ​ 𝐇 ^ j ​ 𝐠 j \boldsymbol{\eta}_{j}=\frac{1}{a_{j}}\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{j}\mathbf{g}_{j} for j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J , satisfy the KKT condition ( 38 ). Clearly, it must have a j ≠ 0 {a}_{j}\neq 0 for all j j , otherwise ( A.2 ) cannot hold for ∇ V j ​ ( 𝐱 j ∗ ) &gt; 𝟎 \nabla V_{j}(\mathbf{x}^{*}_{j})&gt;\mathbf{0} and 𝐠 j ≥ 𝟎 \mathbf{g}_{j}\geq\mathbf{0} . Indeed, if a j = 0 a_{j}=0 for some j j , then ∇ V j ​ ( 𝐱 ^ j ) = 𝐇 ^ j ​ 𝐠 j ≤ 𝟎 \nabla V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j})=\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{j}\mathbf{g}_{j}\leq\mathbf{0} , which contradicts with the monotonicity of V j V_{j} . That is to say, ( 𝐱 ^ 1 , … , 𝐱 ^ J ) (\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{J}) is a Pareto optimal allocation. (III) At last, we then show that every convergent subsequence of ( 𝐱 1 , t , … , 𝐱 J , t ) (\mathbf{x}_{1,t},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J,t}) shares an identical limit. Suppose there is another wealth allocation ( 𝐱 ¯ 1 , … , 𝐱 ¯ J ) ∈ 𝒜 (\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{J})\in\mathcal{A} satisfying ( 𝐱 ¯ 1 , … , 𝐱 ¯ J ) ≠ ( 𝐱 ^ 1 , … , 𝐱 ^ J ) (\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{J})\neq(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{J}) and achieving the same utility level as V j ​ ( 𝐱 ¯ j ) = V j ​ ( 𝐱 ^ j ) = V j ∗ V_{j}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{j})=V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j})=V_{j}^{*} for all j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J . Then we can construct another allocation ( 𝐱 ˇ 1 , … , 𝐱 ˇ J ) (\check{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\check{\mathbf{x}}_{J}) as 𝐱 ˇ j = ( 𝐱 ^ j + 𝐱 ¯ j ) / 2 \check{\mathbf{x}}_{j}=(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j}+\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{j})/2 for j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J . Due to the convexity of 𝒜 \mathcal{A} , it has ( 𝐱 ˇ 1 , … , 𝐱 ˇ J ) ∈ 𝒜 (\check{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\check{\mathbf{x}}_{J})\in\mathcal{A} . Moreover, for any j = 1 , ⋯ , J j=1,\cdots,J , it has V j ​ ( 𝐱 ˇ j ) ≥ 1 2 ​ ( V j ​ ( 𝐱 ^ j ) + V j ​ ( 𝐱 ¯ j ) ) = V j ∗ \displaystyle V_{j}(\check{\mathbf{x}}_{j})\geq\frac{1}{2}(V_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{j})+V_{j}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{j}))=V_{j}^{*} with strict inequality holds for at least one j ∈ { 1 , … , J } j\in\{1,\ldots,J\} , due to the strict concavity of V j ​ ( ⋅ ) V_{j}(\cdot) . Clearly this contradicts with the Pareto optimality of ( 𝐱 ^ 1 , … , 𝐱 ^ J ) (\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{J}) . The uniqueness of ( 𝐱 ^ 1 , … , 𝐱 ^ J ) (\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{J}) implies that every convergent subsequence of ( 𝐱 1 , t , … , 𝐱 J , t ) (\mathbf{x}_{1,t},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J,t}) for t = 1 , 2 , … t=1,2,\ldots converges to the identical limiting allocation ( 𝐱 ^ 1 , … , 𝐱 ^ J ) (\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{J}) , which further implies the original bounded sequence ( 𝐱 1 , t , … , 𝐱 J , t ) (\mathbf{x}_{1,t},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J,t}) also converges to the limiting allocation ( 𝐱 ^ 1 , … , 𝐱 ^ J ) (\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{J}) (e.g., see page 58 in Abbott 2001 ). □ \square A.3. Proofs of Main Results in Section 4 Proof of Proposition 4.1 . Proof. Proof. Given the utility function ( 10 ) with the wealth vector 𝐲 t = ( y 1 , t , … , y I , t ) \mathbf{y}_{t}=\big(y_{1,t},\ldots,y_{I,t}\big) , we can explicitly compute the instantaneous price vector 𝐩 t = ( p 1 , t , … , p I , t ) \mathbf{p}_{t}=\big(p_{1,t},\ldots,p_{I,t}\big) using Eq. ( 9 ) as follows: (39) p i , t = θ i ​ e − β ​ y i , t ∑ k = 1 I θ k ​ e − β ​ y k , t . \displaystyle p_{i,t}=\frac{\theta_{i}e^{-\beta y_{i,t}}}{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}\theta_{k}e^{-\beta y_{k,t}}}. for i = 1 , … , I i=1,\ldots,I . We then prove the result (a). The j j -th trader interacts with the market maker at trading time t t and solves the problem 𝒫 j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) \mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}) with the market states 𝐱 j , t \mathbf{x}_{j,t} and 𝐲 t = w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 − ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j , t \mathbf{y}_{t}={w}^{\textrm{all}}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j,t} . Under the assumption of exponential utility in ( 10 ), after eliminating the negative sign in the objective function, problem 𝒫 j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) \mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}) becomes, 𝒫 j ​ ( 𝐱 j , t ) : min 𝐳 \displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j,t}):~~\min_{\mathbf{z}}~ ∑ i = 1 I π ^ i , j ⋅ e − α j ​ z i \displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{I}\hat{\pi}_{i,j}\cdot e^{-\alpha_{j}z_{i}} (40) Subjec to ∑ i = 1 I θ ^ i ⋅ e β ​ z i ≤ e − β ​ W 0 , \displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{I}\hat{\theta}_{i}\cdot e^{\beta z_{i}}\leq e^{-\beta W_{0}}, where π ^ i , j ≜ π i , j e − α j ⋅ x i , j , t \hat{\pi}_{i,j}\triangleq\pi_{i,j}e^{-\alpha_{j}\cdot x_{i,j,t}} and θ ^ i ≜ θ i e − β ⋅ y i , t \hat{\theta}_{i}\triangleq\theta_{i}e^{-\beta\cdot y_{i,t}} . Due to the convexity, the following Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) conditions fully characterize the solution as, (41) − α j π ^ i , j e − α j ​ z i ∗ + ζ ∗ β ⋅ θ ^ i e β ​ z i ∗ = 0 ⇒ z i ∗ = 1 α j + β ln ( α j ​ π ^ i , j ζ ∗ ​ β ​ θ ^ i ) , i = 1 , … , I , \displaystyle-\alpha_{j}\hat{\pi}_{i,j}e^{-\alpha_{j}z^{*}_{i}}+\zeta^{*}\beta\cdot\hat{\theta}_{i}e^{\beta z^{*}_{i}}=0~\Rightarrow~~z_{i}^{*}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{j}+\beta}\ln\left(\frac{\alpha_{j}\hat{\pi}_{i,j}}{\zeta^{*}\beta\hat{\theta}_{i}}\right),~~i=1,\ldots,I, (42) ∑ i = 1 I θ ^ i ​ e β ​ z i ∗ = e − β ​ W 0 , \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{I}\hat{\theta}_{i}e^{\beta z^{*}_{i}}=e^{-\beta W_{0}}, where ζ ∗ &gt; 0 \zeta^{*}&gt;0 is the langrange multiplier. The condition ( 42 ) is from the fact that ζ ∗ ≠ 0 \zeta^{*}\not=0 . Otherwise, the problem admits no solution. It implies that the constraint ( 40 ) always holds equality in each trading round. Thus, before the j j -th trader interacts with market maker, the condition ( 42 ) also holds, i.e., it has (43) ∑ i = 1 I θ ^ i ​ e β ​ z i ∗ = ∑ i = 1 I θ ^ i ​ e β ​ z i | z i = 0 , i = 1 , … , I = ∑ k = 1 I θ ^ k . \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{I}\hat{\theta}_{i}e^{\beta z^{*}_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\hat{\theta}_{i}e^{\beta z_{i}}|_{z_{i}=0,i=1,\ldots,I}=\sum_{k=1}^{I}\hat{\theta}_{k}. In addition, the price formula ( 39 ) implies that p i , t = θ ^ i ∑ k = 1 I θ ^ k p_{i,t}=\frac{\hat{\theta}_{i}}{\sum_{k=1}^{I}\hat{\theta}_{k}} for i = 1 , … , I i=1,\ldots,I . Using this pricing formula and subtituting ( 41 ) into condition ( 43 ) give the lagrange multiplier ζ ∗ = α j β ​ ( ∑ i = 1 I p i , t ​ ( π ^ i , j θ ^ i ) β α j + β ) α j + β β . \displaystyle\zeta^{*}=\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\beta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{I}p_{i,t}\left(\frac{\hat{\pi}_{i,j}}{\hat{\theta}_{i}}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha_{j}+\beta}}\right)^{\frac{\alpha_{j}+\beta}{\beta}}. Combining the above expression with ( 41 ) gives the solution ( 11 ). We then prove the result (b). After the trading, the market state becomes 𝐱 j , t + 1 = 𝐱 j , t + 𝐳 ∗ \mathbf{x}_{j,t+1}=\mathbf{x}_{j,t}+\mathbf{z}^{*} and 𝐲 t + 1 = 𝐲 t − 𝐳 ∗ \mathbf{y}_{t+1}=\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{z}^{*} . We then compute the updated price p i , t + 1 p_{i,t+1} by Proposition 2.3 which gives (44) p i , t + 1 = ∂ U ⁡ ( 𝐲 t + 1 ) ∂ y i , t + 1 ∑ k = 1 I ∂ U ⁡ ( 𝐲 t + 1 ) ∂ y k , t + 1 = θ ^ i ​ e β ​ z i ∗ ∑ k = 1 I θ ^ k ​ e β ​ z k ∗ = θ ^ i α j β + α j ​ ( π ^ i , j ) β β + α j ∑ k = 1 I ( θ ^ k ) α j β + α j ​ ( π ^ k , j ) β β + α j . \displaystyle p_{i,t+1}=\frac{\frac{\partial U(\mathbf{y}_{t+1})}{\partial y_{i,t+1}}}{\sum_{k=1}^{I}\frac{\partial U(\mathbf{y}_{t+1})}{\partial y_{k,t+1}}}=\frac{\hat{\theta}_{i}e^{\beta z_{i}^{*}}}{\sum_{k=1}^{I}\hat{\theta}_{k}e^{\beta z_{k}^{*}}}=\frac{\hat{\theta}_{i}^{\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\beta+\alpha_{j}}}(\hat{\pi}_{i,j})^{\frac{\beta}{\beta+\alpha_{j}}}}{\sum_{k=1}^{I}(\hat{\theta}_{k})^{\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\beta+\alpha_{j}}}(\hat{\pi}_{k,j})^{\frac{\beta}{\beta+\alpha_{j}}}}. Note that, at time t t , the instantaneous price is p i , t = θ ^ i ∑ k = 1 I θ ^ k p_{i,t}=\frac{\hat{\theta}_{i}}{\sum_{k=1}^{I}\hat{\theta}_{k}} . Then, replacing θ ^ i \hat{\theta}_{i} by p i , t ​ ( ∑ k = 1 I θ ^ k ) p_{i,t}(\sum_{k=1}^{I}\hat{\theta}_{k}) in ( 44 ) gives the updated price. To prove result (c), we compute the limiting price p i ∗ p_{i}^{*} when t → ∞ t\rightarrow\infty . The exponential utility naturally meet the convergence conditions in Theorem 3.2 . Thus, the wealth process { 𝐱 j , t } t = 1 , 2 , … \{\mathbf{x}_{j,t}\}_{t=1,2,\ldots} indeed converges to some Pareto optimal allocation as t → ∞ t\rightarrow\infty . Let 𝝂 = ( ν 1 , … , ν J ) ∈ ℝ + I ∖ { 𝟎 } \boldsymbol{\nu}=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{J})\in\mathbb{R}^{I}_{+}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\} . We then solve problem 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) for the Pareto optimal wealth allocation, max 𝐱 1 , … , 𝐱 J , 𝐲 \displaystyle\max_{\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J},\mathbf{y}}~ ∑ j = 1 J ν j V j ( 𝐱 j ) = − ∑ j = 1 J ν j α j ∑ i = 1 I π i , j e − α j ​ x i , j \displaystyle~\sum_{j=1}^{J}\nu_{j}V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j})=-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{\nu_{j}}{\alpha_{j}}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\pi_{i,j}e^{-\alpha_{j}x_{i,j}} (45) Subject to − 1 β ∑ i = 1 I θ i e − β ​ y i ≥ − 1 β e − β ​ W 0 \displaystyle~-\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}e^{-\beta y_{i}}\geq-\frac{1}{\beta}e^{-\beta W_{0}} ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j + 𝐲 = w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 , \displaystyle~\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j}+\mathbf{y}={w}^{\textrm{all}}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}, where 𝐱 j = ( x 1 , j , x 2 , j , … , x I , j ) \mathbf{x}_{j}=(x_{1,j},x_{2,j},\ldots,x_{I,j}) and 𝐲 = ( y 1 , … , y I ) \mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{I}) . The last equality constraint helps to eliminates 𝐲 \mathbf{y} in this problem. The KKT condition characterizes the optimal solution as follows, (46) ν j π i , j e − α j ​ x i , j ∗ − λ θ i B e β ​ ∑ j = 1 J x i , j ∗ = 0 , i = 1 , 2 , … , I , and j = 1 , … , J , \displaystyle\nu_{j}\pi_{i,j}e^{-\alpha_{j}x_{i,j}^{*}}-\lambda\theta_{i}Be^{\beta\sum_{j=1}^{J}x_{i,j}^{*}}=0,~~i=1,2,\ldots,I,~\textrm{and}~~j=1,\ldots,J, where λ ≥ 0 \lambda\geq 0 is the Lagrange multiplier for constraint ( 45 ) and B ≜ e − β ​ W 0 B\triangleq e^{-\beta W_{0}} . Note that it must have λ &gt; 0 \lambda&gt;0 , as the constraint ( 45 ) is binding at optimal solution. We then let M i = λ ​ θ i ​ e β ​ ∑ j = 1 J x i , j ∗ M_{i}=\lambda\theta_{i}e^{\beta\sum_{j=1}^{J}x_{i,j}^{*}} in equation ( 46 ), which leads to x i , j ∗ = 1 α j ​ ln ⁡ ( ν j ​ π i , j B ⋅ M i ) x_{i,j}^{*}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{j}}\ln\left(\frac{\nu_{j}\pi_{i,j}}{B\cdot M_{i}}\right) . Substituting x i , j ∗ x_{i,j}^{*} back to the definition of M i M_{i} gives, (47) 1 β ​ ln ⁡ ( M i λ ​ θ i ) = ∑ j = 1 J x i , j ∗ = ∑ j = 1 J 1 α j ​ ln ⁡ ( ν j ​ π i , j B ⋅ M i ) ⇒ M i = ( λ ​ θ i ) η ​ ∏ j = 1 J ( ν j ​ π i , j B ) η j , \displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\ln\left(\frac{M_{i}}{\lambda\theta_{i}}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{J}x_{i,j}^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{1}{\alpha_{j}}\ln\left(\frac{\nu_{j}\pi_{i,j}}{B\cdot M_{i}}\right)\Rightarrow~M_{i}=(\lambda\theta_{i})^{\eta}\prod_{j=1}^{J}\left(\frac{\nu_{j}\pi_{i,j}}{B}\right)^{\eta_{j}}, where η j ≜ 1 / α j ∑ j = 1 J 1 α j + 1 β \eta_{j}\triangleq\frac{1/\alpha_{j}}{\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{1}{\alpha_{j}}+\frac{1}{\beta}} and η ≜ 1 / β ∑ j = 1 J 1 α j + 1 β \eta\triangleq\frac{1/\beta}{\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{1}{\alpha_{j}}+\frac{1}{\beta}} . We then compute the instantaneous price associated with the Pareto optimal wealth x i , j ∗ x_{i,j}^{*} which gives p i ∗ \displaystyle p^{*}_{i} = θ i ​ e − β ⁡ ( w 0 all − ∑ j = 1 J x i , j ∗ ) ∑ k = 1 I θ k ​ e − β ⁡ ( w 0 all − ∑ j = 1 J x k , j ∗ ) = M i ∑ k = 1 I M k , i = 1 , … , I , \displaystyle=\frac{\theta_{i}e^{-\beta(w^{\textrm{all}}_{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{J}x_{i,j}^{*})}}{\sum_{k=1}^{I}\theta_{k}e^{-\beta(w^{\textrm{all}}_{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{J}x_{k,j}^{*})}}=\frac{M_{i}}{\sum_{k=1}^{I}M_{k}},~i=1,\ldots,I, where w 0 all = W 0 + ∑ j = 1 J w j , 0 w_{0}^{\textrm{all}}=W_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}w_{j,0} . Substituting equation ( 47 ) into the above formulation gives the result ( 12 ). □ \square Example of Risk Measure Based-MU Function Example A.2. We present an example to illustrate that, without additional conditions, the convex risk measure-based MU function may fail to satisfy the convergence condition in Theorem 3.2 . In this example, we consider the widely used risk measure called average value at risk (AVaR). Given 𝐱 ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} and a probability vector 𝝅 ∈ Θ I \boldsymbol{\pi}\in\Theta_{I} , the AVaR of 𝐱 \mathbf{x} is defined as AVaR β ​ ( 𝐱 ) = max ⁡ 𝐩 ∈ Θ I ​ { − 𝐩 ⊤ ​ 𝐱 | 𝟎 ≤ 𝐩 ≤ 1 β ​ 𝝅 } \text{AVaR}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x})=\max{\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}}\left\{-\mathbf{p}^{\top}\mathbf{x}~|~\mathbf{0}\leq\mathbf{p}\leq\frac{1}{\beta}\boldsymbol{\pi}\right\} , where β ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \beta\in(0,1) represents the confidence level. In a binary setting with 𝝅 = ( 0.5 , 0.5 ) \boldsymbol{\pi}=(0.5,0.5) and β = 0.95 \beta=0.95 , the probability 𝐩 = ( p 1 , p 2 ) \mathbf{p}=(p_{1},p_{2}) has a feasible set 𝒜 p ≜ { ( p 1 , p 2 ) | p 1 + p 2 = 1 , p 1 ∈ [ 0.4737 , 0.5263 ] } \mathcal{A}_{p}\triangleq\{(p_{1},p_{2})~|~p_{1}+p_{2}=1,~p_{1}\in[0.4737,0.5263]\} . We then consider the direction 𝐝 = ( − 2 , 3 ) \mathbf{d}=(-2,3) . For any α &gt; 0 \alpha&gt;0 , we have − 𝐩 ⊤ ​ ( 𝐱 + α ​ 𝐝 ) = − 𝐩 ⊤ ​ 𝐱 − α ⋅ ( − 2 ​ p 1 + 3 ​ p 2 ) -\mathbf{p}^{\top}(\mathbf{x}+\alpha\mathbf{d})=-\mathbf{p}^{\top}\mathbf{x}-\alpha\cdot(-2p_{1}+3p_{2}) . Note that, for any 𝐩 ∈ 𝒜 p \mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{A}_{p} , it has ( − 2 ​ p 1 + 3 ​ p 2 ) &gt; 0 (-2p_{1}+3p_{2})&gt;0 . Thus,as α → ∞ \alpha\rightarrow\infty , AVaR β ​ ( 𝐱 + α ​ 𝐝 ) → ∞ \text{AVaR}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x}+\alpha\mathbf{d})\rightarrow\infty , proving that 𝐝 \mathbf{d} is a direction of recession for the function AVaR β ​ ( 𝐱 ) \text{AVaR}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x}) . Consequently, the function AVaR ​ β ​ ( 𝐱 ) \text{AVaR}\beta(\mathbf{x}) violates condition (ii) in Assumption 2 . Furthermore, since AVaR is a coherent risk measure, the translation-invariant property implies AVaR β ​ ( α ⋅ 𝐱 ) = α ⋅ AVaR β ​ ( 𝐱 ) \text{AVaR}_{\beta}(\alpha\cdot\mathbf{x})=\alpha\cdot\text{AVaR}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x}) for any α ∈ ℝ \alpha\in\mathbb{R} . This property contradicts the strict concavity condition for non-equivalent vectors, i.e., condition (i) in Assumption 2 . Proof of Proposition 4.2 . Proof. Proof. As this proof applies for all ρ j ​ ( ⋅ ) \rho_{j}(\cdot) , j = 0 , 1 ​ … , J j=0,1\ldots,J , we simply drop the index j j in the following proof. First, we show that ρ \rho is well-defined for any 𝐱 ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} . Indeed, for 𝐱 = ( x 1 , … , x I ) ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{I})\in\mathbb{R}^{I} , KKT conditions characterize the optimizing probability 𝐪 ∗ \mathbf{q}^{*} for the dual representation ( 14 ) as, 𝐱 + ∇ α ​ ( 𝐪 ∗ ) − λ ∗ ⋅ 𝐞 + 𝝁 ∗ = 𝟎 , 𝐪 ∗ ⊤ ​ 𝐞 = 1 , 𝐪 ∗ ⊤ ​ 𝝁 ∗ = 0 , 𝝁 ∗ ≥ 𝟎 , \displaystyle\mathbf{x}+\nabla\alpha(\mathbf{q}^{*})-\lambda^{*}\cdot\mathbf{e}+\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}=\mathbf{0},~{\mathbf{q}^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{e}=1,~{\mathbf{q}^{*}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}=0,~\boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}\geq\mathbf{0}, where 𝝁 \boldsymbol{\mu} is the Lagrange multiplier. One can see that the KKT system is still complicated. However, by temporarily assuming 𝝁 ∗ = 𝟎 \boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}=\mathbf{0} and noting that f i ​ ( q i ) = ∂ α ⁡ ( 𝐪 ) ∂ q i f_{i}(q_{i})=\frac{\partial\alpha(\mathbf{q})}{\partial q_{i}} is an invertible function due to its strict monotonicity, we see that the solution 𝐪 ∗ \mathbf{q}^{*} and λ ∗ \lambda^{*} should satisfy: (48) ∑ i = 1 I f i − 1 ​ ( λ − x i ) = 1 , \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{I}f^{-1}_{i}(\lambda-x_{i})=1, where f i − 1 ​ ( ⋅ ) f^{-1}_{i}(\cdot) is the inverse function of f i ​ ( ⋅ ) f_{i}(\cdot) . Clearly, if λ ¯ \overline{\lambda} large enough, it has λ ¯ − x i ′ = f i ′ ​ ( 1 ) \overline{\lambda}-x_{i^{\prime}}=f_{i^{\prime}}(1) ⇒ \Rightarrow f i ′ − 1 ​ ( λ ¯ − x i ′ ) = 1 f^{-1}_{i^{\prime}}(\overline{\lambda}-x_{i^{\prime}})=1 for some i ′ i^{\prime} , which further implies that ∑ i = 1 I f i − 1 ​ ( λ ¯ − x i ) &gt; 1 \sum_{i=1}^{I}f^{-1}_{i}(\overline{\lambda}-x_{i})&gt;1 . On the other hand, it has lim λ → − ∞ ​ ∑ i = 1 I f i − 1 ​ ( λ − x i ) = 0 &lt; 1 \underset{\lambda\to-\infty}{\lim}\sum_{i=1}^{I}f^{-1}_{i}(\lambda-x_{i})=0&lt;1 . Thus, we can find a λ ∗ \lambda^{*} that uniquely solves the equation ( 48 ). Furthermore, since ∇ 2 α ​ ( 𝐪 ) = diag ⁡ ( f 1 ′ ​ ( p 1 ) , … , f I ′ ​ ( p I ) ) \nabla^{2}\alpha(\mathbf{q})=\mathrm{diag}\big(f^{\prime}_{1}(p_{1}),\ldots,f^{\prime}_{I}(p_{I})\big) which is positive definite for any 𝐪 ∈ Θ I \mathbf{q}\in\Theta_{I} , we know that α ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \alpha(\cdot) is strictly convex, therefore the optimal solution is unique. As a result, it indeed confirms our previous assumption 𝝁 ∗ = 𝟎 \boldsymbol{\mu}^{*}=\mathbf{0} . Moreover, if x i = − ∞ x_{i}=-\infty for some i i , by setting the corresponding p i ∗ = 0 p^{*}_{i}=0 , we may still solve ( 48 ) for some λ ∗ ∈ ℝ \lambda^{*}\in\mathbb{R} . Next, we show that the recession cone of ρ \rho is contained in the non-negative orthant. Assume 𝐝 ∈ recc ⁡ ( ρ ) \mathbf{d}\in\mathrm{recc}(\rho) with d i &lt; 0 d_{i}&lt;0 for some i i . Without loss of generality, we assume d 1 &lt; 0 d_{1}&lt;0 . Due to the strict monotonicity of f i ​ ( ⋅ ) f_{i}(\cdot) , it has ∫ 0 1 f 1 ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t &lt; f ⁡ ( 1 ) ​ and ​ lim p → 0 ​ ∫ 0 p f i ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t ≤ lim p → 0 ​ p ​ f i ​ ( p ) &lt; 0 , \displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}f_{1}(t)dt&lt;f(1)~\mbox{ and }~\underset{p\to 0}{\lim}\int_{0}^{p}f_{i}(t)dt\leq\underset{p\to 0}{\lim}~pf_{i}(p)&lt;0, which implies ρ ⁡ ( 𝐱 + τ ⋅ 𝐝 ) &gt; − x 1 − τ ​ d 1 − f ⁡ ( 1 ) \rho(\mathbf{x}+\tau\cdot\mathbf{d})&gt;-x_{1}-\tau d_{1}-f(1) . As f ⁡ ( 1 ) f(1) is finite, d 1 &lt; 0 d_{1}&lt;0 and the fact that τ \tau can be arbitrarily large, we see that such 𝐝 \mathbf{d} cannot be a direction of recession of ρ \rho . As for the strict convexity of ρ \rho . First, we show that, if 𝐱 1 \mathbf{x}_{1} and 𝐱 2 \mathbf{x}_{2} are not equivalent wealth, the correspondent optimal probabilities 𝐪 1 ∗ \mathbf{q}_{1}^{*} and 𝐪 2 ∗ \mathbf{q}_{2}^{*} generated from the dual problem ( 14 ) do not coincide, i.e., it has 𝐩 1 ∗ ≠ 𝐩 2 ∗ \mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}\neq\mathbf{p}_{2}^{*} . Indeed, from the above discussion, we know that (49) ∇ α ​ ( 𝐪 1 ∗ ) = λ 1 ∗ ⋅ 𝐞 − 𝐱 1 , ∇ α ​ ( 𝐪 2 ∗ ) = λ 2 ∗ ⋅ 𝐞 − 𝐱 2 , \displaystyle\nabla\alpha(\mathbf{q}_{1}^{*})=\lambda_{1}^{*}\cdot\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{x}_{1},~~\nabla\alpha(\mathbf{q}_{2}^{*})=\lambda_{2}^{*}\cdot\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{x}_{2}, for some λ 1 , λ 2 ∈ ℝ \lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{R} . Since 𝐱 1 \mathbf{x}_{1} and 𝐱 2 \mathbf{x}_{2} are not in equivalent class, ( 49 ) implies ∇ α ​ ( 𝐪 1 ∗ ) ≠ ∇ α ​ ( 𝐪 2 ∗ ) ⟹ 𝐪 1 ∗ ≠ 𝐪 2 ∗ , \displaystyle\nabla\alpha(\mathbf{q}_{1}^{*})\neq\nabla\alpha(\mathbf{q}_{2}^{*})\implies\mathbf{q}_{1}^{*}\neq\mathbf{q}_{2}^{*}, due to strict monotonicity of f i ​ ( ⋅ ) f_{i}(\cdot) , i = 1 , … , I i=1,\ldots,I . If 𝐱 1 \mathbf{x}_{1} and 𝐱 2 \mathbf{x}_{2} are not in equivalent class, then τ ​ 𝐱 1 + ( 1 − τ ) ​ 𝐱 2 \tau\mathbf{x}_{1}+(1-\tau)\mathbf{x}_{2} will not be in the equivalent class with 𝐱 1 \mathbf{x}_{1} and 𝐱 2 \mathbf{x}_{2} for all t ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) t\in(0,1) . Then, let 𝐪 ∗ \mathbf{q}^{*} , 𝐪 1 ∗ \mathbf{q}_{1}^{*} and 𝐪 2 ∗ \mathbf{q}_{2}^{*} be the solutions of problem ( 14 ) for τ ​ 𝐱 1 + ( 1 − τ ) ​ 𝐱 2 \tau\mathbf{x}_{1}+(1-\tau)\mathbf{x}_{2} , 𝐱 1 \mathbf{x}_{1} and 𝐱 2 \mathbf{x}_{2} , respectively. Then, it has 𝐪 ∗ ≠ 𝐪 1 ∗ \mathbf{q}^{*}\neq\mathbf{q}_{1}^{*} and 𝐪 ∗ ≠ 𝐪 2 ∗ \mathbf{q}^{*}\neq\mathbf{q}_{2}^{*} , which further implies, ρ ⁡ ( τ ​ 𝐱 1 + ( 1 − τ ) ​ 𝐱 2 ) \displaystyle\rho(\tau\mathbf{x}_{1}+(1-\tau)\mathbf{x}_{2}) = − 𝐪 ∗ ⊤ ​ ( τ ​ 𝐱 1 + ( 1 − τ ) ​ 𝐱 2 ) − α ⁡ ( 𝐪 ∗ ) \displaystyle=-{\mathbf{q}^{*}}^{\top}(\tau\mathbf{x}_{1}+(1-\tau)\mathbf{x}_{2})-\alpha(\mathbf{q}^{*}) = τ ⁡ ( − 𝐪 ∗ ⊤ ​ 𝐱 1 − α ⁡ ( 𝐪 ∗ ) ) + ( 1 − τ ) ​ ( − 𝐪 ∗ ⊤ ​ 𝐱 2 − α ⁡ ( 𝐪 ∗ ) ) \displaystyle=\tau\big(-{\mathbf{q}^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{1}-\alpha(\mathbf{q}^{*})\big)+(1-\tau)\big(-{\mathbf{q}^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{2}-\alpha(\mathbf{q}^{*})\big) &lt; τ ⁡ ( − 𝐪 1 ∗ ⊤ ​ 𝐱 1 − α ⁡ ( 𝐪 1 ∗ ) ) + ( 1 − τ ) ​ ( − 𝐪 2 ∗ ⊤ ​ 𝐱 2 − α ⁡ ( 𝐪 2 ∗ ) ) \displaystyle&lt;\tau\big(-{\mathbf{q}_{1}^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{1}-\alpha(\mathbf{q}_{1}^{*})\big)+(1-\tau)\big(-{\mathbf{q}_{2}^{*}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{2}-\alpha(\mathbf{q}_{2}^{*})\big) = τ ​ ρ ​ ( 𝐱 1 ) + ( 1 − τ ) ​ ρ ​ ( 𝐱 2 ) . \displaystyle=\tau\rho(\mathbf{x}_{1})+(1-\tau)\rho(\mathbf{x}_{2}). The above inequality implies the strict convexity of ρ ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \rho(\cdot) . □ \square Proof of Theorem 4.3 Proof. Using Theorem 3.2 , the limiting allocation can be identified by solving the following problem for some weights ( ν 1 , ν 2 , … , ν J ) ∈ ℝ + J (\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{J})\in\mathbb{R}^{J}_{+} , min 𝐱 1 , … , 𝐱 J \displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}}{\min}~ ∑ j = 1 J ν j ​ ρ j ​ ( 𝐱 j ) \displaystyle~\sum_{j=1}^{J}~\nu_{j}\rho_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j}) (50) Subjec to ρ 0 ​ ( 𝐲 ) ≤ ρ 0 ​ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) , \displaystyle~\rho_{0}(\mathbf{y})\leq\rho_{0}(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}), 𝐲 + ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j = w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 . \displaystyle~\mathbf{y}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j}={w}_{0}^{\textrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{e}. Then optimality condition implies that, the optimal allocation ( 𝐱 1 ∗ , … , 𝐱 J ∗ ) (\mathbf{x}_{1}^{*},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}^{*}) should satisfy, ν j ∇ ρ j ( 𝐱 j ∗ ) = μ ∇ ρ 0 ( w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 − ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j ∗ ) , ∀ j = 1 , … , J , \displaystyle\nu_{j}\nabla\rho_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j}^{*})=\mu\nabla\rho_{0}({w}_{0}^{\textrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{e}-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{*}),\quad\forall j=1,\ldots,J, where μ ≥ 0 \mu\geq 0 is the Lagrange multiplier for constraint ( 50 ). On the other hand, since ρ j ​ ( ⋅ ) \rho_{j}(\cdot) is a convex risk measure for all j = 0 , … , J j=0,\ldots,J , it has ∇ 𝐱 j ρ j ​ ( ⋅ ) ≥ 𝟎 \nabla_{\mathbf{x}_{j}}\rho_{j}(\cdot)\geq\mathbf{0} and ∇ 𝐱 j ρ j ​ ( ⋅ ) ⊤ ​ 𝐞 = 1 \nabla_{\mathbf{x}_{j}}\rho_{j}(\cdot)^{\top}\mathbf{e}=1 for all j = 0 , … , J j=0,\ldots,J , which implies that ν 1 = ⋯ = ν J = μ \nu_{1}=\cdots=\nu_{J}=\mu . (Otherwise problem ( 50 ) is unbounded, which will not give any Pareto wealth allocation.) Clearly, this is exactly the optimality conditions that characterizes the solution of 𝒫 po RM \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}^{\mathrm{RM}} . As previous analysis shows that ν 1 = ⋯ = ν J = μ \nu_{1}=\cdots=\nu_{J}=\mu , we consider ( 50 ) with all weights in the objective being 1 1 . To simplify the notation, we let 𝐱 0 = 𝐲 \mathbf{x}_{0}=\mathbf{y} . Then, using dual representation of convex risk measure and considering the Lagrangian, problem 𝒫 po RM \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}^{\mathrm{RM}} becomes (51) max 𝐱 0 , 𝐱 1 , … , 𝐱 J ​ { ∑ j = 0 J inf 𝐪 j ∈ Θ I ​ { 𝐪 j ⊤ ​ 𝐱 j + α j ​ ( 𝐪 j ) } | ∑ j = 0 J 𝐱 j = w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 } . \displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}}{\max}\Big\{\sum_{j=0}^{J}\underset{\mathbf{q}_{j}\in\Theta_{I}}{\inf}~\{\mathbf{q}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{j}+\alpha_{j}(\mathbf{q}_{j})\}~|~\sum_{j=0}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j}=w_{0}^{\textrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{e}\Big\}. Denote by 𝐪 j ​ ( 𝐱 j ) \mathbf{q}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j}) the optimizing probability generated from dual representation ( 14 ) for a given 𝐱 j \mathbf{x}_{j} for all j = 0 , … , J j=0,\ldots,J . Obviously, the gradient of ρ j ​ ( ⋅ ) \rho_{j}(\cdot) at 𝐱 j \mathbf{x}_{j} is exactly ∇ 𝐱 j ρ j ​ ( 𝐱 j ) = 𝐪 j ​ ( 𝐱 j ) \nabla_{\mathbf{x}_{j}}\rho_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j})=\mathbf{q}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j}) . Thus, if 𝐱 0 \mathbf{x}_{0} and ( 𝐱 1 ∗ , … , 𝐱 J ∗ ) (\mathbf{x}_{1}^{*},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}^{*}) are the optimal solution of problem 𝒫 po RM \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}^{\mathrm{RM}} , it has, { ∇ ρ 0 ​ ( 𝐱 0 ∗ ) = ∇ ρ 1 ​ ( 𝐱 1 ∗ ) = ⋯ = ∇ ρ J ​ ( 𝐱 J ∗ ) ∑ j = 0 J 𝐱 j ∗ = w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 . \displaystyle\begin{cases}\nabla\rho_{0}(\mathbf{x}_{0}^{*})=\nabla\rho_{1}(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{*})=\cdots=\nabla\rho_{J}(\mathbf{x}_{J}^{*})\\ \sum_{j=0}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{*}=w_{0}^{\textrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{e}.\end{cases} The above property means all the dual variables 𝐪 j ​ ( 𝐱 j ) \mathbf{q}_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j}) should be identical at optimal solution. Thus, we may rewrite problem ( 51 ) in the following equivalent form, max 𝐱 0 , … , 𝐱 J ​ inf 𝐪 ∈ Θ I ​ { 𝐪 ⊤ ​ ∑ j = 0 J 𝐱 j + ∑ j = 0 J α j ​ ( 𝐪 ) | ∑ j = 0 J 𝐱 j = w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 } , \displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{x}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}}{\max}~\underset{\mathbf{q}\in\Theta_{I}}{\inf}~\Big\{\mathbf{q}^{\top}\sum_{j=0}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j}+\sum_{j=0}^{J}\alpha_{j}(\mathbf{q})~\Big|~~\sum_{j=0}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j}=w_{0}^{\textrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{e}\}, which further implies that the optimal objective value of problem ( 51 ) is independent to OPEN ( 𝐱 0 ∗ , … , 𝐱 J ∗ ) ) (\mathbf{x}_{0}^{*},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}^{*})) . Thus, we can find the limiting price by 𝐩 ∗ = arg ⁡ min 𝐪 ∈ Θ I ​ { ∑ j = 0 J α j ​ ( 𝐪 ) } \mathbf{p}^{*}=\arg\min_{\mathbf{q}\in\Theta_{I}}~\{\sum_{j=0}^{J}\alpha_{j}(\mathbf{q})\} . □ \square Proof of Corollary 4.4 . Proof. We focus on the case of γ ≠ 0 \gamma\neq 0 , the case of γ = 0 \gamma=0 can be proved in the similar fashion. It can be verified that α j ​ ( ⋅ ) \alpha_{j}(\cdot) meets the conditions in Theorem 4.3 for all j j . Thus, the limiting price can be identified by solving (52) max 𝐪 ∈ Θ I \displaystyle\max_{\mathbf{q}\in\Theta_{I}}~ ∑ i = 1 I q i ​ ( q i θ i ) γ − 1 ​ h 0 + ∑ j = 1 J ∑ i = 1 I q i ​ ( q i π i , j ) γ − 1 ​ h j . \displaystyle~~\sum_{i=1}^{I}~q_{i}\Big(\frac{q_{i}}{\theta_{i}}\Big)^{\gamma-1}h_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sum_{i=1}^{I}~q_{i}\Big(\frac{q_{i}}{\pi_{i,j}}\Big)^{\gamma-1}h_{j}. Let q ∗ = ( q 1 , … , q I ) q^{*}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{I}) be the optimal solution of above problem. The optimality condition implies that q i ∗ γ − 1 ⋅ ( θ i 1 − γ ​ h 0 + ∑ j = 1 J π i , j 1 − γ ​ h j ) = λ ∗ ⇒ q i ∗ \displaystyle{q_{i}^{*}}^{\gamma-1}\cdot\Big(\theta_{i}^{1-\gamma}h_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\pi_{i,j}^{1-\gamma}h_{j}\Big)=\lambda^{*}~~~\Rightarrow~~{q_{i}^{*}} = ( θ i 1 − γ ​ h 0 + ∑ j = 0 J π i , j 1 − γ ​ h j λ ∗ ) 1 1 − γ \displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\theta_{i}^{1-\gamma}h_{0}+\sum_{j=0}^{J}\pi_{i,j}^{1-\gamma}h_{j}}{\lambda^{*}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}} for i = 1 , … , I i=1,\ldots,I . From proof of Theorem 4.3 , we know that the gradient of all ρ j ​ ( ⋅ ) \rho_{j}(\cdot) , j = 0 , … , J j=0,\ldots,J , share the same vector 𝐪 ∗ \mathbf{q}^{*} . Thus, the limiting price is p i ∗ = q i ∗ ∑ k = 1 I q k ∗ = ( θ i 1 − γ ​ h 0 + ∑ j = 1 J h j ​ π i , j 1 − γ ) 1 1 − γ ∑ k = 1 I ( θ k 1 − γ ​ h 0 + ∑ j = 1 J h j ​ π k , j 1 − γ ) 1 1 − γ , \displaystyle p_{i}^{*}=\frac{q_{i}^{*}}{\sum_{k=1}^{I}q_{k}^{*}}=\frac{\Big(\theta_{i}^{1-\gamma}h_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}h_{j}\pi_{i,j}^{1-\gamma}\Big)^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}}{\sum_{k=1}^{I}\Big(\theta_{k}^{1-\gamma}h_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}h_{j}\pi_{k,j}^{1-\gamma}\Big)^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}}, for all i = 1 , … , I i=1,\ldots,I , which completes the proof. □ \square A.4.
