This Pareto optimal wealth allocation can be described by problem 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) with a set of weighting parameters 𝝂 ∗ = ( ν 1 ∗ , … , ν J ∗ ) \boldsymbol{\nu}^{*}=(\nu_{1}^{*},\ldots,\nu_{J}^{*}) : 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ∗ ) : \displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{*}):~ max 𝐲 , 𝐱 1 , … , 𝐱 J ⁡ 1 γ ​ ∑ j = 1 J ( ν j ∗ ⋅ ∑ i = 1 I π i , j ​ x i , j γ ) \displaystyle~\max_{\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}}~~~\frac{1}{\gamma}\sum_{j=1}^{J}(\nu_{j}^{*}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{I}\pi_{i,j}x_{i,j}^{\gamma}) (53) Subject to: 𝐲 + ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j = w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 , \displaystyle~\mathbf{y}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j}={w}_{0}^{\textrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{e}, (54) ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ y i γ ≥ W 0 γ . \displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}y_{i}^{\gamma}\geq W_{0}^{\gamma}. This problem is convex, and we can verify its optimality conditions as follows: (55) ν j ∗ π i , j ( x i , j ∗ ) γ − 1 = ξ i ⇒ x i , j ∗ = ( ν j ∗ ​ π i , j ξ i ) 1 1 − γ , i = 1 , … , I ; j = 1 , … , J , \displaystyle\nu_{j}^{*}\pi_{i,j}(x_{i,j}^{*})^{\gamma-1}=\xi_{i}~~\Rightarrow~~x_{i,j}^{*}=\left(\frac{\nu_{j}^{*}\pi_{i,j}}{\xi_{i}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}},~i=1,\ldots,I;~j=1,\ldots,J, (56) η θ i ( y i ∗ ) γ m − 1 = ξ i ⇒ y i ∗ = ( η ​ θ i ξ i ) 1 1 − γ m , i = 1 , … , I , \displaystyle\eta\theta_{i}(y^{*}_{i})^{\gamma_{m}-1}=\xi_{i}~~\Rightarrow~~y^{*}_{i}=\left(\frac{\eta\theta_{i}}{\xi_{i}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma_{m}}},~~i=1,\ldots,I, where 𝝃 = ( ξ 1 , ξ 2 , … , ξ I ) \boldsymbol{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\ldots,\xi_{I}) and η ≥ 0 \eta\geq 0 are the Lagrange multipliers for constraints ( 53 ) and ( 54 ), respectively. From ( 55 ) and ( 56 ), if η = 0 \eta=0 or ξ = 0 \xi=0 , then then x i , j ∗ = 0 x^{*}_{i,j}=0 and y i ∗ y_{i}^{*} for all i i and j j . Under this case, constraint ( 53 ) can not be satisfied. Thus, we have ξ i &gt; 0 \xi_{i}&gt;0 for all i i and η &gt; 0 \eta&gt;0 . Note that, η &gt; 0 \eta&gt;0 means that the cosntraint ( 54 ) is binding. We substitute ( 56 ) in constraint ( 54 ) which gives (57) η = W 0 1 − γ m ( ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ ( θ i / ξ i ) γ m 1 − γ m ) 1 − γ m γ m . \displaystyle\eta=\frac{W_{0}^{1-\gamma_{m}}}{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}(\theta_{i}/\xi_{i})^{\frac{\gamma_{m}}{1-\gamma_{m}}}\right)^{\frac{1-\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{m}}}}. Substituting ( 55 ) and ( 56 ) to Eq.( 53 ) yields (58) ξ i = ( ∑ j = 1 J ( ν j ∗ ​ π i , j ) 1 1 − γ + ξ i γ − γ m ( 1 − γ m ) ​ ( 1 − γ ) ⋅ ( η ​ θ i ) 1 1 − γ m w 0 all ) 1 − γ , \displaystyle\xi_{i}=\Bigg(\frac{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}(\nu_{j}^{*}\pi_{i,j})^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}+\xi_{i}^{\frac{\gamma-\gamma_{m}}{(1-\gamma_{m})(1-\gamma)}}\cdot(\eta\theta_{i})^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma_{m}}}}{w_{0}^{\textrm{all}}}\Bigg)^{1-\gamma}, for i = 1 , … , I i=1,\ldots,I . Combining ( 57 ) into Eq. ( 58 ) gives the equations for ξ i \xi_{i} : (59) ξ i \displaystyle\xi_{i} = ( ∑ j = 1 J ( ν j ∗ ) 1 1 − γ ⋅ π i , j 1 1 − γ + ( ξ i ) γ − γ m ( 1 − γ ) ​ ( 1 − γ m ) ⋅ ℒ ⁡ ( 𝝃 , 𝜽 ) ⋅ θ i 1 1 − γ m ⋅ W 0 w 0 all ) 1 − γ , i = 1 , … , I , \displaystyle=\Bigg(\frac{\sum\limits^{J}_{j=1}(\nu_{j}^{*})^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}\cdot\pi_{i,j}^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}+(\xi_{i})^{\frac{\gamma-\gamma_{m}}{(1-\gamma)(1-\gamma_{m})}}\cdot\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\theta})\cdot\theta_{i}^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma_{m}}}\cdot W_{0}}{w_{0}^{\textrm{all}}}\Bigg)^{1-\gamma},~~i=1,\ldots,I, where ℒ ⁡ ( 𝝃 , 𝜽 ) ≜ ( ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ ( θ i / ξ i ) γ m 1 − γ m ) − 1 γ m \mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\theta})\triangleq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}(\theta_{i}/\xi_{i})^{\frac{\gamma_{m}}{1-\gamma_{m}}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\gamma_{m}}} . We denote the solution of system of equations ( 59 ) as 𝝃 ∗ = ( ξ 1 ∗ , … , ξ I ∗ ) \boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}=(\xi^{*}_{1},\ldots,\xi^{*}_{I}) . As the constraint ( 54 ) is binding, this optimization problem can be viewed as a convex optimization problem with equality constraints. From Proposition 3.1.1 in [ Bertsekas 2016 ] , we know that this problem always has unique Lagrange multipliers, which further implies Eq. ( 59 ) always admits the solution 𝜼 ∗ \boldsymbol{\eta}^{*} together with η ∗ \eta^{*} . To compute the limiting price, we use Proposition 2.3 , which gives (60) p i ∗ = θ i ⋅ ( y i ∗ ) γ m − 1 ∑ k = 1 I θ k ⋅ ( y k ∗ ) γ m − 1 = ξ i ∗ , ∑ k = 1 I ξ k ∗ \displaystyle p_{i}^{*}=\frac{\theta_{i}\cdot(y_{i}^{*})^{\gamma_{m}-1}}{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}\theta_{k}\cdot(y_{k}^{*})^{\gamma_{m}-1}}=\frac{\xi^{*}_{i},}{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}\xi_{k}^{*}} where the last equality is from ( 56 ). This completes the proof for the result (i). Then we focus on result (ii). For any i ∈ { 1 , … , I } i\in\{1,\ldots,I\} , we define s i , j ≜ ( ν j ∗ ⋅ π i , j ) 1 / ( 1 − γ ) s_{i,j}\triangleq(\nu_{j}^{*}\cdot\pi_{i,j})^{1/(1-\gamma)} for j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J . The assumption of this result says that π i , j \pi_{i,j} and w 0 , j w_{0,j} for j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J are independently sampled from a given distribution. Furthermore, given these traders’ parameters, the distribution of the weight parameters { ν j ∗ } | j = 1 J \{\nu_{j}^{*}\}|_{j=1}^{J} is purely determined by different trading sequences. Note that changing the indices of any two traders does not affect the distribution of { ν j ∗ } | j = 1 J \{\nu_{j}^{*}\}|_{j=1}^{J} . That is to say, the homogeneity of all traders implies { s i , 1 , s i , 2 , … , s i , J } \{s_{i,1},s_{i,2},\ldots,s_{i,J}\} is sequence of exchangeable random variable (see [ Klenke 2013 ] ). It is known that an infinite exchangeable sequence is almost surely Cesàro convergent to a constant (see, e.g., [ Klenke 2013 ] or Proposition 1.1 in [ Tappe 2021 ] ). That is to say, the Cesàro summation of s i , j s_{i,j} satisfies, lim J → ∞ 1 J ​ ∑ j = 1 J s i , j → a . s . C i \lim\limits_{J\to\infty}\frac{1}{J}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}s_{i,j}\xrightarrow{a.s.}C_{i} almost surely where C i C_{i} is a finite number. Let 𝝃 ∗ | J = ( ξ 1 ∗ | J , … , ξ I ∗ | J ) \boldsymbol{\xi^{*}}|_{J}=(\xi^{*}_{1}|_{J},\ldots,\xi^{*}_{I}|_{J}) be solution to the Eq. ( 59 ) for any fixed J J . Then Eq. ( 59 ) can be written as, (61) ξ i ∗ | J \displaystyle\xi^{*}_{i}|_{J} = ( ∑ j = 1 J s i , j + M ⁡ ( 𝝃 ∗ | J ) w 0 all ) 1 − γ ⇒ ξ i ∗ | J = ( 1 J ​ ∑ j = 1 J s i , j + 1 J ​ M ​ ( 𝝃 ∗ | J ) 1 J ​ ∑ j = 1 J w 0 , j + W 0 J ) 1 − γ , \displaystyle=\Bigg(\frac{\sum\limits^{J}_{j=1}s_{i,j}+M(\boldsymbol{\xi^{*}}|_{J})}{w_{0}^{\textrm{all}}}\Bigg)^{1-\gamma}~~\Rightarrow~~\xi^{*}_{i}|_{J}=\Bigg(\frac{\frac{1}{J}\sum\limits^{J}_{j=1}s_{i,j}+\frac{1}{J}M(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}|_{J})}{\frac{1}{J}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}w_{0,j}+\frac{W_{0}}{J}}\Bigg)^{1-\gamma}, where M ⁡ ( 𝝃 ∗ | J ) = ( ξ i ∗ | J ) γ − γ m ( 1 − γ ) ​ ( 1 − γ m ) ⋅ ℒ ⁡ ( 𝝃 ∗ | J , 𝜽 ) ⋅ θ i 1 1 − γ m ⋅ W 0 M(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}|_{J})=(\xi_{i}^{*}|_{J})^{\frac{\gamma-\gamma_{m}}{(1-\gamma)(1-\gamma_{m})}}\cdot\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}|_{J},\boldsymbol{\theta})\cdot\theta_{i}^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma_{m}}}\cdot W_{0} . Recall that the j j -th trader’s initial wealth is sampled from a given distribution where we use w ¯ ≜ E ​ [ w ¯ j , 0 ] \bar{w}\triangleq\textrm{E}[\bar{w}_{j,0}] to denote its mean value. Note that market maker’s parameters (i.e., { θ i } | i = 1 I \{\theta_{i}\}|_{i=1}^{I} and W 0 W_{0} ) are constant and 𝝃 ∗ | J \boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}|_{J} is the finite solution of equations ( 59 ) for any J J . Clearly, it has lim J → ∞ 1 J ​ M ​ ( 𝝃 ∗ | J ) → 0 \lim\limits_{J\rightarrow\infty}~\frac{1}{J}M(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}|_{J})\rightarrow 0 and lim J → ∞ W 0 J → 0 \lim\limits_{J\rightarrow\infty}~\frac{W_{0}}{J}\rightarrow 0 . Morever, due to the Law of large number, it has lim J → ∞ 1 J ​ ∑ j = 1 J w 0 , j → a . s . w ¯ \lim\limits_{J\rightarrow\infty}~\frac{1}{J}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}w_{0,j}\xrightarrow{a.s.}\bar{w} almost surely. Thus, as J → ∞ J\rightarrow\infty , Eq. ( 61 ) implies lim J → ∞ ξ i ∗ | J → a . s . ( C i / w ¯ ) 1 − γ \lim\limits_{J\rightarrow\infty}\xi^{*}_{i}|_{J}\xrightarrow{a.s.}(C_{i}/\bar{w})^{1-\gamma} almost surely. We consider the price formula ( 60 ) and use the above result, it has lim J → ∞ p i ∗ = lim J → ∞ ξ i ∗ | J ∑ k = 1 J ξ k ∗ | J = C i 1 − γ ∑ k = 1 I C k 1 − γ , i = 1 , … , I , \displaystyle\lim_{J\rightarrow\infty}p_{i}^{*}=\lim_{J\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\xi^{*}_{i}|_{J}}{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{J}\xi^{*}_{k}|_{J}}=\frac{C_{i}^{1-\gamma}}{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}C_{k}^{1-\gamma}},~~i=1,\ldots,I, which completes the proof of the second result. □ \square A.5. Price Formula for Market with Logarithm-Exponential Utilities In this section, we explore a market model featuring mixed utility functions. Specifically, we consider a setup where the MU functions for the market maker (market maker) and the j j -th trader are defined as follows: (62) U ( 𝐲 ) = ∑ i = 1 I θ i 1 − e − β ⋅ y i β and V j ( 𝐱 ) = ∑ i = 1 I π i , j ln ( x i , j ) , j = 1 , … , J , \displaystyle U(\mathbf{y})=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}\frac{1-e^{-\beta\cdot y_{i}}}{\beta}~~\textrm{and}~~V_{j}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\pi_{i,j}\ln(x_{i,j}),~~j=1,\ldots,J, respectively. Here, 𝐲 = ( y 1 , … , y I ) \mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{I}) represents the wealth vectors for the market maker, and 𝐱 j = ( x 1 , j , … , x I , j ) \mathbf{x}_{j}=(x_{1,j},\ldots,x_{I,j}) represents the wealth vector for the j j -th trader. The followin results characterizes the limiting wealth allocation and limiting price of such a market. Proposition A.3. For the Logarithm-Exponential market model defined by ( 62 ), the limiting wealth allocation is characterized by problem 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝛎 ∗ ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{*}) for some weighting parameter 𝛎 ∗ = ( ν 1 ∗ , … , ν J ∗ ) ∈ ℝ + J ∖ { 𝟎 } \boldsymbol{\nu}^{*}=(\nu_{1}^{*},\ldots,\nu_{J}^{*})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{J}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\} and the limiting price 𝐩 ∗ = ( p 1 ∗ , … , p I ∗ ) \mathbf{p}^{*}=(p_{1}^{*},\ldots,p_{I}^{*}) is given by: (63) p ∗ i = ξ i ∗ / ∑ k = 1 I ξ k ∗ , i = 1 , … , I , \displaystyle p^{*}_{i}=\xi_{i}^{*}/\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}\xi_{k}^{*},~~i=1,\ldots,I, where 𝛏 ∗ = ( ξ 1 ∗ , … , ξ I ∗ ) \boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}=(\xi_{1}^{*},\ldots,\xi_{I}^{*}) is the solution of the following equations, (64) ξ i ∗ = ∑ j = 1 J ν j ∗ ⋅ π i , j + 1 β ​ ξ i ∗ ​ log ⁡ ( θ i / ξ i ∗ ∑ k = 1 I ξ k ∗ ) ∑ j = 1 J w j , 0 , i = 1 , … , I . \displaystyle\xi_{i}^{*}=\frac{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}\nu_{j}^{*}\cdot\pi_{i,j}+\frac{1}{\beta}{\xi}_{i}^{*}\log\left(\theta_{i}\Big/\frac{{\xi}_{i}^{*}}{\sum_{k=1}^{I}{\xi}_{k}^{*}}\right)}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}w_{j,0}},~~i=1,\ldots,I. Proof. The MU function defined in ( 62 ) satisfies the convergence condition stated in Theorem 3.2 . Therefore, in this market model, the market state converges to a Pareto-optimal wealth allocation, which can be determined by solving problem 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) for some weighting parameter 𝝂 ∗ = ( ν 1 ∗ , … , ν J ∗ ) \boldsymbol{\nu}^{*}=(\nu_{1}^{*},\ldots,\nu_{J}^{*}) . In this context, problem 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ∗ ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{*}) can be formulated as follows: 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ∗ ) : \displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{*}):~ max ⁡ ∑ j = 1 J 𝐲 , 𝐱 1 , … , 𝐱 J ⁡ ( ν j ∗ ⋅ ∑ i = 1 I π i , j ​ ln ⁡ ( x i , j ) ) \displaystyle~\max_{\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}}~~~\sum_{j=1}^{J}(\nu_{j}^{*}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{I}\pi_{i,j}\ln(x_{i,j})) Subject to: 𝐲 + ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j = w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 , \displaystyle~\mathbf{y}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j}={w}_{0}^{\textrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{e}, − 1 β ∑ i = 1 I θ i exp ( − β y i ) ≥ − 1 β exp ( − β W 0 ) , \displaystyle~-\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}\exp(-\beta y_{i})\geq-\frac{1}{\beta}\exp(-\beta W_{0}), where the decision varibles are 𝐲 = ( y 1 , … , y I ) \mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{I}) and 𝐱 j = ( x 1 , j , … , x I , j ) \mathbf{x}_{j}=(x_{1,j},\ldots,x_{I,j}) for j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J . We may eliminate W 0 W_{0} from this problem by introducing a new decision variable 𝐳 = ( z 1 , … , z I ) \mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{I}) , where z i = y i − W 0 z_{i}=y_{i}-W_{0} for i = 1 , … , I i=1,\ldots,I . This transforms problem 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ∗ ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{*}) into the following form: 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ∗ ) : \displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{*}):~ max ⁡ ∑ j = 1 J 𝐳 , 𝐱 1 , … , 𝐱 J ⁡ ( ν j ∗ ⋅ ∑ i = 1 I π i , j ​ ln ⁡ ( x i , j ) ) \displaystyle~\max_{\mathbf{z},\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}}~~~\sum_{j=1}^{J}(\nu_{j}^{*}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{I}\pi_{i,j}\ln(x_{i,j})) (65) Subject to: 𝐳 + ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j = ( ∑ j = 1 J w j , 0 ) ⋅ 𝐞 , \displaystyle~\mathbf{z}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j}=(\sum_{j=1}^{J}w_{j,0})\cdot\mathbf{e}, (66) ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ exp ⁡ ( − β ​ z i ) ≤ 1 . \displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}\exp(-\beta z_{i})\leq 1. Let 𝐳 ∗ \mathbf{z}^{*} and 𝐱 j ∗ \mathbf{x}_{j}^{*} for j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J be the optimal solution to this problem. Due to convexity, the solution can be characterized by the following optimality conditions: x ∗ i , j = ν j ∗ ​ π i , j ξ i ∗ , i = 1 , … , I , j = 1 , … , J , \displaystyle x^{*}_{i,j}=\frac{\nu_{j}^{*}\pi_{i,j}}{\xi_{i}^{*}},~~i=1,\ldots,I,~~j=1,\ldots,J, (67) η ∗ ⋅ θ i ⋅ exp ( − β z i ∗ ) = ξ i ∗ , i = 1 , … , I , \displaystyle\eta^{*}\cdot\theta_{i}\cdot\exp(-\beta z_{i}^{*})=\xi_{i}^{*},~~i=1,\ldots,I, where 𝝃 ∗ = ( ξ 1 ∗ , … , ξ I ∗ ) \boldsymbol{\xi}^{*}=(\xi_{1}^{*},\ldots,\xi_{I}^{*}) and η ∗ \eta^{*} are the Lagrange multipliers for constraints ( 65 ) and ( 66 ), respectively. It is straightforward to verify that ξ i &gt; ​ 0 \xi_{i}^{&gt;}0 for i = 1 , … , I i=1,\ldots,I and η &gt; ​ 0 \eta^{&gt;}0 , as otherwise, the problem would have no solution. This implies that constraint ( 66 ) is binding, i.e., ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ exp ⁡ ( − β ​ z i ∗ ) = 1 \sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}\exp(-\beta z_{i}^{*})=1 , which further leads to the following relationships: η ∗ = ∑ i = 1 i ξ i ∗ ​ and ​ z i ∗ = 1 β ​ ln ⁡ ( θ i / ξ i ∗ ∑ k = 1 I ξ k ∗ ) . \displaystyle\eta^{*}=\sum_{i=1}^{i}\xi_{i}^{*}~~\textrm{and}~~z_{i}^{*}=\frac{1}{\beta}\ln\left(\theta_{i}\Big/\frac{\xi_{i}^{*}}{\sum_{k=1}^{I}\xi_{k}^{*}}\right). Substituting z i ∗ z^{*}_{i} and x i , j ∗ x^{*}_{i,j} to the equality version of constraint ( 66 ) gives equation ( 64 ) for multiplier 𝝃 ∗ \boldsymbol{\xi}^{*} . On the other hand, using Eq. ( 9 ), we can compute the limiting price as p i ∗ = θ i ​ exp ⁡ ( − β ​ z i ∗ ) ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ exp ⁡ ( − β ​ z i ∗ ) = ξ i ∗ ∑ k = 1 I ξ k ∗ , \displaystyle p_{i}^{*}=\frac{\theta_{i}\exp(-\beta z_{i}^{*})}{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}\exp(-\beta z_{i}^{*})}=\frac{\xi_{i}^{*}}{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}\xi_{k}^{*}}, where the last equality is from Eq. ( 67 ). □ \square Although Proposition A.3 characterizes the limiting price, the weighting parameter is still unknown. We can use the heuristic weight 𝝂 ^ \hat{\boldsymbol{\nu}} given in ( 23 ) as an approximation for the true weighting parameter 𝝂 ∗ \boldsymbol{\nu}^{*} . Since traders have Logarithmic utility ( γ = 0 \gamma=0 ), these weighting parameters become ν ^ j = w ​ j , 0 \hat{\nu}_{j}=w{j,0} for j = 1 , … , J j=1,\ldots,J . By replacing ν j ∗ \nu_{j}^{*} with ν ^ j = w j , 0 \hat{\nu}_{j}=w_{j,0} in ( 63 ) and ( 64 ), we obtain the heuristic price p ^ i \hat{p}_{i} as follows: (68) p ^ i = ξ ^ i ∑ k = 1 I ξ ^ k , i = 1 , … , I , \displaystyle\hat{p}_{i}=\frac{\hat{\xi}_{i}}{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}\hat{\xi}_{k}},~~i=1,\ldots,I, where ξ ^ i \hat{\xi}_{i} is the solution of the following equations (i.e., the equations ( 27 )) (69) ξ ^ i = ∑ j = 1 J w j , 0 ⋅ π i , j + 1 β ​ ξ ^ i ​ log ⁡ ( θ i / ξ ^ i ∑ k = 1 I ξ ^ k ) ∑ j = 1 J w j , 0 , i = 1 , 2 , … , I . \displaystyle\hat{\xi}_{i}=\frac{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}w_{j,0}\cdot\pi_{i,j}+\frac{1}{\beta}\hat{\xi}_{i}\log\left(\theta_{i}\Big/\frac{\hat{\xi}_{i}}{\sum_{k=1}^{I}\hat{\xi}_{k}}\right)}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}w_{j,0}},\quad i=1,2,...,I. Finally, we present an evaluation of the price formula ( 68 ). As a benchmark, we adopt the heuristic price formula given in [ Sethi and Vaughan 2016 ] , which is represented using our notation as: p ˘ i = ∑ j = 1 J w j , 0 ⋅ π i , j ∑ j = 1 J w j , 0 , i = 1 , … , I . \displaystyle\breve{p}_{i}=\frac{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}w_{j,0}\cdot\pi_{i,j}}{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{J}w_{j,0}},~~i=1,\ldots,I. We replicate the experiment presented in Section 7 of [ Sethi and Vaughan 2016 ] , where the beliefs of J = 5 J=5 traders are randomly sampled from a beta distribution, and the market maker’s liquidity parameter is β = 0.05 \beta=0.05 . Figure 6 presents the comparison results. In these figures, the y-axis represents the samples of the true limiting price generated from the simulation, and the x-axis represents the heuristic price. Figure 6(a) shows the performance of Sethi’s heuristic price p ˘ i \breve{p}_{i} . As we can see, Sethi’s formula exhibits a systematic bias, where the limiting price tends to be higher (lower) than the heuristic price when the heuristic price is high (low). On the other hand, as shown in Figure 6(b) , our price formula ( 68 ), which we call the Pareto-Optimal induced price, almost perfectly aligns with the true limiting price. (a) Sethi’s heuristic price p ˘ \breve{p} (b) Pareto Optimality-induced price p ^ \hat{p} Figure 6 . Comparison of the heuristic prices A.6. Price Formula for Market with HARA Utility In this section, we briefly discuss how to generalize the price formula derived from the CRRA-utility-based market to a more general utility family, namely, the utility with hyperbolic absolute risk aversion (HARA utility). We adopt similar notations in Section 5 and define the HARA utility-based MU functions as follows. Given wealth vectors 𝐲 ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} and 𝐱 j ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{x}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} , we assume the market maker’s MU function and the j j -th trader’s MU function are (70) U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) ≜ 1 − γ m γ m ​ ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ ( a m ​ y i 1 − γ m + b m ) γ m ​ and ​ V j ​ ( 𝐱 j ) ≜ 1 − γ γ ​ ∑ i = 1 I π i , j ​ ( a j ​ x i , j 1 − γ + b j ) γ , \displaystyle U(\mathbf{y})\triangleq\frac{1-\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{m}}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}\left(\frac{a_{m}y_{i}}{1-\gamma_{m}}+b_{m}\right)^{\gamma_{m}}~\textrm{and}~~V_{j}(\mathbf{x}_{j})\triangleq\frac{1-\gamma}{\gamma}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\pi_{i,j}\left(\frac{a_{j}x_{i,j}}{1-\gamma}+b_{j}\right)^{\gamma}, respectively, where ( θ 1 , … , θ I ) ∈ Θ I (\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{I})\in\Theta_{I} and ( π 1 , j , … , π I , j ) ∈ Θ I (\pi_{1,j},\ldots,\pi_{I,j})\in\Theta_{I} are the subjective beliefs, γ m \gamma_{m} and γ \gamma are the risk aversion parameters of the market maker and the j j -th trader, respectively; a m &gt; 0 a_{m}&gt;0 , b m ≥ 0 b_{m}\geq 0 , a j &gt; 0 a_{j}&gt;0 and b j ≥ 0 b_{j}\geq 0 for j = 1 , 2 , … , J j=1,2,\ldots,J are the parameters associated with the HARA utility functions. Clearly, CRRA utility is a special case of HARA utility. For examplem, if we set b m = 0 b_{m}=0 and a m = ( 1 − γ m ) γ m − 1 γ m a_{m}=(1-\gamma_{m})^{\frac{\gamma_{m}-1}{\gamma_{m}}} in U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) U(\mathbf{y}) it becomes the CRRA utility. The above definitions of HARA utility-based MU functions satisfy the convergence conditions in Theorem 3.2 . Thus, for a given trading sequence, the market state converges to a Pareto optimal point. We may solve problem 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) with a set of weighting parameters 𝝂 ∗ = ( ν 1 ∗ , … , ν J ∗ ) \boldsymbol{\nu}^{*}=(\nu_{1}^{*},\ldots,\nu_{J}^{*}) for the Pareto optimal wealth allocation, i.e., we have the problem: 𝒫 po ​ ( 𝝂 ∗ ) : \displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{po}}(\boldsymbol{\nu}^{*}):~ max 𝐲 , 𝐱 1 , … , 𝐱 J ⁡ γ 1 − γ ​ ∑ j = 1 J ( ν j ∗ ⋅ ∑ i = 1 I π i , j ​ ( a j ​ x i , j 1 − γ + b j ) γ ) \displaystyle~\max_{\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{J}}~~~\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left(\nu_{j}^{*}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{I}\pi_{i,j}\left(\frac{a_{j}x_{i,j}}{1-\gamma}+b_{j}\right)^{\gamma}\right) (71) Subject to: 𝐲 + ∑ j = 1 J 𝐱 j = w 0 all ⋅ 𝐞 , \displaystyle~\mathbf{y}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mathbf{x}_{j}={w}_{0}^{\textrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{e}, (72) γ m 1 − γ m ​ ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ ( a m ​ y i 1 − γ m + b m ) γ m ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ) ≜ γ m 1 − γ m ​ ( a m ​ W 0 1 − γ m + b m ) γ m . \displaystyle~\frac{\gamma_{m}}{1-\gamma_{m}}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}\left(\frac{a_{m}y_{i}}{1-\gamma_{m}}+b_{m}\right)^{\gamma_{m}}\geq U(W_{0})\triangleq\frac{\gamma_{m}}{1-\gamma_{m}}\left(\frac{a_{m}W_{0}}{1-\gamma_{m}}+b_{m}\right)^{\gamma_{m}}. Similar to the CRRA utility, we have the optimality conditions: (73) ν j ∗ π i , j a j ( a j ​ x i , j 1 − γ + b j ) γ − 1 = ξ , i = 1 , … , I ; j = 1 , … , J , \displaystyle\nu_{j}^{*}\pi_{i,j}a_{j}\left(\frac{a_{j}x_{i,j}}{1-\gamma}+b_{j}\right)^{\gamma-1}=\xi,~~i=1,\ldots,I;j=1,\ldots,J, (74) η θ i a m ( a m ​ y i 1 − γ m + b m ) γ m − 1 = ξ , i = 1 , … , I , \displaystyle\eta\theta_{i}a_{m}\left(\frac{a_{m}y_{i}}{1-\gamma_{m}}+b_{m}\right)^{\gamma_{m}-1}=\xi,~~i=1,\ldots,I, where 𝝃 = ( ξ 1 , ξ 2 , … , ξ I ) \boldsymbol{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\ldots,\xi_{I}) and η ≥ 0 \eta\geq 0 are the Lagrange multipliers for the two contratints. Using ( 74 ) in Eq. ( 72 ) gives (75) η = ( a m ​ W 0 1 − γ m + b m ) 1 − γ m ( ∑ k = 1 I θ k ​ ( a m ​ θ k / ξ k ) γ m 1 − γ m ) 1 − γ m γ m . \displaystyle\eta=\frac{\left(\frac{a_{m}W_{0}}{1-\gamma_{m}}+b_{m}\right)^{1-\gamma_{m}}}{\left(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}\theta_{k}\left(a_{m}\theta_{k}/\xi_{k}\right)^{\frac{\gamma_{m}}{1-\gamma_{m}}}\right)^{\frac{1-\gamma_{m}}{\gamma_{m}}}}. Combining Eqs. ( 73 ), ( 74 ) and ( 75 ), we have the sytem of equations for 𝝃 \boldsymbol{\xi} : ( 1 − γ ) ​ ∑ j = 1 J a j − 1 ​ ( ( ν j ∗ ​ π i , j ​ a j ξ i ) 1 1 − γ − b j ) \displaystyle(1-\gamma)\sum_{j=1}^{J}a_{j}^{-1}\left(\left(\frac{\nu_{j}^{*}\pi_{i,j}a_{j}}{\xi_{i}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}}-b_{j}\right) (76) + 1 − γ m a m ( ( θ i ​ a m ξ i ) 1 1 − γ m ⋅ ( a m ​ W 0 1 − γ m + b m ) ⋅ ℒ ( 𝜽 , 𝝃 ) − b m ) = w 0 all , i = 1 , … , I , \displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~+\frac{1-\gamma_{m}}{a_{m}}\left(\left(\frac{\theta_{i}a_{m}}{\xi_{i}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma_{m}}}\cdot\Big(\frac{a_{m}W_{0}}{1-\gamma_{m}}+b_{m}\Big)\cdot\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\xi})-b_{m}\right)=w_{0}^{\textrm{all}},~~~i=1,\ldots,I, where ℒ ⁡ ( 𝜽 , 𝝃 ) = ∑ k = 1 I θ k ​ ( θ k / ξ k ) γ m 1 − γ m \mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\xi})=\sum_{k=1}^{I}\theta_{k}(\theta_{k}/\xi_{k})^{\frac{\gamma_{m}}{1-\gamma_{m}}} . Similar to the case of CRRA utility, the above equations admit a unique solution (see Appedix A.4 ), which we denote as ξ ∗ = ( ξ 1 ∗ , … , ξ I ∗ ) \xi^{*}=(\xi_{1}^{*},\ldots,\xi_{I}^{*}) . Then, the limiting price can be computed as (77) p i ∗ = ξ ∗ i / ∑ k = 1 I ξ k ∗ , i = 1 , … , I . \displaystyle p_{i}^{*}=\xi^{*}_{i}\Big/\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}\xi_{k}^{*},~~i=1,\ldots,I. In the market with a random trading sequence, we propose to use the following heuristic weights as an approximation, (78) ν ^ j = 1 a j ( a j ​ w j , 0 1 − γ + b j ) 1 − γ , for j = 1 , 2 , … , J . \displaystyle\hat{\nu}_{j}=\frac{1}{a_{j}}\Big(\frac{a_{j}w_{j,0}}{1-\gamma}+b_{j}\Big)^{1-\gamma},\quad\mbox{ for }j=1,2,\ldots,J. Similar to the CRRA utility-based price formula, we may use the heuristic weight ν ^ j \hat{\nu}_{j} to replace ν j ∗ \nu_{j}^{*} in Eq. ( 76 ). Then, solving these equations gives the solution 𝝃 ^ = ( ξ ^ 1 , … , ξ ^ I ) \hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\hat{\xi}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\xi}_{I}) . The associated approximate limiting price is p ^ i = ξ ^ i / ∑ k = 1 I ξ ^ k \hat{p}_{i}=\hat{\xi}_{i}/\sum_{k=1}^{I}\hat{\xi}_{k} . In an unreported experiment, we find that the above approximation scheme accurately predicts the limiting price. A.7. Trading Decision for Risk-Neutral Traders We consider the case when the market maker adopts the exponential utility (given in ( 10 )) and all the traders are risk neutral. Using the similar notations in Section 4.1 , in t t -th round of trading, the j j -th trader interacts with the market. The market states are 𝐲 t = ( y 1 , t , … , y I , t ) \mathbf{y}_{t}=(y_{1,t},\ldots,y_{I,t}) and j j -the trader’s wealth is 𝐱 j , t = ( x 1 , j , t , … , x I , j , t ) \mathbf{x}_{j,t}=(x_{1,j,t},\ldots,x_{I,j,t}) . The j j -th trader’s decision problem is as follows, max 𝐳 \displaystyle\max_{\mathbf{z}}~~ ∑ i = 1 I π i , j ​ ( x i , j , t + z i ) \displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{I}\pi_{i,j}(x_{i,j,t}+z_{i}) (79) Subject to ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ ( 1 − e − β ⁡ ( y i − z i ) ) ≥ 1 − e − β ​ W 0 . \displaystyle~~~\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}(1-e^{-\beta(y_{i}-z_{i})})\geq 1-e^{-\beta W_{0}}. Checking the optimality condition, we get the optimal solution, z i ∗ = y i − 1 β ln ( λ ​ β ​ θ i π i , j ) , i = 1 , … , I , \displaystyle z_{i}^{*}=y_{i}-\frac{1}{\beta}\ln\left(\frac{\lambda\beta\theta_{i}}{\pi_{i,j}}\right),~~i=1,\ldots,I, where λ ≥ 0 \lambda\geq 0 is the Lagrange multiplier. It can be verified that λ ≠ 0 \lambda\not=0 . Thus the constraint ( 79 ) is binding for z i ∗ z_{i}^{*} , which gives the solution of λ ∗ = 1 β ​ e β ​ W 0 \lambda^{*}=\frac{1}{\beta}e^{\beta W_{0}} . After the trading, the market state is updated to y i , t + 1 = y i , t − z i ∗ = 1 β ​ ln ⁡ ( λ ∗ ​ β ​ θ i π i , j ) y_{i,t+1}=y_{i,t}-z^{*}_{i}=\frac{1}{\beta}\ln\left(\frac{\lambda^{*}\beta\theta_{i}}{\pi_{i,j}}\right) for i = 1 , … , I i=1,\ldots,I . The price is updated to p i , t + 1 = θ i ​ e − β ⁡ ( y i − z i ∗ ) ∑ k = 1 I θ k ​ e − β ⁡ ( y k − z k ∗ ) = π i , j , \displaystyle p_{i,t+1}=\frac{\theta_{i}e^{-\beta(y_{i}-z_{i}^{*})}}{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{I}\theta_{k}e^{-\beta(y_{k}-z_{k}^{*})}}=\pi_{i,j}, for i = 1 , … , I i=1,\ldots,I . That is to say, after the j j -th trader interacts with the market, the price is modified exactly according to the j j -th trader’s belief. Appendix B Properties of MU functions and Examples B.1. Examples of MU Functions One prominent feature of our framework is that it provides more freedom in designing multivariate utility function. The following example shows how various desirable objectives can be made explicit and combined with each other using our MU framework. We then provide some examples of MU functions which go beyond the conventional framework of Expected Utility or the Risk measure -based MU function. Let I I represent the number of securities, ( θ 1 , … , θ I ) ∈ Θ (\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{I})\in\Theta denote the market maker’s belief, and 𝐲 = ( y 1 , … , y I ) \mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{I}) the wealth vector. • We may consider the following MU function, (80) U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) \displaystyle U(\mathbf{y}) = − 1 β ​ ln ⁡ ( ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ e − β ​ y i ) + η ​ ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ ln ⁡ ( y i + B ) , \displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\beta}\ln\left(\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}e^{-\beta y_{i}}\right)+\eta\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}\ln\big(y_{i}+B\big), where η ≥ 0 \eta\geq 0 is the parameter. The first term of U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) U(\mathbf{y}) is the so called Entropic Risk Measure [ Föllmer and Schied 2011 ] and the second term represents the classical expected utility. It’s worth noting that the second term establishes an explicit lower bound of − B -B for the market maker’s terminal wealth. In fact, if an explicit worst-case loss is desired, one can always add a penalty term 1 γ ​ ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ ( y i + B ) γ \frac{1}{\gamma}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}(y_{i}+B)^{\gamma} with γ ≤ 0 \gamma\leq 0 to the original multivariate utility function U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) U(\mathbf{y}) . • We can construct the following MU function, (81) U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) = min i = 1 , … , I ⁡ { y i } + η γ ​ ( ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ y i + B ) γ , \displaystyle U(\mathbf{y})=\min_{i=1,\ldots,I}~\{y_{i}\}+\frac{\eta}{\gamma}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}y_{i}+B\big)^{\gamma}, where η ≥ 0 \eta\geq 0 and γ ≤ 1 \gamma\leq 1 are the paramter. In this MU function, the first term represents the worst-case terminal wealth for the market maker, and it is a concave function because it is the point-wise minimum of 𝐲 \mathbf{y} . The second term establishes a specific lower bound of − B -B for the expectation, ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ y i \sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}y_{i} . It’s important to note that as the parameter γ \gamma approaches 1 1 , the second term approximates the expectation of the wealth (with a shift of B B ). Consequently, a market maker employing this MU function ( 81 ) may seek to strike a balance between minimizing worst-case losses and maximizing expectations. • Using the MU framework, we can not only design the functional form of the MU function but also customize the domain of the MU function. Thus, it is possible to include some “hard constraint” on the market state in MU function. Specifically, consider the function U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) = ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ u ​ ( y i ) U(\mathbf{y})=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}u(y_{i}) where u ⁡ ( ⋅ ) u(\cdot) is an conventional univariate utility function. We then define the domain of this MU function as dom ​ ( U ) ≜ { 𝐲 : 𝐲 ≥ 𝐋 } ∩ { 𝐲 : ρ ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) ≤ ρ 0 } ∩ { 𝐲 : ∑ i = 1 I θ i ​ y i ≥ − B } , \displaystyle\textbf{{dom}}(U)\triangleq\{\mathbf{y}:~\mathbf{y}\geq\mathbf{L}\}\cap\{\mathbf{y}:~\rho(\mathbf{y})\leq\rho_{0}\}\cap\{\mathbf{y}:~\sum_{i=1}^{I}\theta_{i}y_{i}\geq-B\}, where ρ 0 ​ ( ⋅ ) \rho_{0}(\cdot) can be viewed as some risk measure. As for the domain of the MU function, the first term bounds the worst-case loss, the second term bounds the risk, and the last term bounds the expectation of the wealth. B.2. Equivalence Between Various Mechanisms In this section, we explicitly assume the domain of U U is polyhedral: dom ( U ) ≜ { 𝐲 : 𝐚 k ⊤ 𝐲 ≥ b k , 𝐚 k &gt; 𝟎 , k ∈ 𝒦 } \textbf{{dom}}(U)\triangleq\{\mathbf{y}:~\mathbf{a}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{y}\geq b_{k},~\mathbf{a}_{k}&gt;\mathbf{0},~k\in\mathcal{K}\} where 𝒦 \mathcal{K} is some index set. The proof for the case dom ​ ( U ) = ℝ I \textbf{{dom}}(U)=\mathbb{R}^{I} is similar. A scoring rule 𝐒 \mathbf{S} is a reward mechanism that prompts participants to report their subjective probability belief and pays them according to their predicting accuracy. Specifically, if someone reports 𝐩 ∈ Θ I \mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I} , then he/she gets paid 𝐒 ​ ( 𝐩 ) i \mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p})_{i} if outcome i i happens. A scoring rule is proper if truthfully reporting belief is a utility maximizing strategy for a myopic, risk-neutral participant. Additionally, it is strictly proper if truthful reporting is the only utility-maximizing strategy. [ Hanson 2003 ] invented a market implementation for scoring rules by transforming the belief reporting process into a sequential procedure. Following a similar rationale with the Market Equivalence Theorem given by [ Chen and Pennock 2007 ] , the following proposition shows how to retrieve a proper scoring rule from a multivariate utility function. We first introduce the following definition: Definition B.1. Consider a mapping 𝐒 ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \mathbf{S}(\cdot) from a point to a set. If for any 𝐩 n → 𝐩 ∗ \mathbf{p}_{n}\to\mathbf{p}^{*} , 𝐬 n ∈ 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 n ) \mathbf{s}_{n}\in\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p}_{n}) , it has lim n → ∞ 𝐬 n ∈ 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ∗ ) \lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{s}_{n}\in\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p}^{*}) , then we say 𝐒 ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \mathbf{S}(\cdot) is upper-hemicontinuous. Proposition B.2. If U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) : ℝ I → ℝ U(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{R} is a concave function and U U -based pricing rule has bounded loss, for 𝐩 ∈ Θ I \mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I} , we introduce 𝐒 \mathbf{S} as, (82) 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) ≜ − arg ⁡ min \displaystyle\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p})\triangleq-{\arg\min} { 𝐩 ⊤ 𝐲 | U ( 𝐲 ) ≥ U ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) , 𝐲 ∈ dom ( U ) } , \displaystyle~~\Big\{\mathbf{p}^{\top}\mathbf{y}~|~U(\mathbf{y})\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}),~\mathbf{y}\in\textbf{{dom}}(U)\Big\}, where dom ( U ) = { 𝐲 : 𝐚 k ⊤ 𝐲 ≥ b k , 𝐚 k &gt; 𝟎 , k ∈ 𝒦 } \textbf{{dom}}(U)=\{\mathbf{y}:~\mathbf{a}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{y}\geq b_{k},~\mathbf{a}_{k}&gt;\mathbf{0},~k\in\mathcal{K}\} , then 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) \mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p}) is an upper-hemicontinuous correspondence on Θ I \Theta_{I} and also a proper scoring rule. Furthermore, 𝐒 ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \mathbf{S}(\cdot) is continuous within the interior of Θ I \Theta_{I} when U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) is strictly concave. Proof of Proposition B.2 . For convenience, we denote by ℒ \mathcal{L} = = { 𝐲 | U ( 𝐲 ) ≥ U ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) , 𝐚 k ⊤ 𝐲 ≥ b k , k ∈ 𝒦 } \big\{\mathbf{y}~|~U(\mathbf{y})\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}),~\mathbf{a}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{y}\geq b_{k},~k\in\mathcal{K}\big\} the constraint set of ( 82 ). Since the market maker has bounded loss, then there exists a 𝐲 ¯ ∈ ℝ I \underline{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} such that 𝐲 ≥ 𝐲 ¯ , ∀ 𝐲 ∈ ℒ \mathbf{y}\geq\underline{\mathbf{y}},~\forall\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{L} . This suggests that inf 𝐲 ∈ ℒ { 𝐞 i ⊤ ​ 𝐲 } \inf_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{L}}\{\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{y}\} has a solution (possibly a set of extended reals). for all i i . Then for any 𝐩 ∈ Θ I \mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I} we have inf 𝐲 ∈ ℒ { 𝐩 ⊤ ​ 𝐲 } ≥ ∑ i p i ​ inf 𝐲 ∈ ℒ { 𝐞 i ⊤ ​ 𝐲 } \inf_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{L}}\{\mathbf{p}^{\top}\mathbf{y}\}\geq\sum_{i}p_{i}\inf_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{L}}\{\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{y}\} , which implies ( 82 ) has a solution for any 𝐩 ∈ Θ I \mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I} . Specifically, for 𝐩 ∈ interior ​ ( Θ I ) \mathbf{p}\in\textrm{interior}(\Theta_{I}) the intersection of recession cone of 𝐩 ⊤ ​ 𝐲 \mathbf{p}^{\top}\mathbf{y} and ℒ \mathcal{L} is { 𝟎 } \{\mathbf{0}\} , therefore the solution set is compact [ Bertsekas et al. 2003 ] , and the solution set is a unique element when U U is strictly concave, hence 𝐒 : interior ​ ( Θ I ) → ℝ I \mathbf{S}:\textrm{interior}(\Theta_{I})\to\mathbb{R}^{I} is a function. For the upper-hemicontinuity of 𝐒 \mathbf{S} , suppose there exists 𝐩 n → 𝐩 ^ \mathbf{p}_{n}\to\hat{\mathbf{p}} , 𝐬 n ∈ 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 n ) \mathbf{s}_{n}\in\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p}_{n}) such that: lim n → ∞ 𝐬 n = 𝐬 ^ ∉ 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ^ ) \lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{s}_{n}=\hat{\mathbf{s}}\not\in\mathbf{S}(\hat{\mathbf{p}}) . Here we consider extended reals, i.e., some elements of 𝐬 n , 𝐬 ^ \mathbf{s}_{n},\hat{\mathbf{s}} may be + ∞ +\infty . Then we apply the Bolzano-Weistrass theorem on extended reals 𝐬 n \mathbf{s}_{n} to derive a convergence subsequence that converges to some 𝐬 ^ \hat{\mathbf{s}} . By continuity of U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) , it has U ⁡ ( − 𝐬 ^ ) ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) U(-\hat{\mathbf{s}})\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}) and − 𝐚 k ⊤ ​ 𝐬 ^ ≥ b k -\mathbf{a}_{k}^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{s}}\geq b_{k} for all k k . This means 𝐬 ^ \hat{\mathbf{s}} is a feasible solution for ( 82 ) at point 𝐩 ^ \hat{\mathbf{p}} . Due to the optimality of − 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ^ ) -\mathbf{S}(\hat{\mathbf{p}}) at 𝐩 ^ \hat{\mathbf{p}} , there exists an 𝐬 ¯ ∈ 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ^ ) \overline{\mathbf{s}}\in\mathbf{S}(\hat{\mathbf{p}}) such that − 𝐩 ^ ⊤ ​ 𝐬 ¯ &lt; − 𝐩 ^ ⊤ ​ 𝐬 ^ -\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{s}}&lt;-\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{s}} . Again by continuity, monotonicity of U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) and the fact that 𝐚 k &gt; 𝟎 , k ∈ 𝒦 \mathbf{a}_{k}&gt;\mathbf{0},~k\in\mathcal{K} , we can subtract a small ϵ ​ 𝐞 \epsilon\mathbf{e} from 𝐬 ¯ \overline{\mathbf{s}} to get another 𝐬 ~ \tilde{\mathbf{s}} such that, { − 𝐩 ^ ⊤ ​ 𝐬 ~ &lt; − 𝐩 ^ ⊤ ​ 𝐬 ^ , U ⁡ ( − 𝐬 ~ ) &gt; U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) , − 𝐚 k ⊤ 𝐬 ~ &gt; b k , k ∈ 𝒦 . \displaystyle\begin{cases}-\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}&lt;-\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{\top}\hat{\mathbf{s}},\\ U(-\tilde{\mathbf{s}})&gt;U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}),\\ -\mathbf{a}_{k}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}&gt;b_{k},~~k\in\mathcal{K}.\end{cases} Since we have 𝐩 n → 𝐩 ^ \mathbf{p}_{n}\to\hat{\mathbf{p}} , 𝐬 n → 𝐬 ^ \mathbf{s}_{n}\to\hat{\mathbf{s}} , there exists an N N large enough such that the following system of inequalities hold, { − 𝐩 N ⊤ ​ 𝐬 ~ &lt; − 𝐩 N ⊤ ​ 𝐬 N , U ⁡ ( − 𝐬 ~ ) &gt; U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) , − 𝐚 k ⊤ 𝐬 ~ &gt; b k , k ∈ 𝒦 . \displaystyle\begin{cases}-\mathbf{p}_{N}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}&lt;-\mathbf{p}_{N}^{\top}\mathbf{s}_{N},\\ U(-\tilde{\mathbf{s}})&gt;U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}),\\ -\mathbf{a}_{k}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}&gt;b_{k},~~k\in\mathcal{K}.\end{cases} Such a fact means 𝐬 ~ \tilde{\mathbf{s}} is a feasible solution of ( 82 ) at 𝐩 N \mathbf{p}_{N} , and its corresponding objective value is smaller than 𝐬 N \mathbf{s}_{N} which is a contradiction. Thus, when 𝐩 n → 𝐩 ^ \mathbf{p}_{n}\to\hat{\mathbf{p}} , 𝐬 n ∈ 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 n ) \mathbf{s}_{n}\in\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p}_{n}) implies lim n → ∞ 𝐬 n = 𝐬 ^ ∈ 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ^ ) \lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{s}_{n}=\hat{\mathbf{s}}\in\mathbf{S}(\hat{\mathbf{p}}) , i.e., 𝐒 ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \mathbf{S}(\cdot) is upper-hemicontinuous. Furthermore, upper-hemicontinuity is equivalent with continuity when 𝐒 ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \mathbf{S}(\cdot) is a function, therefore 𝐒 \mathbf{S} is continuous on interior ​ ( Θ I ) \textrm{interior}(\Theta_{I}) for a strictly concave MU function U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) . As for properness, when a risk-neutral trader is facing a scoring rule 𝐒 \mathbf{S} , if we assume his/her true probability estimate is 𝐩 \mathbf{p} , then his/her decision problem can be expressed as, (83) max 𝐫 ∈ Θ I \displaystyle\max_{\mathbf{r}\in\Theta_{I}} 𝐩 ⊤ ​ 𝐬 \displaystyle\mathbf{p}^{\top}\mathbf{s} Subject to \displaystyle\textrm{Subject to} 𝐬 ∈ 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐫 ) , \displaystyle\mathbf{s}\in\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{r}), 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐫 ) = − arg ⁡ max 𝐲 ∈ ℒ − 𝐫 ⊤ ​ 𝐲 , \displaystyle\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{r})=-\underset{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{L}}{\arg\max}~-\mathbf{r}^{\top}\mathbf{y}, where ℒ = { 𝐲 : U ( 𝐲 ) ≥ U ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) , 𝐚 k ⊤ 𝐲 ≥ b k , ∀ k } \mathcal{L}=\big\{\mathbf{y}:~U(\mathbf{y})\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}),~~\mathbf{a}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{y}\geq b_{k},~\forall k\big\} . From the structure of such problem, it has 𝐩 ⊤ ​ 𝐬 1 ≥ 𝐩 ⊤ ​ 𝐬 2 , ∀ 𝐫 ≠ 𝐩 , 𝐬 1 ∈ 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) , 𝐬 2 ∈ 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐫 ) , \displaystyle\mathbf{p}^{\top}\mathbf{s}_{1}\geq\mathbf{p}^{\top}\mathbf{s}_{2},\quad\forall\mathbf{r}\neq\mathbf{p},\mathbf{s}_{1}\in\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{s}_{2}\in\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{r}), which means truthfully reporting 𝐩 \mathbf{p} is a utility-maximizing strategy. □ \hfill\Box Remark 4. From the above discussion one can also easily verify that the MU-induced scoring rule is strictly proper within the interior of Θ I \Theta_{I} when the underlying utility is strictly concave. The next proposition shows how to induce an MU with bounded loss from a proper scoring rule. Proposition B.3. Let 𝐒 : Θ I → R \mathbf{S}:\Theta_{I}\to R be a proper scoring rule and 𝐒 \mathbf{S} is continuous on Θ I \Theta_{I} , let ℒ ≜ \displaystyle\mathcal{L}\triangleq − ∩ 𝐩 ∈ Θ I ​ { 𝐬 : 𝐩 ⊤ ​ 𝐬 ≤ 𝐩 ⊤ ​ 𝐒 ​ ( 𝐩 ) } \displaystyle-\underset{\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}}{\cap}\big\{\mathbf{s}:~\mathbf{p}^{\top}\mathbf{s}\leq\mathbf{p}^{\top}\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p})\big\} 𝒟 ≜ \displaystyle\mathcal{D}\triangleq { 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) : 𝐩 ∈ Θ I } \displaystyle~~~\big\{\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p}):~\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}\big\} then ℒ \mathcal{L} is a closed and convex set and − 𝒟 -\mathcal{D} lie on the boundary of ℒ \mathcal{L} . Define (84) U ( 𝐲 ) ≜ − inf { m : 𝐲 + m ⋅ 𝐞 ∈ ℒ } = inf 𝐩 ∈ Θ I { 𝐩 ⊤ ( 𝐒 ( 𝐩 ) + 𝐲 ) } \displaystyle U(\mathbf{y})\triangleq-\inf\{m:~\mathbf{y}+m\cdot\mathbf{e}\in\mathcal{L}\}=\underset{\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}}{\inf}~\big\{\mathbf{p}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p})+\mathbf{y}\big)\big\} then U : ℝ I → ℝ U:\mathbb{R}^{I}\to\mathbb{R} is a weakly monotone 17 17 17 A multivariate utility U U is said to be weakly monotone if 𝐲 ≥ 𝐲 ^ ⟹ U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) ≥ U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ^ ) \mathbf{y}\geq\hat{\mathbf{y}}\implies U(\mathbf{y})\geq U(\hat{\mathbf{y}}) , y i &gt; y ^ i , ∀ i ⟹ U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) &gt; U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ^ ) y_{i}&gt;\hat{y}_{i},\forall i\implies U(\mathbf{y})&gt;U(\hat{\mathbf{y}}) and for any i i and any 𝐲 ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} there exists some β &gt; 0 \beta&gt;0 such that U ⁡ ( 𝐲 − β ​ 𝐞 i ) &lt; U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) U(\mathbf{y}-\beta\mathbf{e}_{i})&lt;U(\mathbf{y}) . multivariate utility function, while the U U -based MU suffers bounded loss and satisfies (85) − 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐫 ) ∈ arg ⁡ min 𝐲 ​ { 𝐫 ⊤ ​ 𝐲 | U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) ≥ 0 } . \displaystyle-\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{r})\in\underset{\mathbf{y}}{\arg\min}~\big\{~\mathbf{r}^{\top}\mathbf{y}~|~U(\mathbf{y})\geq 0\big\}. Proof of Proposition B.3 . Clearly, { 𝐬 | 𝐩 ⊤ ​ 𝐬 ≤ 𝐩 ⊤ ​ 𝐒 ​ ( 𝐩 ) } \big\{\mathbf{s}~|~\mathbf{p}^{\top}\mathbf{s}\leq\mathbf{p}^{\top}\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p})\big\} defines a closed halfspace for all 𝐩 ∈ Θ I \mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I} . Since closed halfspace is natively convex, ℒ \mathcal{L} can be regarded as the intersection of closed convex sets. Thus, it is also closed and convex. Also, for 𝐩 ∈ Θ I \mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I} , it is easy to see that − 𝒟 -\mathcal{D} lies on the boundary of ℒ \mathcal{L} . Note that ( 84 ) can be expressed as, U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) ≜ − min m \displaystyle U(\mathbf{y})\triangleq-\min_{m} { m | − 𝐩 ⊤ ( 𝐲 + m ⋅ 𝐞 ) ≤ 𝐩 ⊤ 𝐒 ( 𝐩 ) , ∀ 𝐩 ∈ Θ I } . \displaystyle~~~\big\{~m~|~-\mathbf{p}^{\top}(\mathbf{y}+m\cdot\mathbf{e})\leq\mathbf{p}^{\top}\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p}),\quad\forall\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}\big\}. Therefore MU function U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) U(\mathbf{y}) is equivalent to (86) U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) = − sup 𝐩 ∈ Θ I ​ { − 𝐩 ⊤ ​ ( 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) + 𝐲 ) } = inf 𝐩 ∈ Θ I ​ { 𝐩 ⊤ ​ ( 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) + 𝐲 ) } . \displaystyle U(\mathbf{y})=-\underset{\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}}{\sup}~\big\{-\mathbf{p}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p})+\mathbf{y}\big)\big\}=\underset{\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}}{\inf}~\big\{\mathbf{p}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p})+\mathbf{y}\big)\big\}. At last, we check whether U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) meets Assumption 1 . • Domain condition: Since 𝐒 ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \mathbf{S}(\cdot) is continuous on Θ I \Theta_{I} , U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) is actually defined as the negative of minimum value of a continuous function over a closed and compact set, then Weistrass Theorem says such minimum value is attainable and well-defined, therefore U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) is well defined on whole ℝ I \mathbb{R}^{I} . • Continuity condition: Suppose 𝐲 n → 𝐲 ¯ \mathbf{y}_{n}\to\overline{\mathbf{y}} but U ⁡ ( 𝐲 n ) U(\mathbf{y}_{n}) does not approach U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ¯ ) U(\overline{\mathbf{y}}) . Then, it must have one of the following cases: (1) There exists a δ &gt; 0 \delta&gt;0 such that ∃ N &gt; Z \exists N&gt;Z , U ⁡ ( 𝐲 N ) &gt; U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ¯ ) + δ , ∀ Z &gt; 0 U(\mathbf{y}_{N})&gt;U(\overline{\mathbf{y}})+\delta,~~\forall Z&gt;0 ; (2) There exists a δ &gt; 0 \delta&gt;0 such that ∃ N &gt; Z , U ⁡ ( 𝐲 N ) + δ &lt; U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ¯ ) \exists N&gt;Z,~U(\mathbf{y}_{N})+\delta&lt;U(\overline{\mathbf{y}}) , ∀ Z &gt; 0 \forall Z&gt;0 . Let 𝐩 N ∗ ∈ arg ⁡ min 𝐩 ∈ Θ I ​ { 𝐩 ⊤ ​ ( 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) + 𝐲 N ) } \mathbf{p}_{N}^{*}\in\underset{\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}}{\arg\min}~\big\{\mathbf{p}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p})+\mathbf{y}_{N}\big)\big\} and 𝐩 ¯ ∗ ∈ arg ⁡ min 𝐩 ∈ Θ I ​ { 𝐩 ⊤ ​ ( 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) + 𝐲 ¯ ) } \overline{\mathbf{p}}^{*}\in\underset{\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}}{\arg\min}~\big\{\mathbf{p}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p})+\overline{\mathbf{y}}\big)\big\} . As for case (2), there also exists an N N sufficiently large such that, U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ¯ ) = 𝐩 ¯ ∗ ⊤ ​ ( 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ¯ ∗ ) + 𝐲 ¯ ) &gt; 𝐩 N ∗ ⊤ ​ ( 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 N ∗ ) + 𝐲 ¯ ) \displaystyle U(\overline{\mathbf{y}})={\overline{\mathbf{p}}^{*}}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{S}(\overline{\mathbf{p}}^{*})+\overline{\mathbf{y}}\big)&gt;{\mathbf{p}_{N}^{*}}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p}_{N}^{*})+\overline{\mathbf{y}}\big) which is contradictory to the optimality of 𝐩 ¯ ∗ \overline{\mathbf{p}}^{*} . Therefore, it must have 𝐲 n → 𝐲 ¯ ⟹ U ⁡ ( 𝐲 n ) → U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ¯ ) \mathbf{y}_{n}\to\overline{\mathbf{y}}\implies U(\mathbf{y}_{n})\to U(\overline{\mathbf{y}}) . • Bounded loss condition: Since Θ I \Theta_{I} is a closed and bounded set while 𝐒 \mathbf{S} is continuous on Θ I \Theta_{I} , therefore, 𝒟 = { 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) : 𝐩 ∈ Θ I } \mathcal{D}=\big\{\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p}):~\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}\big\} is also a closed and bounded set. By the structure of U U we know U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) ≥ 0 ⇔ 𝐲 ∈ ℒ U(\mathbf{y})\geq 0\iff\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{L} . Since 𝒟 \mathcal{D} is bounded, the left-hand side of the above inequality is also bounded, therefore the worst-case loss of the market maker is bounded. • Concavity for 𝐲 1 , 𝐲 2 ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} : Note that the following inequality holds, U ⁡ ( α ​ 𝐲 1 + ( 1 − α ) ​ 𝐲 2 ) \displaystyle U\big(\alpha\mathbf{y}_{1}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{y}_{2}\big) = inf 𝐩 ∈ Θ I ​ { 𝐩 ⊤ ​ ( 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) + α ​ 𝐲 1 + ( 1 − α ) ​ 𝐲 2 ) } \displaystyle=\underset{\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}}{\inf}~\big\{\mathbf{p}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p})+\alpha\mathbf{y}_{1}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{y}_{2}\big)\big\} ≥ α ​ inf 𝐩 ∈ Θ I ​ { 𝐩 ⊤ ​ ( 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) + 𝐲 1 ) } + ( 1 − α ) ​ inf 𝐩 ∈ Θ I ​ { 𝐩 ⊤ ​ ( 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) + 𝐲 2 ) } \displaystyle\geq\alpha\underset{\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}}{\inf}~\big\{\mathbf{p}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p})+\mathbf{y}_{1}\big)\big\}+(1-\alpha)\underset{\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}}{\inf}~\big\{\mathbf{p}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p})+\mathbf{y}_{2}\big)\big\} = α ​ U ​ ( 𝐲 1 ) + ( 1 − α ) ​ U ​ ( 𝐲 2 ) . \displaystyle=\alpha U(\mathbf{y}_{1})+(1-\alpha)U(\mathbf{y}_{2}). • Weak monotonicity: The following inequalities holds true, U ⁡ ( 𝐲 + 𝜹 ) \displaystyle U(\mathbf{y}+\bm{\delta}) = inf 𝐩 ∈ Θ I ​ { 𝐩 ⊤ ​ ( 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) + 𝐲 + 𝜹 ) } ≥ inf 𝐩 ∈ Θ I ​ { 𝐩 ⊤ ​ ( 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) + 𝐲 ) } = U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) , ∀ 𝜹 ≥ 𝟎 , \displaystyle=\underset{\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}}{\inf}~\big\{\mathbf{p}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p})+\mathbf{y}+\bm{\delta}\big)\big\}\geq\underset{\mathbf{p}\in\Theta_{I}}{\inf}~\big\{\mathbf{p}^{\top}\big(\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{p})+\mathbf{y}\big)\big\}=U(\mathbf{y}),~~\forall\bm{\delta}\geq\mathbf{0}, U ⁡ ( 𝐲 + 𝜹 ) \displaystyle U(\mathbf{y}+\bm{\delta}) = inf 𝐩 ∈ Θ I ​ { 𝐩 ⊤ ​ ( 𝐒 ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) + 𝐲 + 𝜹 ) } &gt; Cost function-based mechanism is a prediction market making method proposed by [ Chen and Pennock 2007 ] . It is now one of the most widely-used market making mechanism in prediction market literature due to its implementation simplicity. A cost function C : ℝ I → ℝ C:\mathbb{R}^{I}\to\mathbb{R} should satisfy the following properties: • C ⁡ ( ⋅ ) C(\cdot) is continuous and defined on whole ℝ I \mathbb{R}^{I} ; • Convexity: C ⁡ ( α ​ 𝐪 1 + ( 1 − α ) ​ 𝐪 2 ) ≤ α ​ C ​ ( 𝐪 1 ) + ( 1 − α ) ​ C ​ ( 𝐪 2 ) C(\alpha\mathbf{q}_{1}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{q}_{2})\leq\alpha C(\mathbf{q}_{1})+(1-\alpha)C(\mathbf{q}_{2}) ; • Monotonicity: for any 𝐪 , 𝐪 ^ \mathbf{q},\hat{\mathbf{q}} , if 𝐪 ≥ 𝐪 ^ \mathbf{q}\geq\hat{\mathbf{q}} , it has C ⁡ ( 𝐪 ) ≥ C ⁡ ( 𝐪 ^ ) C(\mathbf{q})\geq C(\hat{\mathbf{q}}) and the inequality is held strictly if 𝐪 &gt; 𝐪 ^ \mathbf{q}&gt;\hat{\mathbf{q}} ; • Translation invariance: C ⁡ ( 𝐪 + t ⋅ 𝐞 ) = C ⁡ ( 𝐪 ) + t , ∀ t ∈ ℝ C(\mathbf{q}+t\cdot\mathbf{e})=C(\mathbf{q})+t,~\forall t\in\mathbb{R} . Then given current outstanding securities 𝐪 \mathbf{q} , a new order Δ ​ 𝐪 \Delta\mathbf{q} is charged with C ⁡ ( 𝐪 + Δ ​ 𝐪 ) − C ⁡ ( 𝐪 ) C(\mathbf{q}+\Delta\mathbf{q})-C(\mathbf{q}) . One can see that a ρ ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) ≜ C ⁡ ( − 𝐲 ) \rho(\mathbf{y})\triangleq C(-\mathbf{y}) defines a risk measure, therefore V ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) ≜ − C ⁡ ( − 𝐲 ) V(\mathbf{y})\triangleq-C(-\mathbf{y}) is always a multivariate utility function. The following proposition formally establishes their equivalence. Proposition B.4. For any given 𝐪 ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} any multivariate-utility U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) , let C U ​ ( 𝐪 ) ≜ min \displaystyle C_{U}(\mathbf{q})\triangleq\min W \displaystyle~~W s . t . \displaystyle s.t. U ⁡ ( W ⋅ 𝐞 − 𝐪 ) ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) , \displaystyle~~U(W\cdot\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{q})\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}), W ⋅ 𝐞 − 𝐪 ∈ dom ​ ( U ) , \displaystyle~~W\cdot\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{q}\in\textbf{{dom}}(U), then C U ​ ( ⋅ ) C_{U}(\cdot) is a well-defined cost function, and C U ​ ( 𝐪 t + Δ ​ 𝐪 ) − C U ​ ( 𝐪 t ) C_{U}(\mathbf{q}_{t}+\Delta\mathbf{q})-C_{U}(\mathbf{q}_{t}) is exactly the price charge of Δ ​ 𝐪 \Delta\mathbf{q} under U U -based MU when outstanding securities are 𝐪 t \mathbf{q}_{t} . Conversely, for any cost function C ⁡ ( ⋅ ) C(\cdot) , let (87) U C ​ ( 𝐲 ) = − C ⁡ ( − 𝐲 ) , \displaystyle U_{C}(\mathbf{y})=-C(-\mathbf{y}), then U C ​ ( 𝐲 ) U_{C}(\mathbf{y}) is a qualified multivariate utility as per Assumption 1 . Furthermore, denote by W t W_{t} the amount of cash the cost function-based market maker collected by time t t , then such a cost function-based market maker is equivalent to U C ​ ( W t ⋅ 𝐞 − 𝐪 t ) = U C ​ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) U_{C}(W_{t}\cdot\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{q}_{t})=U_{C}(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}) . Proof of Proposition B.4 . MU → \rightarrow Cost function: That C U C_{U} is a function follows directly from uniqueness of solution of 𝒫 Cmin \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{Cmin}} ; the translation invariance property is obvious. We now focus on the continuity and convexity of C U C_{U} : • Continuity: Suppose there exists a sequence 𝐪 n → 𝐪 ¯ \mathbf{q}_{n}\to\overline{\mathbf{q}} such that lim n → ∞ C U ​ ( 𝐪 n ) = C ^ ≠ C U ​ ( 𝐪 ¯ ) \lim_{n\to\infty}C_{U}(\mathbf{q}_{n})=\hat{C}\neq C_{U}(\overline{\mathbf{q}}) ( C ^ \hat{C} may be + ∞ +\infty ). Now we can achieve a smaller objective value by slightly reducing C U ​ ( 𝐪 N ) C_{U}(\mathbf{q}_{N}) , which contradicts with the optimality of C U ​ ( 𝐪 N ) C_{U}(\mathbf{q}_{N}) . Conversely, if C ^ &lt; C U ​ ( 𝐪 ¯ ) \hat{C}&lt;C_{U}(\overline{\mathbf{q}}) , then by continuity of U U we have U ⁡ ( C ^ ⋅ 𝐞 − 𝐪 ¯ ) ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) U(\hat{C}\cdot\mathbf{e}-\overline{\mathbf{q}})\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}) , 𝐚 k ⊤ ​ ( C ^ ⋅ 𝐞 − 𝐪 ¯ ) ≥ b k , ∀ k \mathbf{a}_{k}^{\top}(\hat{C}\cdot\mathbf{e}-\overline{\mathbf{q}})\geq b_{k},~\forall k , which again contradicts with the optimality of C U ​ ( 𝐪 ¯ ) C_{U}(\overline{\mathbf{q}}) . Therefore 𝐪 n → 𝐪 ¯ ⟹ C U ​ ( 𝐪 n ) → C U ​ ( 𝐪 ¯ ) \mathbf{q}_{n}\to\overline{\mathbf{q}}\implies C_{U}(\mathbf{q}_{n})\to C_{U}(\overline{\mathbf{q}}) , that is to say C U C_{U} is continuous. • Convexity: By the concavity of U U and the convexity of dom ​ ( U ) \textbf{{dom}}(U) we know that for any 𝐪 1 , 𝐪 2 \mathbf{q}_{1},\mathbf{q}_{2} : U ⁡ ( ( α ​ C U ​ ( 𝐪 1 ) + ( 1 − α ) ​ C U ​ ( 𝐪 2 ) ) ⋅ 𝐞 − ( α ​ 𝐪 1 + ( 1 − α ) ​ 𝐪 2 ) ) \displaystyle U\Big(\big(\alpha C_{U}(\mathbf{q}_{1})+(1-\alpha)C_{U}(\mathbf{q}_{2})\big)\cdot\mathbf{e}-\big(\alpha\mathbf{q}_{1}+(1-\alpha)\mathbf{q}_{2}\big)\Big) = U ⁡ ( α ⁡ ( C U ​ ( 𝐪 1 ) ​ 𝐞 − 𝐪 1 ) + ( 1 − α ) ​ ( C U ​ ( 𝐪 2 ) ​ 𝐞 − 𝐪 2 ) ) \displaystyle=U\Big(\alpha\big(C_{U}(\mathbf{q}_{1})\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{q}_{1}\big)+(1-\alpha)\big(C_{U}(\mathbf{q}_{2})\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{q}_{2}\big)\Big) ≥ α ​ U ​ ( C U ​ ( 𝐪 1 ) ​ 𝐞 − 𝐪 1 ) + ( 1 − α ) ​ U ​ ( C U ​ ( 𝐪 2 ) ​ 𝐞 − 𝐪 2 ) \displaystyle\geq\alpha U\Big(C_{U}(\mathbf{q}_{1})\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{q}_{1}\Big)+(1-\alpha)U\Big(C_{U}(\mathbf{q}_{2})\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{q}_{2}\Big) ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) . \displaystyle\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}). The price equivalence between C U C_{U} and ( 87 ) is obvious. □ \square Cost function → \rightarrow MU: Clearly U C U_{C} inherits concavity, continuity and monotonicity from C C , therefore is a qualified risk preference function per Assumption 1 . On the other hand, assume the cost function-based market maker has W t W_{t} cash by time t t , while the aggregate outstanding securities are 𝐪 t \mathbf{q}_{t} then the price charge for an incoming new order is Δ ​ W t = C ⁡ ( 𝐪 t + Δ ​ 𝐪 t ) − C ⁡ ( 𝐪 t ) \Delta W_{t}=C(\mathbf{q}_{t}+\Delta\mathbf{q}_{t})-C(\mathbf{q}_{t}) . Additional Properties of MU Function • No-arbitrage for Surebet : Under Assumption 1 , the market making mechanism induced by multivariate utility function U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) is well defined and has the following properties. Proposition B.5. If the market maker adopts MU function U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) to price any order Δ ​ 𝐪 \Delta\mathbf{q} by solving problem 𝒫 cmin ​ ( Δ ​ 𝐪 ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{cmin}}(\Delta\mathbf{q}) , it has following properties: (i): It can appropriately price any finite order Δ ​ 𝐪 \Delta\mathbf{q} with finite price Δ ​ w ∗ \Delta w^{*} and the price Δ ​ w ∗ \Delta w^{*} is unique; (ii): The k ∈ ℝ k\in\mathbb{R} units of surebet order Δ ​ 𝐪 = k ⋅ 𝐞 \Delta\mathbf{q}=k\cdot\mathbf{e} is always charged with k k dollars. 18 18 18 As all outcomes of the prediction market are mutually exclusive and exhaustive, the surebet means that the order covers all the states. The surebet order is in form Δ ​ q = k ⋅ 𝐞 \Delta q=k\cdot\mathbf{e} for some k k . Proof. Proof. Given market state ( 𝐪 , w ) (\mathbf{q},w) and incoming order { Δ ​ 𝐪 } \{\Delta\mathbf{q}\} , we denote the constraint set of 𝒫 cmin \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{cmin}} at this state by 𝒞 ⁡ ( w , 𝐪 , Δ ​ 𝐪 ) \mathcal{C}(w,\mathbf{q},\Delta\mathbf{q}) . By the structure of the problem it is not hard to see that the only common direction of recession of set 𝒞 ⁡ ( w , 𝐪 , Δ ​ 𝐪 ) \mathcal{C}(w,\mathbf{q},\Delta\mathbf{q}) and objective function is 𝟎 \mathbf{0} , therefore 𝒫 cmin \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{cmin}} admits a solution for any { 𝐪 , w } \{\mathbf{q},w\} and Δ ​ 𝐪 \Delta\mathbf{q} , and the solution is clearly unique. As for the price of surebet Δ ​ q = k ​ 𝐞 \Delta q=k\mathbf{e} , we consider two cases. In the first case, the market state { 𝐪 , w } \{\mathbf{q},w\} resulted from previous trading satisfies U ⁡ ( w ⋅ 𝐞 − 𝐪 ) = U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) U(w\cdot\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{q})=U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}) . If we solve problem 𝒫 cmin \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{cmin}} for the surebet order k ⋅ 𝐞 k\cdot\mathbf{e} in current round of trading, the constraint becomes U ⁡ ( ( w + Δ ​ w ) ⋅ 𝐞 − ( 𝐪 + k ⋅ 𝐞 ) ) ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) U((w+\Delta w)\cdot\mathbf{e}-(\mathbf{q}+k\cdot\mathbf{e}))\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}) , which implies Δ ​ w ∗ = k \Delta w^{*}=k . In the second case, if the previous trading gives U ⁡ ( w ⋅ 𝐞 − 𝐪 ) &gt; U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) U(w\cdot\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{q})&gt;U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}) , we may still have the solution Δ ​ w ∗ = k \Delta w^{*}=k for the order k ⋅ 𝐞 k\cdot\mathbf{e} . Otherwise, the previous trading may not achieve its optimal price. □ \square • Bounded Worst-case Loss : Although a market maker is designed to provide liquidity, it is certainly not designed to be infinitely exploited. To avoid such catastrophe, one might wish to put a cap on the worst-case loss. Obviously MU function based market maker suffers bounded worst-case loss if and only if the utility level set ℒ ≜ { 𝐲 ∈ ℝ I | U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) } \mathcal{L}\triangleq\{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{I}~|~U(\mathbf{y})\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e})\} is lower bounded by some vector 𝐲 ¯ ∈ ℝ I \underline{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{I} , i.e., it has 𝐲 ¯ ≤ 𝐲 \underline{\mathbf{y}}\leq\mathbf{y} for all 𝐲 ∈ ℒ \mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{L} . The utility level set ℒ \mathcal{L} actually describes all the acceptable terminal wealth 𝐲 \mathbf{y} of the market maker. Indeed, each element of 𝐲 ¯ = ( y ¯ 1 , y ¯ 2 , … , y ¯ n ) \underline{\mathbf{y}}=\big(\underline{y}_{1},\underline{y}_{2},\ldots,\underline{y}_{n}\big) can be characterized as follows, (88) 𝒫 i \displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{i}~ min 𝐲 { y i | 𝐲 = ( y 1 , ⋯ , y I ) , U ( 𝐲 ) ≥ U ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) } . \displaystyle~\min_{\mathbf{y}}\{y_{i}~|~\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\cdots,y_{I}),~U(\mathbf{y})\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e})\}. Worst-case loss bound can be found by solving subproblems 𝒫 i \mathcal{P}_{i} for all i = 1 , ⋯ , I i=1,\cdots,I . Obviously, the worst-case loss has a natural bound when the domain of utility U U itself is bounded from below, e.g., U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) = ∑ i π i ​ ( y i + B i ) γ , γ &lt; 1 U(\mathbf{y})=\sum_{i}\pi_{i}(y_{i}+B_{i})^{\gamma},~\gamma&lt;1 . If such a domain bound design is not desired, then we need to find to other means to bound the loss. One viable way is to resort to transaction restrictions. Specifically, in real world traders are usually constrained to have non-negative wealth position when true state is revealed (It is known as the no-bankruptcy restriction ). Under this case, bounded loss can be easily guaranteed. Besides the no-bankruptcy restriction , we provide the following results for the bounded worst-case loss. Proposition B.6. If multivariate utility function U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) satisfies Assumption 1 and sup 𝐲 U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) = B &lt; + ∞ \sup_{\mathbf{y}}U(\mathbf{y})=B&lt;+\infty with (89) lim t → ∞ U ( t ( 𝐞 − 𝐞 i ) ) &lt; B , for i = 1 , ⋯ , I , \displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}U(t(\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{e}_{i}))&lt;B,~~\textrm{for}~i=1,\cdots,I, then the MU-function based market maker has bounded loss. Proof. The multivariate utility function is bounded above means that, there exist some finite number B B , such that U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ) &lt; B U(\mathbf{y})&lt;B for any 𝐲 ∈ dom ​ ( U ) \mathbf{y}\in\textbf{{dom}}(U) . Under the condition given in this proposition, we assume the market maker has unbounded loss. Without loss of generality, we assume the first element of 𝐲 \mathbf{y} may go to − ∞ -\infty and y ∈ ℒ y\in\mathcal{L} . This assumption is equivalent to say there exists vector 𝐲 ⁡ ( τ ) ≜ ( − τ , g ⁡ ( τ ) , g ⁡ ( τ ) , ⋯ , g ⁡ ( τ ) ) ∈ ℝ I \mathbf{y}(\tau)\triangleq(-\tau,g(\tau),g(\tau),\cdots,g(\tau))\in\mathbb{R}^{I} with 𝐲 ⁡ ( τ ) ∈ ℒ \mathbf{y}(\tau)\in\mathcal{L} when τ → ∞ \tau\rightarrow\infty , where τ &gt; 0 \tau&gt;0 and g ⁡ ( τ ) g(\tau) is any function satisfying g ⁡ ( τ ) &gt; τ g(\tau)&gt;\tau . Clearly, it has 𝐲 ⁡ ( τ ) = g ⁡ ( τ ) ​ ( 𝐞 − 𝐞 i ) − τ ​ 𝐞 i \mathbf{y}(\tau)=g(\tau)(\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{e}_{i})-\tau\mathbf{e}_{i} . Since U ⁡ ( ⋅ ) U(\cdot) is a concave function, it has (90) lim τ → ∞ U ( g ( τ ) ( 𝐞 − 𝐞 i ) − τ 𝐞 i ) ≤ lim τ → ∞ ( U ( g ( τ ) ( 𝐞 − 𝐞 i ) ) − τ ∇ U ( g ( τ ) ( 𝐞 − 𝐞 i ) ) ⊤ 𝐞 i ) \displaystyle\lim_{\tau\rightarrow\infty}U\big(g(\tau)(\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{e}_{i})-\tau\mathbf{e}_{i}\big)\leq\lim_{\tau\rightarrow\infty}\Big(U\big(g(\tau)(\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{e}_{i})\big)-\tau\nabla U(g(\tau)(\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{e}_{i}))^{\top}\mathbf{e}_{i}\Big) (91) lim τ → ∞ U ( g ( τ ) ( 𝐞 − 𝐞 i ) + t 𝐞 i ) ≤ lim τ → ∞ ( U ( g ( τ ) ( 𝐞 − 𝐞 i ) ) + τ ∇ U ( g ( τ ) ( 𝐞 − 𝐞 i ) ) ⊤ 𝐞 i ) \displaystyle\lim_{\tau\rightarrow\infty}U\big(g(\tau)(\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{e}_{i})+\sqrt{t}\mathbf{e}_{i}\big)\leq\lim_{\tau\rightarrow\infty}\Big(U(g(\tau)(\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{e}_{i}))+\sqrt{\tau}\nabla U\big(g(\tau)(\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{e}_{i})\big)^{\top}\mathbf{e}_{i}\Big) The left-hand side of inequality ( 91 ) tends to the infimum B B . According to ( 89 ), it has lim τ → ∞ U ⁡ ( g ⁡ ( τ ) ​ ( 𝐞 − 𝐞 i ) ) &lt; B \lim_{\tau\rightarrow\infty}U(g(\tau)(\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{e}_{i}))&lt;B . Thus, there exists some δ &gt; 0 \delta&gt;0 satisfying δ = B − lim τ → ∞ U ⁡ ( g ⁡ ( τ ) ​ ( 𝐞 − 𝐞 i ) ) &gt; 0 \delta=B-\lim\limits_{\tau\rightarrow\infty}U(g(\tau)(\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{e}_{i}))&gt;0 . Also, inequality ( 91 ) becomes (92) lim t → ∞ t ⋅ ∇ U ( g ( t ) ( 𝐞 − 𝐞 i ) ) ⊤ 𝐞 i ≥ δ \displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\sqrt{t}\cdot\nabla U\big(g(t)(\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{e}_{i})\big)^{\top}\mathbf{e}_{i}\geq\delta Substituting the above inequality in ( 90 ) and noticing τ = τ ​ τ \tau=\sqrt{\tau}\sqrt{\tau} , it has lim t → ∞ U ⁡ ( g ⁡ ( τ ) ​ ( 𝐞 − 𝐞 i ) − τ ​ 𝐞 i ) \displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}U\big(g(\tau)(\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{e}_{i})-\tau\mathbf{e}_{i}\big) ≤ lim τ → ∞ ( F ⁡ ( g ⁡ ( τ ) ​ ( 𝐞 − 𝐞 i ) ) − τ ​ δ ) \displaystyle\leq\lim_{\tau\rightarrow\infty}\Big(F\big(g(\tau)(\mathbf{e}-\mathbf{e}_{i})\big)-\sqrt{\tau}\delta\Big) (93) &lt; B − lim τ → ∞ τ ​ δ = − ∞ . \displaystyle&lt;B-\lim_{\tau\rightarrow\infty}\sqrt{\tau}\delta=-\infty. That is to say, the utility of U ⁡ ( 𝐲 ⁡ ( τ ) ) U(\mathbf{y}(\tau)) is infinitely low, thus can not be maintained above U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}) . It contradicts our previous statement 𝐲 ⁡ ( τ ) ∈ ℒ \mathbf{y}(\tau)\in\mathcal{L} , which also implies our assumption that ℒ \mathcal{L} has no lower bound is not true. □ \square • Acceptance of Limit Order: The MU-function based pricing rule can be easily modified to accept the limit order. We refer to 𝐩 \mathbf{p} as the ‘instantaneous’ price since the actual price charge for any non-trivial order Δ ​ 𝐪 \Delta\mathbf{q} is not 𝐩 ⊤ ​ Δ ​ 𝐪 \mathbf{p}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{q} but rather the amount Δ ​ w ∗ \Delta w^{*} calculated by solving problem 𝒫 cmin ​ ( Δ ​ 𝐪 ) \mathcal{P}_{\textrm{cmin}}(\Delta\mathbf{q}) . This difference is due to the fact that the instantaneous price varies continuously along with the state variable 𝐲 \mathbf{y} . Such a phenomenon also persists in other mechanisms like the cost-function based mechanism. A limit order implementation could be a good rescue for this situation. The limit order could provide much more leeway for someone to express her valuation. [ Agrawal et al. 2011 ] formally characterize a limit order by a triplet ( 𝐚 , Γ , p lim ) (\mathbf{a},\Gamma,p^{\textrm{lim}}) , where 𝐚 \mathbf{a} is a vector that describes the order by setting each component of 𝐚 \mathbf{a} being either 1 1 (if the specified state is desired) or 0 0 (if it is not desired), Γ ∈ ℝ \Gamma\in\mathbb{R} denotes the maximum desired units of that bundle and p lim ∈ ℝ p^{\textrm{lim}}\in\mathbb{R} is the maximum averaged price the trader is willing to pay for the bundle. [ Agrawal et al. 2011 ] shows that SCPM market maker decides the units to grant via solving a convex optimization problem. They also show that such implementation is a generalized Vickrey-Clarke-Groves(VCG) scheme(see, e.g., [ Roughgarden 2010 ] ) and therefore admits myopic truthful reporting in the sense that traders will not lie about their true valuation of bid price p lim p^{\textrm{lim}} . Similar to the SCPM, our MU function-based framework can be easily modified to incorporate limit order submission. Specifically, given market state ( 𝐪 , w ) (\mathbf{q},w) , a limit order of the form ( 𝐚 , Γ , p lim ) (\mathbf{a},\Gamma,p^{\textrm{lim}}) , the price of this limit order can be computed by solving the following problem, (94) 𝒫 Smax : max 0 ≤ τ ≤ Γ , Δ ​ w \displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{Smax}}:~~\max_{0\leq\tau\leq\Gamma,\Delta w} p lim ⋅ τ − Δ ​ w \displaystyle p^{\textrm{lim}}\cdot\tau-\Delta w Subject to: \displaystyle\textrm{Subject to:} U ⁡ ( ( w + Δ ​ w ) ⋅ 𝐞 − ( 𝐪 + τ ​ 𝐚 ) ) ≥ U ⁡ ( W 0 ⋅ 𝐞 ) , \displaystyle U((w+\Delta w)\cdot\mathbf{e}-(\mathbf{q}+\tau\mathbf{a}))\geq U(W_{0}\cdot\mathbf{e}), ( w + Δ ​ w ) ⋅ 𝐞 − ( 𝐪 + τ ​ 𝐚 ) ∈ dom ​ ( U ) . \displaystyle(w+\Delta w)\cdot\mathbf{e}-(\mathbf{q}+\tau\mathbf{a})\in\textbf{{dom}}(U). After solving problem 𝒫 S ​ m ​ a ​ x \mathcal{P}_{Smax} , the trader is granted τ ∗ \tau^{*} shares and charged with Δ ​ w ∗ \Delta w^{*} , where ( τ ∗ , Δ ​ w ∗ ) (\tau^{*},\Delta w^{*}) is the solution of problem 𝒫 S ​ m ​ a ​ x \mathcal{P}_{Smax} . The objective function τ ​ p lim − Δ ​ w \tau p^{\textrm{lim}}-\Delta w can be understood as follows. The term τ ​ p lim \tau p^{\textrm{lim}} is the maximum price one is willing to pay for the order, and Δ ​ w \Delta w is the price actually charged for such an order. Maximizing τ ​ p lim − Δ ​ w \tau p^{\textrm{lim}}-\Delta w is actually finding the highest possible cost reduction for the trader. Like SCPM, our limit order implementation is a generalized VCG scheme and admits truthful reporting of the bid price. The proof of this claim is similar to the one of Theorem 1 in [ Agrawal et al. 2011 ] . Thus, we omit the details. ◄ Feeling lucky?
